<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:spike}
                                                                      
Fix $ \lambda > 1 $. 
Assume $d = O(\log(n)) $. 
Then the following results hold. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{itm:opt}
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= \Phi\mleft( \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n} \mright) + o(1) , \notag 
    \end{align}
    where $ \lambda_\star, K_d $ and $ \Phi $ are defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda_star,eqn:Kd,eqn:Phi}, respectively. 
    This can be achieved by the following polynomial
    \begin{align}
        q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(x) &\coloneqq \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,x)}{\sqrt{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}} . \label{eqn:def_qopt} 
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:val_sub} If 
    \begin{align}
        \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n} &\to 0 , \label{eqn:cond_sub} 
    \end{align}
    then 
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= r_d + o(1) , \notag 
    \end{align}
    where $ r_d $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:rd}. 
    This can be achieved by the following polynomial
    \begin{align}
        q_{\mathrm{val}}(x) &\coloneqq \frac{K_d(r_d,x)}{\sqrt{K_d(r_d,r_d)}} . \label{eqn:def_qval} 
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:val_sup} If 
    \begin{align}
        \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n} &\to \infty , \label{eqn:cond_sup} 
    \end{align}
    then 
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= \lambda_\star + o(1) . \notag 
    \end{align}
    This can be achieved by the polynomial $ q_{{\mathrm{opt}}} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qopt}. 

    \item \label{itm:concl} If 
    \begin{align}
        \frac{d}{\log(n)} \to \delta \in (0,\infty) \label{eqn:d}
    \end{align}
    as $ n\to\infty $, then 
                                                      
    \begin{align}
    &&
        \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= \begin{cases}
            0 , & \delta < \delta_c \\
            1 , & \delta > \delta_c
        \end{cases} , & 
        \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= \begin{cases}
            2 , & \delta < \delta_c \\
            \lambda_\star , & \delta > \delta_c
        \end{cases} , & 
    & \label{eqn:lim} 
    \end{align}
    where
    \begin{align}
        \delta_c &\coloneqq \frac{1}{2\log(\lambda)} . \label{eqn:deltac}
    \end{align}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
As shown in \zcref[S]{itm:concl} of \zcref[S]{thm:spike}, the limit of $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d} $ undergoes a discontinuous phase transition (in fact, a $0$-$1$ transition) around $ \delta_c $. 
    The critical case $ \delta = \delta_c $ is not covered since the limit of $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d} $ does not exist. 
    This can be seen by taking $ d = \mleft\lfloor \delta_c \log(n) - \sqrt{\log(n)} \mright\rfloor $ and $ d = \mleft\lfloor \delta_c \log(n) + \sqrt{\log(n)} \mright\rfloor $ which lead to different limits $ 0 $ and $ \infty $, respectively, of $ K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)/n $. 
    On the other hand, by the non-asymptotic result in \zcref[S]{itm:opt} of \zcref[S]{thm:spike}, under the refined scaling limit $ d - \delta_c \log(n) \to s \in {\mathbb R} $, 
                          
    one has 
    \begin{align}
    &&
        \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star, \lambda_\star)}{n} &\to \frac{\lambda^6}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^3} \lambda^{2s} , & 
        \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &\to \Phi\mleft( \frac{\lambda^6}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^3} \lambda^{2s} \mright) . & 
    & \notag 
    \end{align}
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\zcref[S]{thm:spike} not only characterizes $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}, \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d} $, but also identifies maximizing polynomials for them (see $ q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}, q_{\mathrm{val}} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qopt,eqn:def_qval}, respectively).
    These polynomials are certain linear combinations of Chebyshev polynomials. 
    The appearance of Chebyshev polynomials as maximizers is not surprising. 
    It has been known that such polynomials encode non-backtracking walks \cite{Sodin,Feldheim_Sodin} and self-avoiding walks \cite{Hopkins_Steurer,Ding_Hopkins_Steurer} which are certain optimal tree-structured polynomials. 
    These connections are obtained by viewing the matrix as the adjacency matrix of a graph. 
    For Wigner matrices, such connections are not exact, and only hold approximately for large dimensions. 
    Our proofs do not exploit these connections since we never work with individual matrix elements and only work with eigenvalue spectra. 
    The reason why Chebyshev polynomials show up as maximizers is explained in the following passage; see in particular \zcref[S]{eqn:obj,eqn:obj2}.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
In the \textsc{LowDeg}\xspace literature, many analyses for Gaussian models are based on Hermite polynomials \cite{Schramm_Wein,Montanari_Wein,Sohn_Wein,Li_corr,Fu_Sohn,Damian_Pillaud-Vivien_Lee_Bruna,Jia_Vijayaraghavan}. 
This is a sensible choice since Hermite polynomials are orthonormal with respect to the Gaussian measure and any polynomial in the entries of the input data admits a decomposition in this basis. 
In contrast, the proof of \zcref[S]{thm:spike} and, indeed, all proofs in this paper are based on Chebyshev polynomials. 
This is because we deal with special polynomials given by linear combinations of matrix powers. 
Equivalently, by functional calculus \zcref[S]{eqn:fun_cal}, they apply to the eigenvalue spectrum component-wise, while retaining eigenvectors. 
Chebyshev polynomials are a convenient tool since they are orthonormal with respect to semicircle law (see \zcref[S]{eqn:orth}) which is the limiting spectral distribution of GOE (and also spiked GOE, by eigenvalue interlacing; recall \zcref[S]{eqn:ESD_cvg}). 

To see the advantage of working in the Chebyshev basis, let us provide an informal derivation of $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}, \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d} $ for the spiked matrix model \zcref[S]{eqn:spiked_model}. 
Fix an arbitrary nonzero polynomial $q\in{\mathcal P}_d$ and without loss of optimality normalize it such that 
\begin{align}
    \int q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x = 1 . \label{eqn:q_norm}
\end{align}
By spectral decomposition of $Y$, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 \mleft\langlev_1(Y),b\mright\rangle^2}{\sum_{j=1}^n q(\lambda_j(Y))^2 \mleft\langlev_j(Y),b\mright\rangle^2} . 
    \label{eqn:spec_decomp}
\end{align}
Since $ b \sim {\mathcal N}(0_n,I_n/n) $ is independent of $Y$ and $ (v_j(Y))_{j=1}^n $ is an orthonormal system, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 \mleft\langlev_1(Y),b\mright\rangle^2}{\sum_{j=1}^n q(\lambda_j(Y))^2 \mleft\langlev_j(Y),b\mright\rangle^2}
    &= \frac{\xi_1^2 q(\lambda_1(Y))^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2}
    = \frac{1}{1 + \frac{\sum_{i=2}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\xi_1^2 q(\lambda_1(Y))^2}} , \notag  
\end{align}
where $ \xi_j \coloneqq \mleft\langlev_j(Y),b\mright\rangle $ and $ \xi_1, \cdots, \xi_n \overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} {\mathcal N}(0,1/n) $ are independent of $ (\lambda_j(Y))_{j=1}^n $. 
Due to concentration of measure of $ (\xi_i)_{i=1}^n $ and the assumed normalization \zcref[S]{eqn:q_norm}, we claim that 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{i=2}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2 &= \int q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x + o_{{\mathbb P}}(1)
    = 1 + o_{{\mathbb P}}(1) . \label{eqn:input1} 
\end{align}
To justify this step, we develop quantitative estimates, such as \zcref[S]{eqn:LSS}, for linear spectral statistics with respect to polynomials of logarithmic degree. 
On the other hand, since $ \lambda_1(Y) \to \lambda_\star $ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda1}), $ q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 \approx q(\lambda_\star)^2 $. 
Combining the above claims, we have 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda))
    = \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y)b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y)b\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright]
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\frac{1}{1 + \frac{1}{\xi_1^2 q(\lambda_\star)^2}}\mright] + o(1)
                                          
    . \label{eqn:ol_TODO} 
\end{align}
Maximizing \zcref[S]{eqn:ol_TODO} over $q\in{\mathcal P}_d\setminus\{0\}$ is then equivalent to the following maximization problem: 
\begin{align}
    \sup\mleft\{ q(\lambda_\star)^2 : q\in{\mathcal P}_d , \, \int q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x = 1 \mright\} . \label{eqn:obj}
\end{align}
This problem can be explicitly solved for any $d$ by decomposing $q$ in the Chebyshev basis and recognizing that \zcref[S]{eqn:obj} can be written as maximization of a linear form over a $(d+1)$-dimensional unit vector recording the coefficients of $q$. 
Solving \zcref[S]{eqn:obj}, we get the exact maximum $ K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) $. 
Plugging this back to \zcref[S]{eqn:ol_TODO} and recalling the definition of $\Phi$ in \zcref[S]{eqn:Phi}, we conclude
\begin{align}
    \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda))
    &= \sup\mleft\{ {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) : q\in{\mathcal P}_d , \, \int q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x = 1 \mright\}
    = \Phi\mleft(\frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n}\mright) + o(1) . \notag 
\end{align}
The unique maximizer (modulo a sign) of \zcref[S]{eqn:obj} is $ q_{{\mathrm{opt}}} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qopt} which, in view of the above display, also achieves $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d} $. 
This (informally) proves \zcref[S]{itm:opt} of \zcref[S]{thm:spike}. 

We follow a similar approach to studying $ \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d} $. 
We do not get into the details here, but only mention that now the technical input analogous to \zcref[S]{eqn:input1} is: 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{i=2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) q(\lambda_i(Y))^2 
    &= \int x q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x + o_{{\mathbb P}}(1) . \notag 
\end{align}
and maximizing the Rayleigh quotient over $q$ boils down to 
\begin{align}
    \sup\mleft\{ \int x q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x : q \in {\mathcal P}_d , \, \int q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x = 1 \mright\} \label{eqn:obj2} 
\end{align}
which again can be solved explicitly by recognizing it as a quadratic form maximization over the coefficient vector of $q$ in the Chebyshev basis, giving the exact maximum $ r_d $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:rd} and the unique maximizer $ q_{\mathrm{val}} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qval}. 
This line of argument eventually leads to \zcref[S]{itm:val_sub} of \zcref[S]{thm:spike}. 
For \zcref[S]{itm:val_sup}, an upper bound is simply given by $ \lambda_1(Y) \to \lambda_\star $, and we verify that this value can be achieved by $ q_{{\mathrm{opt}}} $. 
Finally, taking suitable scaling limits of \zcref[S]{itm:opt,itm:val_sub,itm:val_sup} yields \zcref[S]{itm:concl} of \zcref[S]{thm:spike}.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:SE}
Consider the Bayes-AMP algorithm \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP} for $Y$ in \zcref[S]{eqn:spiked_model_prior} with $ \varpi = {\mathcal N}(0,1) $. 
Suppose that $ (v,v^0) $ is independent of $X$ and satisfies that the empirical distribution of $ (\sqrt{n} v_i, v^0_i)_{i = 1}^n $ converges in Wasserstein-$2$ distance\footnote{We say that the empirical distribution of the rows of a (possibly random) matrix $ \begin{bmatrix}v_1 & \cdots & v_k\end{bmatrix} \in {\mathbb R}^{n\times k} $ converges in Wasserstein-$2$ to a random vector $ \begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_1 & \cdots & {\mathsf{V}}_k\end{bmatrix} \in {\mathbb R}^{1\times k} $ with finite $ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft\|\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_1 & \cdots & {\mathsf{V}}_k\end{bmatrix}\mright\|_{2}^2\mright] $ if for any $ f \colon {\mathbb R}^k \to {\mathbb R} $ satisfying $ \mleft|f(x) - f(y)\mright| \le C \mleft\|x-y\mright\|_{2} (1 + \mleft\|x\mright\|_{2} + \mleft\|y\mright\|_{2}) $ for some constant $C>0$, one has $ n^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^n f(v_{i,1},\cdots,v_{i,k}) \to \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[f({\mathsf{V}}_1, \cdots, {\mathsf{V}}_k)\mright] $ almost surely as $n\to\infty$ and $k$ fixed. See \cite[Chapter 6]{Villani} for the relevant background.} to a pair of random variables $ ({\mathsf{V}}, (\lambda^2 - 1) {\mathsf{V}} + \sqrt{\lambda^2 - 1} {\mathsf{X}}_0) $ where $ ({\mathsf{V}},{\mathsf{X}}_0) \sim {\mathcal N}(0,1)^{\otimes2} $. 
Then for any fixed $t\ge0$, the empirical distribution of $ (\sqrt{n} v_i, v^t_i)_{i = 1}^n $ converges in Wasserstein-$2$ to a pair of random variables $ ({\mathsf{V}}, (\lambda^2 - 1) {\mathsf{V}} + \sqrt{\lambda^2 - 1} {\mathsf{X}}_t) $ where $ ({\mathsf{V}},{\mathsf{X}}_t) \sim {\mathcal N}(0,1)^{\otimes2} $. 
In particular, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev,v^t\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|v\mright\|_{2}^2 \mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] &= 1 - \frac{1}{\lambda^2} , &
    \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev^t,Y v^t\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] &= \lambda_\star , & 
& \label{eqn:SE_E} 
\end{align}
where $ \lambda_\star $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda_star}. 
Moreover, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),v^t\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] &= 1 . \label{eqn:align} 
\end{align}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\zcref[S]{prop:SE} is proved in \zcref[S]{sec:pf_spiked_goe_prior} using techniques developed in \cite{Zhang_Ji_Venkataramanan_Mondelli} (with precursors \cite{Mondelli_Thrampoulidis_Venkataramanan,Mondelli_Venkataramanan_JSTAT,Mondelli_Venkataramanan_NeurIPS,Zhong_Wang_Fan}) for proving alignment between the top eigenvector and the iterate of AMP initialized at stationarity and run till convergence. 
This method offers an alternative to random matrix theory for studying spectral statistics, and can be seamlessly combined with state evolution theory to show further refinement by iterative schemes initialized with spectral estimators. 
This proof strategy was subsequently adopted by \cite{Zhang_Mondelli,Zhang_Ji_Venkataramanan_Mondelli_rotinv,Yang_Sen_Lu} and proved fruitful in the contexts of generalized linear models and PCA. 
Among papers cited above, \cite{Zhang_Mondelli} contains an execution of this argument that is technically closest to the one used for \zcref[S]{prop:SE}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:spike_prior}
Let $Y$ be as in \zcref[S]{eqn:spiked_model_prior} with $ \varpi = {\mathcal N}(0,1) $.
Consider the Bayes-AMP algorithm \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP} initialized with $ v^0 \sim {\mathcal N}(0_n,I_n/n) $ independent of $ Y $. 
If $ t / \log(n) \to \delta \in (0,\infty) $ as $n\to\infty$, then 
\begin{align}
&&
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),v^t\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] &= \begin{cases}
        0 , & \delta < \delta_c \\
        1 , & \delta > \delta_c
    \end{cases} , & 
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev^t,Y v^t\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] &= \begin{cases}
        0 , & \delta < \delta_c \\
        \lambda_\star , & \delta > \delta_c
    \end{cases} , &
& \label{eqn:lim_pt} 
\end{align}
where $ \lambda_\star $ and $ \delta_c $ are defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:deltac,eqn:lambda_star}, respectively.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rk:eigval_below}
Note that when $ \delta < \delta_c $, the limiting Rayleigh quotient achieved by $ v^t $ is $0$ whereas \zcref[S]{eqn:lim} promised that the optimal polynomial achieves $2$ which is the right edge of the bulk of the limiting spectrum of $Y$. 
This nuance is immaterial and can be easily rectified by taking $ \sum_{s = 1}^t \lambda^s v^s $ as the final estimate. 
By \zcref[S]{lem:AMP_poly}, the latter is still a legit low-degree polynomial estimator and we claim that it achieves a limiting value $2$, matching \zcref[S]{eqn:lim}.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\zcref[S]{thm:spike_prior} is proved by unrolling the iteration \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP}, recognizing that it takes the form of $ q(Y) v^0 $ where $ q(x) = p_{t+1}(x) / \lambda^{t+1} $ (see \zcref[S]{lem:AMP_poly}), and then using the techniques developed in \zcref[S]{thm:spike} to compute the two limits in \zcref[S]{eqn:lim_pt}.
In principle, any iteration that does not involve nonlinearities can be unrolled, producing a certain polynomial $q$, and one can study its asymptotic properties within our framework. 
Of particular interest are popular methods in randomized numerical linear algebra, and we leave such studies to future work.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:eigval}
Let $ X \sim \operatorname{GOE}(n) $. 
Fix any $ \varepsilon\in(0,1) $. 
Let $ v^{-1} = 0_n $ and $ v^{0} \sim {\mathcal N}(0_n,I_n) $ independent of $X$. 
Consider $ v^t $ iteratively defined by 
\begin{align}
    v^{t+1} &= X v^t - v^{t-1} . \label{eqn:BAMP_eigval}
\end{align}
For $ T = \mleft\lceil1/\varepsilon\mright\rceil $, let $ \widehat{v}^T \coloneqq \sum_{t = 1}^{T} v^{t+1} $. 
                                                                                                   
Then almost surely, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{\mleft\langle\widehat{v}^T,X\widehat{v}^T\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|\widehat{v}^T\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &\ge 2(1-\varepsilon) . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rk:relation_spin}
    The iteration \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP_eigval} is a natural analogue of \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP} below the BBP threshold $1$ (recall \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda1}) where $\lambda>1$ in the latter is replaced with $1$ in the former. 
    By the three-term recurrence \zcref[S]{eqn:recur}, one has $ p_{t+1}(x) = xp_t(x) - p_{t-1}(x) $, and hence \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP_eigval} is precisely the iterative implementation of Chebyshev polynomials. 
    Another (indirect) way of motivating \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP_eigval} is given by the connection to optimization of Sherrington--Kirkpatrick Hamiltonian \cite{Sherrington_Kirkpatrick}. 
    This problem concerns finding $ \widehat{u} \equiv \widehat{u}(X) \in \{-1,1\}^n $ such that with high probability, $ n^{-1} H_n(\widehat{u}) \ge (1-\varepsilon) {\mathcal H}_n $ for a fixed given $\varepsilon$, where $ H_n(u) \coloneqq \mleft\langleu,Xu\mright\rangle $ and $ {\mathcal H}_n \coloneqq n^{-1} \max_{u\in\{-1,1\}^n} H_n(u) $. 
    The precise limit of $ {\mathcal H}_n $ is given by the Parisi formula \cite{Parisi,Talagrand,Panchenko,Auffinger_Chen} which approximately evaluates to $1.53<2$ and an efficient algorithm that outputs the desired $ \widehat{u} $ with high probability was constructed in \cite{Montanari}. 
    If one replaces the hypercube constraint $ u\in\{-1,1\}^n $ with the spherical constraint $ u\in\sqrt{n}\,{\mathbb S}^{n-1} $ in the definition of $ {\mathcal H}_n $, then this problem reduces to eigenvalue approximation of GOE studied here, and the algorithm of \cite{Montanari} reduces to \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP_eigval}. 
    The proof of \zcref[S]{thm:eigval} is a simple consequence of state evolution theory of AMP (see \zcref[S]{sec:pf_goe_eigval} for details) and we provide a short self-contained proof instead of adapting the result of \cite{Montanari}.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:goe_eigval_opt}
    For every fixed integer $ d\ge0 $, 
    \begin{align}
        \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &= r_d , \label{eqn:VAL_rd}
    \end{align}
    where $ r_d $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:rd}. 
    This can be achieved by the polynomial $ q_{\mathrm{val}} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qval}.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
Recall from \zcref[S]{eqn:edge} that the largest possible value is $ \lambda_1(X) \to 2 $. 
    The deficit of \zcref[S]{eqn:VAL_rd} has the following expansion as $d\to\infty$: 
    \begin{align}
        2 - r_d &= 4 \sin\mleft(\frac{\pi}{d+2}\mright)^2 
        = \frac{\pi^2}{(d+2)^2} + O\mleft(\frac{1}{(d+2)^4}\mright) . \notag 
    \end{align}
    In particular, for any $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, to achieve an asymptotic value at least $ 2(1-\varepsilon) $, the degree $d$ needs to be at least 
    \begin{align}
        \mleft\lceil \frac{\pi}{\arccos(1-\varepsilon)} \mright\rceil - 2
        &\sim \frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2\varepsilon}} , \notag
    \end{align}
    as $ \varepsilon\downarrow0 $. 
    This improves the $ 1/\varepsilon $ dependence of the iteration count $T$ in \zcref[S]{thm:eigval} and is optimal in that it can be achieved by $ q_{\mathrm{val}} $. 
    A lower bound matching \zcref[S]{eqn:VAL_rd} was previously derived in \cite[Theorems 6.9 and 6.12]{Pesenti_thesis} using the Fourier diagram basis introduced in \cite{Jones_Pesenti}. 
    Our proof, based on the Chebyshev basis, directly yields the limit $ r_d $; see \zcref[S]{sec:pf_goe_eigval} for details.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
It was shown in \cite[Theorem 6.3 and Remark 6.8]{Pesenti_thesis} that vanilla power iteration on $X$ (corresponding to taking the monomial $ p(x) = x^t $) initialized with the all-one vector requires $ \Theta(n^{2/3}) $ steps to achieve an asymptotic value $2$.                                                                                                                                                                  
    This is in sharp contrast to the $1/\varepsilon$ and $ O(1/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) $ guarantees in \zcref[S]{thm:eigval,thm:goe_eigval_opt} to achieve $ 2(1-\varepsilon) $ (corresponding to taking the Chebyshev polynomial and $ q_{\mathrm{val}} $, respectively).
    These results altogether signify the importance of choosing a judicious polynomial for efficient eigenvalue approximation of GOE.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:sub}
Fix $ \varepsilon\in(0,1/3) $ and suppose that 
\begin{align}
    \frac{d}{n^{1/3 - \varepsilon}} &\to \delta \in (0,\infty) . \label{eqn:sub}
\end{align}
                          
Then 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:sup}
Fix $\varepsilon\in(0,1/3)$ and suppose 
\begin{align}
    \frac{d}{n^{1/3 + \varepsilon}} &\to \delta \in (0,\infty) . \label{eqn:sup} 
\end{align}
                          
Then 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &= 1 . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{rk:relation_RNLA}
    Taking \zcref[S]{thm:eigval,thm:sub,thm:sup} collectively, we have that for GOE, constant degree suffices for approximating the top eigenvalue, and $ n^{1/3 + o(1)} $ degree is necessary and sufficient for approximating the top eigenvector.
    On the other hand, for an \emph{arbitrary deterministic} positive definite\footnote{One can add $ CI_n $ (for a large constant $ C < \infty $) to a GOE to get a matrix that is positive definite for all large $n$ almost surely. A $t$-step power iteration for the resulting matrix is equivalent to applying the monomial $ (x+C)^t $ to the original GOE.} matrix $ X'\in{\mathbb R}^{n\times n} $, a textbook result in RNLA \cite{Kireeva_Tropp} states that the $T$-th iterate $ \widehat{u}^T $ of power iteration with random initialization satisfies 
    \begin{align}
    &&
        \frac{\lambda_1(X') - \mleft\|\widehat{u}^T\mright\|_{2}^{-2} \mleft\langle\widehat{u}^T,X'\widehat{u}^T\mright\rangle}{\lambda_1(X')} &\in [0,\varepsilon^2] , & 
        \frac{\mleft|\mleft\langlev_1(X'),\widehat{u}^T\mright\rangle\mright|}{\mleft\|\widehat{u}^T\mright\|_{2}}
        &\ge \sqrt{1 - \varepsilon^2} , & 
    & \label{eqn:RNLA_apx} 
    \end{align}
    with high probability, provided 
    \begin{align}
    &&
        \lambda_2(X') &\le (1 - \gamma) \lambda_1(X') , & 
        T \gtrsim \frac{1}{\gamma} \log\mleft(\frac{n}{\varepsilon}\mright) . & 
    & \label{eqn:RNLA_cond}
    \end{align}
    The two conditions in \zcref[S]{eqn:RNLA_cond} enforce a spectral gap and a large degree / iteration count. 
    For GOE $ X $, $ \lambda_1(X) = 2+o(1) $, $ \lambda_2(X) = 2 + o(1) $, and $ \lambda_1(X) - \lambda_2(X) \asymp n^{-2/3} $, typically. 
    Specializing \zcref[S]{eqn:RNLA_cond} by taking $ \gamma \asymp n^{-2/3} $, we obtain the sufficient condition $ T \gtrsim n^{2/3} \log(n/\varepsilon) $ for eigenvalue and eigenvector approximation up to error $ \varepsilon $ in the sense of \zcref[S]{eqn:RNLA_apx}. 
    This is massively overpessimistic compared to the degree thresholds in \zcref[S]{thm:eigval,thm:sub,thm:sup} since such RNLA results only leverage algorithmic randomness in the initialization of power method, and do not account for statistical randomness in the data matrix $X'$.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:crit}
Assume
\begin{align}
    \frac{d}{n^{1/3}} &\to \delta \in (0,\infty) . \label{eqn:crit}
\end{align}
                          
Then 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &= \sup_{u\in L^2([0,1]) \setminus \{0\}} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\frac{\Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2 Z_1}{\sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 Z_j}\mright] , \label{eqn:OPT} 
\end{align}
where $ (Z_j)_{j\ge1} $ are i.i.d.\ $ \chi_1^2 $ random variables independent of the Airy point process $ (-\Lambda_{j-1})_{j\ge1} $.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:extr}
For each $ d\ge0 $, the following results hold. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{itm:extr_pt} For any $ t\in{\mathbb R} $,
    \begin{align}
        \sup\mleft\{ q(t)^2 : q\in{\mathcal P}_d , \int q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) = 1 \mright\} &= K_d(t,t) . \label{eqn:extr_pt} 
    \end{align}
    The unique maximizer (up to sign) is 
    \begin{align}
        q(x) &= \frac{K_d(t,x)}{\sqrt{K_d(t,t)}} . \label{eqn:q} 
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:extr_frac} 
    \begin{align}
        \sup\mleft\{ \frac{\int x q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)}{\int q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)} : q \in {\mathcal P}_d \setminus \{0\} \mright\} &= r_d , \label{eqn:sup_rd}
    \end{align}
    where $ r_d $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:rd}. 
    A maximizer is 
    \begin{align}
        q(x) &= K_d(r_d, x) \propto \sum_{j = 0}^d \sin\mleft(\frac{(j+1)\pi}{d+2}\mright) p_j(x) . \notag 
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:extr_grow} For every $ s\in[0,1] $ and every integer $ m\ge0 $, it holds that 
    \begin{align}
    &&
        \sup_{x\in[-(2+s),2+s]} \mleft|p_m(x)\mright| &\le (m+1) e^{m \sqrt{s}} , & 
        \sup_{x\in[-(2+s),2+s]} \mleft|p'_m(x)\mright| &\le (m+1)^3 e^{m \sqrt{s}} . & 
    & \label{eqn:ps} 
    \end{align}
    Consequently, taking $ s_n = n^{-2/3 + \tau} $ for a fixed $ \tau \in (0,2/3) $ and $ m \le 2d + 2 $ with $ d = O(\log(n)) $, we have 
    \begin{align}
    &&
        \sup_{x\in[-(2+s_n),2+s_n]} \mleft|p_m(x)\mright| &\le C_\tau (m+1) , & 
        \sup_{x\in[-(2+s_n),2+s_n]} \mleft|p'_m(x)\mright| &\le C_\tau (m+1)^3 , & 
    & \label{eqn:psn} 
    \end{align}
    where the constant $ C_\tau > 0 $ depends only on $\tau$. 
    Moreover, 
    \begin{align}
        V_{[-(2+s_n),2+s_n]}(p_m) &\le C_\tau (m+1)^3 , \label{eqn:V}
    \end{align}
    where $ V_I(\cdot) $ denotes the total variation of a function restricted to the domain $I\subset{\mathbb R}$. 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
For \zcref[S]{itm:extr_pt}, take any $ q \in {\mathcal P}_d $ satisfying the condition in \zcref[S]{itm:extr_pt} and decompose it in the basis of Chebyshev polynomials: 
\begin{align}
    q(t) &= \sum_{k = 0}^d a_k p_k(t) = \mleft\langlea,u(t)\mright\rangle , \label{eqn:q_cheb} 
\end{align}
where
\begin{align}
&&
    a &\coloneqq \begin{bmatrix}a_0 & \cdots & a_d\end{bmatrix}^\top , &
    u(t) &\coloneqq \begin{bmatrix}p_0(t) & \cdots & p_d(t)\end{bmatrix}^\top . &
& \label{eqn:au} 
\end{align}
Note that, by orthonormality of $ (p_d)_{d\ge0} $ with respect to $ \mu_{\textnormal{sc}} $ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:orth}), the condition in \zcref[S]{itm:extr_pt} implies 
\begin{align}
    1 &= \int q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)
    = \sum_{k = 0}^d a_k^2 = \mleft\|a\mright\|_{2}^2 . \notag 
\end{align}
By Cauchy--Schwarz, 
\begin{align}
    q(t)^2 &\le \mleft\|a\mright\|_{2}^2 \mleft\|u(t)\mright\|_{2}^2
    = \sum_{k = 0}^d p_k(t)^2
    = K_d(t,t) , \notag 
\end{align}
which establishes \zcref[S]{eqn:extr_pt}. 
Equality holds if and only if the two vectors $ a $ and $ u(t) $ are proportional to each other, leading to the polynomial (up to sign)
\begin{align}
    q(x) &= \frac{\sum_{k = 0}^d p_k(t) p_k(x)}{\sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^d p_k(t)^2}} , \notag 
\end{align}
which is precisely \zcref[S]{eqn:q}. 
This proves \zcref[S]{itm:extr_pt}. 

For \zcref[S]{itm:extr_frac}, we again take any $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d\setminus\{0\} $ and work with the representation \zcref[S]{eqn:q_cheb}. 
By orthonormality of Chebyshev polynomials \zcref[S]{eqn:orth} and the three-term recurrence relation \zcref[S]{eqn:recur}, 
\begin{align}
    \int x q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)
    &= \sum_{k,\ell = 0}^d a_k a_\ell \int x p_k(x) p_\ell(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) \notag \\
    &= \sum_{k,\ell = 0}^d a_k a_\ell \int p_{k+1}(x) p_\ell(x) + p_{k-1}(x) p_\ell(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) \notag \\
    &= \sum_{k = 0}^d a_k a_{k+1} + a_k a_{k-1}
    = a^\top J a , \notag 
\end{align}
where we adopt the convention that summands with out-of-range indices are zero. 
On the RHS of the last equality, the vector $a$ has been defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:au} and the Jacobi matrix $ J\in{\mathbb R}^{(d+1)\times (d+1)} $ is given by 
\begin{align}
    J &= \begin{bmatrix}
        0 & 1 &  &  & \\
        1 & 0 & 1 &  & \\
                           
         & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\
         &  & 1 & 0 & 1 \\
         &  &  & 1 & 0
    \end{bmatrix} . \label{eqn:J} 
\end{align}
Therefore, the LHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:sup_rd} can be written as 
\begin{align}
    \sup\mleft\{ \frac{\int x q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)}{\int q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)} : q \in {\mathcal P}_d \setminus \{0\} \mright\}
    &= \max_{a\in{\mathbb R}^{d+1} \setminus \{0_{d+1}\}} \frac{a^\top J a}{\mleft\|a\mright\|_{2}^2}
    = \lambda_1(J) , \label{eqn:J_opt} 
\end{align}
and any maximizer $ a $ equals $ v_1(J) $ up to rescaling. 
Note that $J$ is a tridiagonal matrix whose spectrum and eigenspace are well understood; see \cite[Section 3]{Losonczi}. 
In particular, the eigenvalues of $J$ are given by 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\{ 2 \cos\mleft( \frac{k \pi}{d + 2} \mright) : 1\le k\le d+1 \mright\} \subset [-2,2] , \notag 
\end{align}
among which the largest one is precisely $ r_d $ given in \zcref[S]{eqn:rd} corresponding to $ k = 1 $. 
Moreover, the eigenvector associated with $ r_d $ is proportional to $ a = \begin{bmatrix}a_0 & \cdots & a_d\end{bmatrix}^\top $ where for each $ 0\le j\le d $, 
\begin{align}
    a_j &= \sin\mleft(\frac{(j+1)\pi}{d+2}\mright) . \notag 
\end{align}
Therefore, a maximizing polynomial $ q $ is given by 
\begin{align}
    q(x) &= \sum_{j = 0}^d a_j p_j(x) = \sum_{j = 0}^d \sin\mleft(\frac{(j+1)\pi}{d+2}\mright) p_j(x) . \notag 
\end{align}
Since any nonzero rescaling preserves optimality of $q$ and by \zcref[S]{eqn:U_small},
\begin{align}
    p_j(r_d) &= U_j\mleft( \cos\mleft( \frac{\pi}{d + 2} \mright) \mright)
    = \frac{\sin\mleft( (j+1) \pi / (d+2) \mright)}{\sin\mleft( \pi / (d+2) \mright)} = \frac{a_j}{\sin\mleft( \pi / (d+2) \mright)} , \notag 
\end{align}
the following polynomial is also a maximizer: 
\begin{align}
    q(x) &= \sum_{j = 0}^d p_j(r_d) p_j(x) = K_d(r_d, x) . \notag 
\end{align}
This completes the proof of \zcref[S]{itm:extr_frac}. 

Turning to \zcref[S]{itm:extr_grow}, first note that on $ [-1,1] $, one has an explicit representation of $ U_m $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:U_small} the RHS of which can be upper bounded as
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{\sin( (m+1) \theta )}{\sin(\theta)} \mright| &\le m+1 . \label{eqn:sin}
\end{align}
To see this, note that for any $ m\in{\mathbb Z}_{\ge0} $ and $ \theta\in{\mathbb R} $, the geometric series on the LHS below sums to 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{j = 0}^m \exp\mleft(\mathrm{i} (m - 2j) \theta\mright) &= \frac{\exp\mleft(\mathrm{i}(m + 1) \theta\mright) - \exp\mleft(-\mathrm{i} (m+1) \theta\mright)}{\exp\mleft(\mathrm{i} \theta\mright) - \exp\mleft(-\mathrm{i}\theta\mright)} , \notag 
\end{align}
where $ \mathrm{i} $ denotes the imaginary unit. 
Using the identity $ \sin(x) = \mleft( \exp\mleft(\mathrm{i} x\mright) - \exp\mleft(-\mathrm{i} x\mright) \mright) / (2\mathrm{i}) $, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\frac{\sin( (m+1) \theta )}{\sin(\theta)}\mright| &= \mleft|\sum_{j = 0}^m \exp\mleft(\mathrm{i}(m - 2 j) \theta\mright)\mright| \le m+1 . \notag 
\end{align}
This justifies \zcref[S]{eqn:sin} which immediately implies 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{x\in[-2,2]} \mleft|p_m(x)\mright| &\le m+1 . \label{eqn:pm} 
\end{align}
For $ x > 2 $, let us write $ x = 2 \cosh(t) $ for some $ t > 0 $. 
Then by the explicit representation of $ U_m $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:U_big}, 
\begin{align}
    p_m(x) &= U_m(\cosh(t)) 
    = \frac{\sinh( (m+1) t )}{\sinh(t)}
    \le (m+1) \exp\mleft(mt\mright) . \label{eqn:sinh}
\end{align}
To see the last inequality, by definition of $ \sinh $, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\sinh( (m+1) t )}{\sinh(t)}
    &= \frac{\exp\mleft((m+1) t\mright) - \exp\mleft(-(m+1) t\mright)}{\exp\mleft(t\mright) - \exp\mleft(-t\mright)} 
    = \exp\mleft(-mt\mright) \frac{\exp\mleft(2(m+1)t\mright) - 1}{\exp\mleft(2t\mright) - 1} \notag \\
    &= \sum_{j = 0}^m e^{(2j-m)t}
    \le (m+1) \exp\mleft(mt\mright) . \notag 
\end{align}
To conclude the first inequality in \zcref[S]{eqn:ps}, we only need to upper bound $t$ in terms of $s$. 
This can be done by recalling the assumption $ 2\cosh(t) = x \le 2 + s $ which implies $ \cosh(t) \le 1 + s/2 $. 
Since $ \cosh(t) > 1 + t^2/2 $ for any $t>0$, we have $ t\le\sqrt{s} $, as desired. 
The case where $ x < -2 $ is similar if one uses \zcref[S]{eqn:U_big_neg} in place of \zcref[S]{eqn:U_big}. 
We omit the details. 

The second inequality in \zcref[S]{eqn:ps} follows by combining the above result with Markov brothers' inequality (see \zcref[S]{prop:markov}), 
                                                                       
                                                                                       
\begin{align}
    \sup_{x\in[-(2+s),2+s]} \mleft|p_m'(x)\mright|
    &\le m^2 \sup_{x\in[-(2+s),2+s]} \mleft|p_m(x)\mright|
    \le (m+1)^3 e^{m \sqrt{s}} . \notag 
\end{align}

Taking $ s_n = n^{-2/3 + \tau} $ and $ m = 2d+2 = O(\log(n)) $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:ps}, one has $ m \sqrt{s_n} = O(\log(n) \cdot n^{-1/3+\tau/2}) = o(1) $, and hence $ \exp\mleft(m\sqrt{s_n}\mright) = 1+o(1) $. 
This allows us to conclude \zcref[S]{eqn:psn}. 
Finally, \zcref[S]{eqn:V} follows since for a differentiable function $p$ and any interval $ I \subset {\mathbb R} $, 
\begin{align}
    V_I(p) &= \int_I \mleft|p'(x)\mright| \,\mathrm{d} x \le \mleft|I\mright| \cdot \sup_{x\in I} \mleft|p'(x)\mright| . \notag 
\end{align}
Plugging the second estimate in \zcref[S]{eqn:psn} to the RHS above gives \zcref[S]{eqn:V}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:tr}
Let $ X,Y $ be $n\times n$ real symmetric matrices and $ f\colon{\mathbb R} \to {\mathbb R} $ be a function with bounded variation. 
Then 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \operatorname{Tr}(f(X)) - \operatorname{Tr}(f(Y)) \mright| &\le \operatorname{rk}(X-Y) \cdot V(f) , \notag 
\end{align}
where $ V(f) $ denotes the total variation of $f$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
For $ Z \in \{X,Y\} $, define, for any $ z\in{\mathbb R} $, 
\begin{align}
    F_Z(z) &= \frac{1}{n} \mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : \lambda_i(Z) \le z \mright\}\mright| \notag 
\end{align}
to be the c.d.f.\ of the empirical spectral distribution of $ Z $. 
Define also the signed measure whose c.d.f.\ is $ F = F_X - F_Y $. 
Then by \cite[Theorem A.43]{Bai_Silverstein}, 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{z\in{\mathbb R}} \mleft| F(z) \mright|
    &\le \frac{1}{n} \operatorname{rk}(X - Y) . \label{eqn:rk_ineq}
\end{align}
Using this and integration by parts, we have
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{n} \mleft( \operatorname{Tr}(f(X)) - \operatorname{Tr}(f(Y)) \mright)
    &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n f(\lambda_i(X)) - f(\lambda_i(Y))
    = \int f(z) \,\mathrm{d} F(z) \notag \\
    &= f(+\infty) F(+\infty) - f(-\infty) F(-\infty) - \int F(z) \,\mathrm{d} f(z) . \label{eqn:tr1} 
\end{align}
The first two terms on the RHS vanish for the following reasons: $ f(+\infty),f(-\infty) $ are finite due to bounded variation of $f$, $ F(-\infty) =0 $ by definition, and $ F(+\infty) = F_X(+\infty) - F_Y(+\infty) = 0 $ since $ F_X,F_Y $ are probability measures.
For the third term, we have
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\int F(z) \,\mathrm{d} f(z)\mright|
    &\le \mleft( \sup_{z\in{\mathbb R}} \mleft|F(z)\mright| \mright) \cdot \int \mleft| \mathrm{d} f(z) \mright|
                                                                            
    \le \frac{1}{n} \operatorname{rk}(X - Y) \cdot V(f) , \label{eqn:tr2}
\end{align}
where the last inequality is by \zcref[S]{eqn:rk_ineq} and the definition of total variation. 
Combining \zcref[S]{eqn:tr1,eqn:tr2} completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:LS}
                                            
Assume $ d = O(\log(n)) $. 
Fix a constant $ \tau \in (0,2/3) $. 
Then there exists a constant $ C_\tau>0 $ depending only on $\tau$ such that with probability $1-o(1)$, uniformly for all $ 0\le m\le 2d+2 $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 2}^n p_m(\lambda_i(Y)) - \mathds1{\mleft\{m = 0\mright\}}\mright| &\le C_\tau (m+1)^3 n^{-1+\tau} . \label{eqn:LS} 
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let $ s_n = n^{-2/3 + \tau} $. 
By the rigidity of GOE eigenvalues (see \zcref[S]{prop:rig}), there exists a constant $ C_\tau>0 $ depending only on $\tau$ such that with probability $ 1 - o(1) $, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \max_{i\in[n]} \, \mleft| \lambda_i(X) - \gamma_i \mright| &\le C_\tau n^{-2/3 + \tau} \min\mleft\{i,n-i+1\mright\}^{-1/3} , & 
    \max_{i\in[n]} \, \mleft|\lambda_i(X)\mright| &\le 2 + s_n , &
& \label{eqn:rig}
\end{align}
where $ \gamma_i $ is the classical location of $ \lambda_i(X) $; see \zcref[S]{eqn:class_loc}. 
By Weyl's inequality, the eigenvalues of $ X $ and $ Y $ interlace each other: 
\begin{align}
    \lambda_1(Y) &\ge \lambda_1(X) \ge \lambda_2(Y) \ge \lambda_2(X) \ge \cdots \ge \lambda_n(Y) \ge \lambda_n(X) . \notag 
\end{align}
Combining this with the second inequality in \zcref[S]{eqn:rig}, we have that for all sufficiently large $n$, with probability $1-o(1)$, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\{\lambda_i(Y) : 2\le i\le n\mright\} &\subset [-(2+s_n), 2+s_n] . \label{eqn:bulk}
\end{align}
Furthermore, since $ \lambda_1(Y) \to \lambda_\star > 2 $ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda1}), for all sufficiently large $n$, with probability $1 - o(1)$, it holds that 
\begin{align}
    \lambda_1(Y) &> 2 + s_n . \label{eqn:out}
\end{align}
In the rest of the proof, we work on the high-probability event where \zcref[S]{eqn:rig,eqn:bulk,eqn:out} simultaneously hold. 

By the fundamental theorem of calculus and the triangle inequality, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\lambda_i(X)) - \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\gamma_i) \mright|
    &= \mleft|\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n \int^{\lambda_i(X)}_{\gamma_i} p_m'(x) \,\mathrm{d} x\mright| \notag \\
    &\le \mleft(\sup_{x\in[-(2+s_n),2+s_n]} \mleft|p_m'(x)\mright|\mright) \cdot \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n \mleft|\lambda_i(X) - \gamma_i\mright| . \notag 
\end{align}
The first factor above can be bounded using \zcref[S]{eqn:psn}. 
The second factor above can be bounded using the rigidity of GOE eigenvalues in \zcref[S]{eqn:rig}, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n \mleft|\lambda_i(X) - \gamma_i\mright|
    &\le C_\tau n^{-2/3 + \tau} \cdot \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n \min\mleft\{i,n-i+1\mright\}^{-1/3} \notag \\
    &\le C_\tau n^{-2/3 + \tau} \cdot \frac{2}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^{\mleft\lceiln/2\mright\rceil} i^{-1/3} \notag \\
    &\le C_\tau n^{-2/3 + \tau} \cdot \frac{2}{n} \int_0^{\mleft\lceiln/2\mright\rceil} x^{-1/3} \,\mathrm{d} x \notag \\
    &= C_\tau n^{-2/3 + \tau} \cdot \frac{2}{n} \frac{3}{2} \mleft\lceiln/2\mright\rceil^{2/3} \notag \\
    &\le 3 C_\tau n^{-1 + \tau} . \notag 
\end{align}
                                
Combining the above two estimates, we have
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\lambda_i(X)) - \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\gamma_i) \mright|
    &\le C_\tau (m+1)^3 n^{-1+\tau} . \label{eqn:close1} 
\end{align}

Recalling from \zcref[S]{eqn:class_loc} the definition of the classical location $ \gamma_i $, we have 
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\gamma_i)
    &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m\mleft( Q\mleft(\frac{i}{n}\mright) \mright) . \notag 
\end{align}
Defining the function $ g\colon[0,1] \to {\mathbb R} $ as $ g(u) \coloneqq p_m\mleft( Q(u) \mright) $, we claim that $g$ has bounded variation. 
Indeed, 
\begin{align}
    V_{[0,1]}(g) &= \int_0^1 \mleft|g'(u)\mright| \,\mathrm{d} u
    = \int_0^1 \mleft| p_m'\mleft(Q(u)\mright) \mright| \cdot \mleft|Q'(u)\mright| \,\mathrm{d} u           
    = \int_0^1 \mleft| p_m'\mleft(Q(u)\mright) \mright| \,\mathrm{d} Q(u) \notag \\
                                                                      
          
    &= \int_{-2}^2 \mleft|p_m'(x)\mright| \,\mathrm{d} x
    = V_{[-2,2]}(p_m) . \notag 
\end{align}
In the third equality above, we use the fact that $Q$ is nondecreasing, so the absolute value around $Q'$ can be removed. 
Now applying \zcref[S]{itm:extr_grow} of \zcref[S]{lem:extr} to the RHS, we have
\begin{align}
    V_{[0,1]}(g) &\le C (m+1)^3 , \label{eqn:Vg} 
\end{align}
for an absolute constant $C>0$.               
Recalling that $ Q $ is the inverse c.d.f.\ of $ \mu_{\textnormal{sc}} $ and applying the change of variable $ x \mapsto Q(x) $, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\gamma_i) - \int p_m(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) \mright|
    &= \mleft| \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m\mleft( Q\mleft( \frac{i}{n} \mright) \mright) - \int p_m(Q(x)) \,\mathrm{d} x \mright| \notag \\
    &= \mleft| \sum_{i = 1}^n \frac{1}{n} g\mleft(\frac{i}{n}\mright) - \sum_{i = 1}^n \int_{(i-1)/n}^{i/n} g(x) \,\mathrm{d} x \mright| \notag \\
    &\le \sum_{i = 1}^n \int_{(i-1)/n}^{i/n} \mleft| g(i/n) - g(x) \mright| \,\mathrm{d} x \notag \\
    &\le \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n V_{[(i-1)/n,i/n]}(g) \notag \\
    &\le \frac{V_{[0,1]}(g)}{n}
    \le \frac{C (m+1)^3}{n} , \label{eqn:close2} 
\end{align}
where the last step is by \zcref[S]{eqn:Vg}. 

By orthonormality of $ (p_m)_{m\ge0} $ with respect to $ \mu_{\textnormal{sc}} $ and the fact $ p_0(x) = 1 $, we have 
\begin{align}
    \int p_m(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= \mathds1{\mleft\{m = 0\mright\}} . \notag 
\end{align}
Combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:close1,eqn:close2} and using the triangle inequality yield 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\lambda_i(X)) - \mathds1{\mleft\{m = 0\mright\}}\mright| &\le C_\tau (m+1)^3 n^{-1+\tau} . \label{eqn:close3} 
\end{align}

Now it remains to relate the LHS of the above estimate to the LHS of the desired one in \zcref[S]{eqn:LS}. 
To this end, we truncate $ p_m $ outside $[-(2+s_n), (2+s_n)]$ and define 
\begin{align}
    \overline{p}_m(x) &\coloneqq \begin{cases}
        p_m(-(2+s_n)) , & x < -(2+s_n) \\
        p_m(x) , & -(2+s_n) \le x \le 2+s_n \\
        p_m(2+s_n) , & x > 2+s_n
    \end{cases} . \notag 
\end{align}
It immediately follows that 
\begin{align}
    V(\overline{p}_m) &\le V_{[-(2+s_n),2+s_n]}(p_m) \le C_\tau (m+1)^3 , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last inequality is from \zcref[S]{eqn:V}. 
Recalling from \zcref[S]{eqn:spiked_model} that $ Y - X $ has rank $1$, \zcref[S]{lem:tr} guarantees that 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n \overline{p}_m(\lambda_i(Y)) - \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n \overline{p}_m(\lambda_i(X))\mright|
    = \mleft|\frac{1}{n} \operatorname{Tr}(\overline{p}_m(Y)) - \frac{1}{n} \operatorname{Tr}(\overline{p}_m(X))\mright| &\le \frac{V(\overline{p}_m)}{n} \le \frac{C_\tau (m+1)^3}{n} . \label{eqn:close4} 
\end{align}
On the high-probability event where \zcref[S]{eqn:rig} holds, we have $ \overline{p}_m(\lambda_i(X)) = p_m(\lambda_i(X)) $ for every $ 1\le i\le n $. 
Similarly, \zcref[S]{eqn:bulk} implies that $ \overline{p}_m(\lambda_i(Y)) = p_m(\lambda_i(Y)) $ for every $ 2\le i\le n $. 
Moreover, by \zcref[S]{eqn:out}, $ \overline{p}_m(\lambda_1(Y)) = p_m(2+s_n) $. 
Therefore, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 2}^n p_m(\lambda_i(Y)) - \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n p_m(\lambda_i(X))\mright|
    &\le \mleft|\frac{1}{n} \operatorname{Tr}(\overline{p}_m(Y)) - \frac{1}{n} \operatorname{Tr}(\overline{p}_m(X))\mright| + \frac{\mleft|p_m(2+s_n)\mright|}{n} \notag \\
    &\le \frac{C_\tau (m+1)^3}{n} + \frac{C_\tau (m+1)}{n}
    \le \frac{2C_\tau (m+1)^3}{n} , \notag 
\end{align}
where we use \zcref[S]{eqn:close4,eqn:psn} in the last line.
Finally, combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:close3} produces the desired estimate \zcref[S]{eqn:LS}. 
This completes the proof of the lemma.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:H-I}
Consider the matrix $H$ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:H} and assume $ d=O(\log(n)) $. 
Then for any fixed constant $ \tau \in (0,2/3) $, there exists a constant $ C_\tau>0 $ depending only on $\tau$ such that with probability $1-o(1)$, it holds that
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|H - I_{d+2}\mright\|_{2} &\le C_\tau d^5 n^{-1+\tau} . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
By the identity \zcref[S]{eqn:prod_sum}, we have that for any $ 0\le k,\ell\le d+1 $, 
\begin{align}
    H_{k,\ell} - \mathds1{\mleft\{k = \ell\mright\}}
    &= \sum_{r = 0}^{\min\mleft\{k,\ell\mright\}} \mleft( \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 2}^n p_{k+\ell-2r}(\lambda_i(Y)) - \mathds1{\mleft\{k+\ell-2r = 0\mright\}} \mright) , \label{eqn:H-I} 
\end{align}
where it is easy to verify that $ \mathds1{\mleft\{k = \ell\mright\}} = \sum_{r = 0}^{\min\mleft\{k,\ell\mright\}} \mathds1{\mleft\{k+\ell-2r=0\mright\}} $. 
The term in parentheses can be bounded using \zcref[S]{lem:LS} with $m$ therein taken to be $ k+\ell-2r $. 
This ensures that with probability $ 1-o(1) $, uniformly for all $ k,\ell,r $, it holds that
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 2}^n p_{k+\ell-2r}(\lambda_i(Y)) - \mathds1{\mleft\{k+\ell-2r = 0\mright\}} \mright|
    &\le C_\tau (k+\ell-2r+1)^3 n^{-1+\tau} . \notag 
\end{align}
Plugging this back in \zcref[S]{eqn:H-I}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|H_{k,\ell} - \mathds1{\mleft\{k = \ell\mright\}}\mright|
    &\le C_\tau n^{-1+\tau} \sum_{r = 0}^{\min\mleft\{k,\ell\mright\}} (k+\ell-2r+1)^3 \notag \\
    &\le C_\tau n^{-1+\tau} \cdot \mleft(\min\mleft\{k,\ell\mright\} + 1\mright) \mleft(k+\ell+1\mright)^3 \notag \\
    &\le C_\tau n^{-1+\tau} \cdot (d+2)(2(d+1)+1)^3
    \le C_\tau n^{-1+\tau} \cdot (3d)^4 , \notag
\end{align}
where in the second line, we upper bound each summand by the largest one. 
To turn this entry-wise estimate into an operator norm bound, we simply apply the elementary inequality 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|A\mright\|_{2} \le p \max_{1\le i,j\le p} \mleft|A_{i,j}\mright| \label{eqn:op}
\end{align}
for any $p\times p$ matrix $A$. 
Specifically, we have that with probability $1 - o(1)$, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|H - I_{d+2}\mright\|_{2} &\le (d+2) \max_{0\le k,\ell\le d+1} \mleft|H_{k,\ell} - \mathds1{\mleft\{k=\ell\mright\}}\mright| \le C_\tau n^{-1+\tau} \cdot (3d)^5 , \notag 
\end{align}
completing the proof.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:G-H}
Consider the matrix $G$ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:G}. 
Assume $ d = O(\log(n)) $. 
Then there exists an absolute constant $ C>0 $ such that with probability $1-o(1)$, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|G - H\mright\|_{2} &\le C d^3 \sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}} . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
By definitions \zcref[S]{eqn:G,eqn:H} of $H$ and $ G $, respectively, 
\begin{align}
    G_{k,\ell} - H_{k,\ell} &= \sum_{i = 2}^n \frac{p_k(\lambda_i(Y)) p_\ell(\lambda_i(Y))}{n} (g_i^2 - 1) , \label{eqn:G-H} 
\end{align}
where $ g_i $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:g}. 
As in the proof of \zcref[S]{lem:LS}, henceforth we work on the high-probability event where \zcref[S]{eqn:rig,eqn:bulk,eqn:out} simultaneously hold.                                      
In particular, since \zcref[S]{eqn:bulk} holds, \zcref[S]{eqn:psn} ensures that uniformly for all $ 2\le i\le n $ and $ 0\le k,\ell\le d+1 $, 
\begin{align}
    \max\mleft\{\mleft|p_k(\lambda_i(Y))\mright|, \mleft|p_\ell(\lambda_i(Y))\mright|\mright\} &\le C_\tau \mleft(\max\mleft\{k,\ell\mright\}+1\mright) \le C_\tau' d . \notag 
\end{align}
This implies that 
\begin{align}
&&
    \max_{2\le i\le n} \mleft| \frac{p_k(\lambda_i(Y)) p_\ell(\lambda_i(Y))}{n} \mright| &\le C_\tau \frac{d^2}{n} , & 
    \sum_{i = 2}^n \mleft(\frac{p_k(\lambda_i(Y)) p_\ell(\lambda_i(Y))}{n}\mright)^2 &\le C_\tau \frac{d^4}{n} . & 
& \label{eqn:psi1}
\end{align}
Note also that $ (g_i^2 - 1)_{i = 1}^n $ are i.i.d.\ centered sub-exponential random variables with finite $ \psi_1 $-norm. 
Therefore, the RHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:G-H} is a weighted sum of i.i.d.\ sub-exponential random variables. 
Using \zcref[S]{eqn:psi1} in the Bernstein inequality (see \cite[Theorem 2.9.1]{Vershynin}), conditioned on $Y$, we have that there exists an absolute constant $c>0$ such that for any $t\ge0$, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft|G_{k,\ell} - H_{k,\ell}\mright| > t \mright) &\le 2 \exp\mleft( -c \min\mleft\{ \frac{n t^2}{d^4} , \frac{n t}{d^2} \mright\} \mright) , \label{eqn:Bernstein}
\end{align}
where the probability is taken only over $ b\sim{\mathcal N}(0_n,I_n/n) $, or equivalently, over $ (g_i)_{i=1}^n \sim {\mathcal N}(0_n,I_n) $. 
Now taking 
\begin{align}
    t &= C d^2 \sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}} \notag
\end{align}
for a sufficiently large absolute constant $C>0$, we have 
\begin{align}
    \min\mleft\{ \frac{n t^2}{d^4} , \frac{n t}{d^2} \mright\}
    &= \min\mleft\{ C^2 \log(n) , C \sqrt{n\log(n)} \mright\}
    = C^2  \log(n) , \notag 
\end{align}
for all sufficiently large $n$. 
Then \zcref[S]{eqn:Bernstein} becomes
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft|G_{k,\ell} - H_{k,\ell}\mright| > C d^2 \sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}} \mright) &\le 2 n^{-cC^2} . \notag 
\end{align}
Taking a union bound over $ 0\le k,\ell\le d+1 $ where $ d = O(\log(n)) $, we get that with probability $1-o(1)$, 
\begin{align}
    \max_{0\le k,\ell\le d+1} \mleft|G_{k,\ell} - H_{k,\ell}\mright| &\le C d^2 \sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}} . \notag 
\end{align}
Finally, we invoke \zcref[S]{eqn:op} to conclude that with probability $1-o(1)$, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|G - H\mright\|_{2} &\le (d+2) \max_{0\le k,\ell\le d+1} \mleft|G_{k,\ell} - H_{k,\ell}\mright| \le C d^3 \sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}} , \notag 
\end{align}
as desired.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:event}
Assume $ d = O(\log(n)) $. 
There exists a deterministic positive sequence $ \eta_n \downarrow 0 $ and events $ {\mathcal E}_n \in \sigma(Y, g_2, \cdots, g_n) $ independent of $ g_1 $ satisfying $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n\mright) \to 1 $ such that on $ {\mathcal E}_n $, the following results hold: 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{itm:event1}
    \begin{align}
    &&
        \mleft\|G - I_{d+2}\mright\|_{2} &\le \eta_n , & 
        d \mleft|\lambda_1(Y) - \lambda_\star\mright| &\le \eta_n ; & 
    & \label{eqn:event1} 
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:event2} for every $ f,g\in{\mathcal P}_{d+1} $, 
    \begin{align}
        \mleft| \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 f(\lambda_i(Y)) g(\lambda_i(Y)) - \int f(x) g(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) \mright|
        &\le \eta_n \sqrt{\int f(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)} \sqrt{\int g(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)} ; \label{eqn:event2}
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:event3} for every $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $ with $ \int q^2 \,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\textnormal{sc}} = 1 $, we have
    \begin{align}
    &&
        1 - \eta_n &\le \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2 \le 1 + \eta_n , &
        \mleft| \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) q(\lambda_i(Y))^2 - \int x q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) \mright| &\le 2 \eta_n . &
    & \label{eqn:event2q} 
    \end{align}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
By \zcref[S]{lem:G-H,lem:H-I}, there exists an absolute constant $ C>0 $ such that with probability $1-o(1)$, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|G - I_{d+2}\mright\|_{2} &\le \mleft\|G - H\mright\|_{2} + \mleft\|H - I_{d+2}\mright\|_{2}
    \le C \mleft( d^5 n^{-1+\tau} + d^3 \sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}} \mright) = o(1) . \label{eqn:G-I}
\end{align}
Moreover, since $ \sqrt{n} (\lambda_1(Y) - \lambda_\star) $ has Gaussian fluctuation (see \zcref[S]{prop:out}), one has 
\begin{align}
    d \mleft|\lambda_1(Y) - \lambda_\star\mright| &= O_{{\mathbb P}}(d/\sqrt{n}) = o_{{\mathbb P}}(1) . \label{eqn:lambda}
\end{align}
Inspecting the RHS's of \zcref[S]{eqn:G-I,eqn:lambda} and recalling the assumption $ d=O(\log(n)) $, we see that to make sure that both events in \zcref[S]{eqn:event1} hold, one can take $ \eta_n \downarrow 0 $ sufficiently slowly so that 
\begin{align}
    C \max\mleft\{ \frac{(\log(n))^5}{n^{1 - \tau}} , \frac{(\log(n))^{3.5}}{\sqrt{n}} , \frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{n}} \mright\} \le \eta_n &\le o(1) . \notag 
\end{align} 
For instance, it suffices to take $ \eta_n = (\log(n))^{5.1} / n^{1/3} = o(1) $. 
This proves \zcref[S]{itm:event1}. 

For \zcref[S]{itm:event2}, take any $ f,g \in {\mathcal P}_{d+1} $ and write them in the basis of Chebyshev polynomials: 
\begin{align}
&&
    f(x) &= \sum_{k = 0}^{d+1} a_k p_k(x) , & 
    g(x) &= \sum_{k = 0}^{d+1} b_k p_k(x) . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
By orthonormality of $ (p_k)_{k\ge0} $ with respect to $ \mu_{\textnormal{sc}} $, 
\begin{align}
    \int f(x) g(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)
    &= \sum_{k,\ell=0}^{d+1} a_k b_\ell \int p_k(x) p_\ell(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)
    = a^\top b , \label{eqn:fg_mu}
\end{align}
where $ a \coloneqq (a_k)_{k=0}^{d+1} , b \coloneqq (b_k)_{k=0}^{d+1} $ as usual. 
Similarly, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \int f(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= \mleft\|a\mright\|_{2}^2 , & 
    \int g(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= \mleft\|b\mright\|_{2}^2 . & 
& \label{eqn:norm}
\end{align}
On the other hand, 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 f(\lambda_i(Y)) g(\lambda_i(Y))
    &= \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 \sum_{k,\ell = 0}^{d+1} a_k b_\ell p_k(\lambda_i(Y)) p_\ell(\lambda_i(Y)) \notag \\
    &= \sum_{k,\ell = 0}^{d+1} a_k b_\ell \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 p_k(\lambda_i(Y)) p_\ell(\lambda_i(Y)) 
    = \sum_{k,\ell = 0}^{d+1} a_k b_\ell G_{k,\ell}
    = a^\top G b , \label{eqn:fg_xi}
\end{align}
So the difference between \zcref[S]{eqn:fg_xi,eqn:fg_mu} is
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 f(\lambda_i(Y)) g(\lambda_i(Y)) - \int f(x) g(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) \mright|
    &= \mleft|a^\top (G - I_{d+2}) b\mright|
    \le \mleft\|G - I_{d+2}\mright\|_{2} \mleft\|a\mright\|_{2} \mleft\|b\mright\|_{2} . \notag 
\end{align}
Applying \zcref[S]{eqn:event1,eqn:norm} establishes \zcref[S]{eqn:event2}. 

Finally, take any $ q \in {\mathcal P}_d $ with $ \int q^2 \,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\textnormal{sc}} = 1 $. 
Applying \zcref[S]{eqn:event2} with $ f = g = q $ gives the first result in \zcref[S]{eqn:event2q} since $ \int f^2 \,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\textnormal{sc}} = \int g^2 \,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\textnormal{sc}} = \int fg \,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\textnormal{sc}} $ all of which are equal to $ \int q^2 \,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\textnormal{sc}} = 1 $. 
Applying \zcref[S]{eqn:event2} with $ f(x) = x q(x) \in {\mathcal P}_{d+1} $ and $ g = q \in {\mathcal P}_d $ yields the second result in \zcref[S]{eqn:event2q} since 
\begin{align}
    \int f(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= \int x^2 q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x)
    \le \sup\mleft\{x^2 : x \in \operatorname{supp}(\mu_{\textnormal{sc}})\mright\} \cdot \int q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) = 4 . \notag 
\end{align}
This completes the proof of \zcref[S]{itm:event3} and therefore the entire lemma.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:K}
The following results hold: 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{itm:K1} for $ t = \lambda_\star $ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda_star}, it holds that 
    \begin{align}
        p_k(\lambda_\star) &= \frac{\lambda^{k+1} - \lambda^{-(k+1)}}{\lambda - \lambda^{-1}} , \label{eqn:plam} \\
        K_d(\lambda_\star, \lambda_\star) &= \frac{1}{(\lambda - \lambda^{-1})^2} \sum_{k = 0}^d (\lambda^{k+1} - \lambda^{-(k+1)})^2 
        = \frac{\lambda^{2(d+3)}}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^3} (1 + o(1)) ; \label{eqn:Klam}
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:K2} for $ t = t_n $ satisfying $ d \mleft|t_n - \lambda_\star\mright| \to 0 $, it holds that 
    \begin{align}
    &&
        K_d(\lambda_\star, t_n) &= (1+o(1)) K_d(\lambda_\star, \lambda_\star) , & 
        K_d(t_n,t_n) &= (1+o(1)) K_d(\lambda_\star, \lambda_\star) . & 
    & \notag                         
    \end{align}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
If $ t = \lambda_\star > 0 $, then \zcref[S]{eqn:plam} follows by noting that for any $t>2$, $ \beta(t) $ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:beta} is the unique solution in $ [1,\infty) $ to $ t = \beta + 1/\beta $, and hence $ \lambda = \beta(\lambda_\star) $. 
Using \zcref[S]{eqn:plam} and recalling from \zcref[S]{eqn:Kd} the definition of $ K_d $ yield
\begin{align}
    K_d(\lambda_\star, \lambda_\star) &= \sum_{k = 0}^d p_k(\lambda_\star)^2 
    = \frac{1}{(\lambda - \lambda^{-1})^2} \sum_{k = 0}^d (\lambda^{k+1} - \lambda^{-(k+1)})^2 \notag \\
    &= \frac{1}{(\lambda - \lambda^{-1})^2} \sum_{k = 0}^d \mleft( \lambda^{2(k+1)} + \lambda^{-2(k+1)} - 2 \mright) \notag \\
    &= \frac{\lambda^2}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^2} \mleft( \lambda^2 \frac{\lambda^{2(d+1)} - 1}{\lambda^2 - 1} + \lambda^{-2} \frac{1 - \lambda^{-2(d+1)}}{1 - \lambda^{-2}} - 2(d+1) \mright) \notag \\
    &= \frac{\lambda^2}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^2} \frac{\lambda^{2(d+2)}}{\lambda^2 - 1} (1+o(1))           
    = \frac{\lambda^{2(d+3)}}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^3} (1+o(1)) , \notag 
\end{align}
where the asymptotic expansions are with respect to $ d\to\infty $. 
This proves \zcref[S]{eqn:Klam} and therefore \zcref[S]{itm:K1}. 

Turning to \zcref[S]{itm:K2}, recall from \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda1} that $ \lambda_\star>2 $. 
Let $ I\subset(2,\infty) $ be a compact interval such that $ I \ni \lambda_\star $. 
On $I$, $ \beta(t) $ is a continuously differentiable function. 
Expanding $ \log(\beta(t)) $ around $ \lambda_\star $ gives 
\begin{align}
    \log(\beta(t)) &= \log(\beta(\lambda_\star)) + \frac{t - \lambda_\star}{\sqrt{\lambda_\star^2 - 4}} + O\mleft( (t - \lambda_\star)^2 \mright) 
    = \log(\lambda) + O\mleft(\mleft|t - \lambda_\star\mright|\mright) . \notag 
\end{align}
Recalling the assumption $ d \mleft|t - \lambda_\star\mright| \to 0 $, we have that uniformly for every $ 0\le k\le d $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft( \frac{\lambda}{\beta(t)} \mright)^{k+1}
    &= \exp\mleft( (k+1) \mleft[ \log(\lambda) - \log(\beta(t)) \mright] \mright)
    = \exp\mleft( O\mleft( d \mleft|t - \lambda_\star\mright| \mright) \mright)
    = \exp\mleft( o(1) \mright)
    = 1 + o(1) . \notag 
                              
\end{align}
In particular, taking $k=0$ above, we have $ \beta(t) \to \lambda $. 
                                                                                                            
                                                                                                                            
                                    
               
                                                        
                                                                                    
                                                                                                             
                                                                                                           
                                                                                                    
             
      
               
                                                        
                                                                                             
                                                                                                     
             
                                                                
               
                                                        
                                           
             
Therefore,                                           
\begin{align}
    \frac{p_k(t)}{p_k(\lambda_\star)}
    &= \frac{\beta(t)^{k+1} - \beta(t)^{-(k+1)}}{\lambda^{k+1} - \lambda^{-(k+1)}} \cdot \frac{\lambda - \lambda^{-1}}{\beta(t) - \beta(t)^{-1}}
                                                                                                         
    = 1+o(1) , \label{eqn:p_close} 
\end{align}
uniformly for all $ 0\le k\le d $. 
Then 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,t)}{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)} - 1 \mright|
    &= \mleft| \frac{\sum_{k=0}^d p_k(\lambda_\star) p_k(t)}{\sum_{k=0}^d p_k(\lambda_\star)^2} - 1 \mright|
    = \mleft| \frac{\sum_{k=0}^d p_k(\lambda_\star)^2 (p_k(t)/p_k(\lambda_\star) - 1)}{\sum_{k=0}^d p_k(\lambda_\star)^2} \mright| \notag \\
    &\le \max_{0\le k\le d} \mleft| \frac{p_k(t)}{p_k(\lambda_\star)} - 1 \mright|
    = o(1) . \notag 
\end{align}
The result $ K_d(t,t) = (1+o(1)) K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) $ follows similarly.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Before diving into the proof, we make a few observations as consequences of the preceding lemmas.
Take any $ q \in {\mathcal P}_d\setminus\{0\} $ and normalize it such that 
\begin{align}
    \int q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= 1 . \notag 
\end{align}
By spectral decomposition of $Y$, we have 
\begin{align}
    q(Y) b &= \sum_{i = 1}^n \xi_i q(\lambda_i(Y)) v_i(Y) , \label{eqn:qb}
\end{align}
where $ \xi_i $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:xi}. 
Using \zcref[S]{eqn:qb}, we can write
\begin{align}
&&
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} &= \frac{\xi_1^2 q(\lambda_1(Y))^2}{\sum_{i = 1}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2} , & 
    \frac{\mleft\langleq(Y) b,Y q(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} &= \frac{\sum_{i = 1}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) q(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\sum_{i = 1}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2} . & 
& \label{eqn:ratio} 
\end{align}

Let the events $ {\mathcal E}_n $ and the sequence $ \eta_n $ be as in \zcref[S]{lem:event}. 
Then on $ {\mathcal E}_n $, \zcref[S]{eqn:event2q} holds. 
Also, by \zcref[S]{itm:extr_pt} of \zcref[S]{lem:extr}, $ q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 \le K_d(\lambda_1(Y), \lambda_1(Y)) $. 
Since the event \zcref[S]{eqn:event1} holds on $ {\mathcal E}_n $, by \zcref[S]{itm:K2} of \zcref[S]{lem:K}, 
\begin{align}
    q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 \le (1+o(1)) K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) . \label{eqn:qlambda1}
\end{align}

We now prove the claimed results item by item. 

\paragraph{Proof of \zcref[S]{itm:opt}.}
We first upper bound $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) $. 
On $ {\mathcal E}_n $, by \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio,eqn:event2q,eqn:qlambda1}, for any $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}
    &= 1 + \frac{\sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\xi_1^2 q(\lambda_1(Y))^2}
    \ge 1 + \frac{1 - \eta_n}{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)} . \notag 
\end{align}
Flipping the ratio, we have 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &\le \frac{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) + 1 - \eta_n} . \label{eqn:ratio_xi} 
\end{align}
Recalling the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:OV}, taking expectation of \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio_xi} and using the law of total expectation, we have 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda))
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, \mathds1_{{\mathcal E}_n} \mright] \mright] \notag \\
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n\mright) \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, {\mathcal E}_n \mright] + \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n^c\mright) \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, {\mathcal E}_n^c \mright] \notag \\
    &\le \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, {\mathcal E}_n \mright] + o(1) , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last step follows since $ \mleft\langlev_1(Y),\cdot\mright\rangle^2 / \mleft\|\cdot\mright\|_{2}^2 \in [0,1] $ and $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n\mright)\to1 $. 
Since $ {\mathcal E}_n\in\sigma(Y,g_2, \cdots, g_n) $ is independent of $ g_1 $, conditioning on $ {\mathcal E}_n $ does not alter the distribution of $ g_1 = \sqrt{n} \xi_1 \sim {\mathcal N}(0,1) $. 
Using this with \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio_xi}, we have 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda))
    &\le \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) + 1 - \eta_n} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, {\mathcal E}_n \mright] + o(1) \notag \\
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{g_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)/n}{g_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)/n + 1} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, {\mathcal E}_n \mright] + o(1)
    = \Phi\mleft( \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n} \mright) + o(1) , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last line is by continuity of $\Phi$ and the fact that $ \eta_n\downarrow0 $. 
Since this holds uniformly over all $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $, maximizing over such $q$ gives the upper bound
\begin{align}
    \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &\le \Phi\mleft( \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n} \mright) + o(1) . \label{eqn:OPT_ub} 
\end{align}

Next, we show a matching lower bound. 
This amounts to exhibiting a polynomial $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $ whose value of $ {\mathcal O}_{n,d} $ asymptotically attains the RHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:OPT_ub}. 
Consider the polynomial $ q_{{\mathrm{opt}}} \in {\mathcal P}_d $ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qopt}. 
               
                                                                                                                         
             
By \zcref[S]{itm:extr_pt} of \zcref[S]{lem:extr}, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \int q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= 1 , & 
    q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(\lambda_\star)^2 &= K_d(\lambda_\star, \lambda_\star) . &  
& \notag 
\end{align}
On $ {\mathcal E}_n $, \zcref[S]{eqn:event1} holds, so by \zcref[S]{itm:K2} of \zcref[S]{lem:K}, 
\begin{align}
    q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(\lambda_1(Y))^2 &= \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star, \lambda_1(Y))^2}{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}
    = (1+o(1)) K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) . \label{eqn:qopt} 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:event2q,eqn:ratio,eqn:qopt}, following similar reasoning leading to \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio_xi}, we obtain
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &\ge \frac{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) + 1 + \eta_n} . \label{eqn:qopt_lb} 
\end{align}
Taking expectation, using independence between $ {\mathcal E}_n,\xi_1 $, continuity of $\Phi$, and the facts $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n\mright)\to1, \eta_n\to0 $, we further have 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q_{{\mathrm{opt}}},\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &\ge \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n\mright) \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, {\mathcal E}_n \mright] \notag \\
    &\ge (1-o(1)) \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{(1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) + 1 + \eta_n} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, {\mathcal E}_n \mright]
    = \Phi\mleft( \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n} \mright) - o(1) . \notag
\end{align}
Therefore the same lower bound holds for $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) $. 
This together with \zcref[S]{eqn:OPT_ub} establishes \zcref[S]{itm:opt}. 

\paragraph{Proof of \zcref[S]{itm:val_sub}.}
Assuming \zcref[S]{eqn:cond_sub}, our strategy is again to establish a pair of matching upper and lower bounds. 
For the upper bound, take any $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $ and without loss of optimality assume $ \int q^2 \,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\textnormal{sc}} = 1 $. 
By \zcref[S]{itm:extr_frac} of \zcref[S]{lem:extr}, 
\begin{align}
    \int x q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &\le r_d . \notag 
\end{align}
On $ {\mathcal E}_n $, \zcref[S]{eqn:event2q} holds. 
Therefore, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) q(\lambda_i(Y))^2 &\le r_d + 2 \eta_n , & 
    \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2 \ge 1 - \eta_n . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
For the term involving the outlying eigenvalue, we have 
\begin{align}
    \xi_1^2 \lambda_1(Y) q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 &\le (1+o(1)) \xi_1^2 \lambda_\star K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) \notag 
\end{align}
obtained from \zcref[S]{eqn:qlambda1,eqn:event1}.
Plugging the above bounds into \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio}, we arrive at
               
                                                          
                                                                                                                                                                    
                                                                                                             
                                                                                                                                
             
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langleq(Y) b,Y q(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{\xi_1^2 \lambda_1(Y) q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 + \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) q(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\xi_1^2 q(\lambda_1(Y))^2 + \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2} 
    \notag \\
    &\le \frac{(1+o(1)) \lambda_\star \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) + r_d + 2\eta_n}{\sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 q(\lambda_i(Y))^2}
    \notag \\
    &\le \frac{(1+o(1)) \lambda_\star \xi_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) + r_d + 2\eta_n}{1 - \eta_n}
    \notag \\
    &= \frac{(1+o(1)) \lambda_\star g_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)/n + r_d + 2\eta_n}{1 - \eta_n} . \label{eqn:val_ub} 
\end{align}
Recalling the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:OV}, first conditioning on $ {\mathcal E}_n $ and then taking expectation of \zcref[S]{eqn:val_ub} over $ g_1 \sim {\mathcal N}(0,1) $, using the facts that $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n^c\mright)\to0, \eta_n\downarrow0 $ and the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:cond_sub}, we have that 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal V}_{n,d}(q,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &\le r_d + o(1) , \notag 
\end{align}
uniformly for all $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $. 
Maximizing over such $ q $, we obtain the desired upper bound $ \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) \le r_d + o(1) $. 

For the lower bound, consider the polynomial $ q_{\mathrm{val}} \in {\mathcal P}_d $ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qval}. 
               
                                                                             
             
By \zcref[S]{itm:extr_frac} of \zcref[S]{lem:extr}, $ q_{\mathrm{val}} $ enjoys the following properties: 
\begin{align}
&&
    \int q_{\mathrm{val}}(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= 1 , & 
    \int x q_{\mathrm{val}}(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= r_d . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
On $ {\mathcal E}_n $, by \zcref[S]{eqn:event2q}, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 q_{\mathrm{val}}(\lambda_i(Y))^2 &= 1+ O(\eta_n) , & 
    \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) q_{\mathrm{val}}(\lambda_i(Y))^2 &= r_d + O(\eta_n) . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{itm:extr_pt} of \zcref[S]{lem:extr} and \zcref[S]{itm:K2} of \zcref[S]{lem:K}, on $ {\mathcal E}_n $, 
\begin{align}
    q_{\mathrm{val}}(\lambda_1(Y))^2 &\le K_d(\lambda_1(Y), \lambda_1(Y)) = (1+o(1)) K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star) , \notag 
\end{align}
so by the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:cond_sub}, 
\begin{align}
    \xi_1^2 q_{\mathrm{val}}(\lambda_1(Y))^2 &\le (1+o(1)) g_1^2 K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)/n = o_{{\mathbb P}}(1) . \notag 
\end{align}
The same estimate holds for $ \xi_1^2 \lambda_1(Y) q_{\mathrm{val}}(\lambda_1(Y))^2 $. 
Using these in \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langleq_{\mathrm{val}}(Y) b,Y q_{\mathrm{val}}(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q_{\mathrm{val}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= r_d + o_{{\mathbb P}}(1) . \label{eqn:qval_p} 
\end{align}
To pass to convergence in expectation, note that 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{\mleft\langleq_{\mathrm{val}}(Y) b,Y q_{\mathrm{val}}(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q_{\mathrm{val}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright|
    &\le \mleft\|Y\mright\|_{2} \notag 
\end{align}
the RHS of which has second moment uniformly bounded in $n$ and is therefore uniformly integrable. 
So the LHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:qval_p} converges to the RHS in $ L^1 $, and therefore also in expectation, that is, 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal V}_{n,d}(q_{\mathrm{val}},\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= r_d + o(1) . \notag 
\end{align}
This proves $ \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) \ge r_d + o(1) $, which, combined with the upper bound, in turn proves \zcref[S]{itm:val_sub}. 

\paragraph{Proof of \zcref[S]{itm:val_sup}.}
Now assume \zcref[S]{eqn:cond_sup}.
Let us first establish a lower bound. 
Consider again $ q_{{\mathrm{opt}}} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qopt}. 
Passing to the $n\to\infty$ limit on the RHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:qopt_lb}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} &\overset{\mathrm{p}}{\to} 1 . \notag 
\end{align}
Since the ratio on the LHS is point-wise nonnegative and at most $1$, and hence uniformly integrable, we also have convergence in $ L^1 $: 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} &\overset{L^1}{\to} 1 . \label{eqn:L1} 
\end{align}
Now define, for each $i\in[n]$, 
\begin{align}
    w_i &\coloneqq \frac{\xi_i^2 q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\sum_{j = 1}^n \xi_j^2 q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(\lambda_j(Y))^2} . \label{eqn:w} 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio}, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} &= w_1 , & 
    \frac{\mleft\langleq_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b,Y q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \sum_{i = 1}^n w_i \lambda_i(Y) . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
By definition \zcref[S]{eqn:w}, $ \sum_{i = 1}^n w_i = 1 $ and $ w_i\ge0 $ for all $i\in[n]$. 
By \zcref[S]{eqn:L1}, $ w_1 \to 1 $ in $ L^1 $. 
Therefore, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{\mleft\langleq_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b,Y q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} - \lambda_1(Y) \mright|
    &= \mleft| \sum_{i = 1}^n (\lambda_i(Y) - \lambda_1(Y)) w_i \mright|
    = \mleft| \sum_{i = 2}^n (\lambda_i(Y) - \lambda_1(Y)) w_i \mright| \notag \\
    &\le \mleft( \max_{2\le i\le n} \mleft|\lambda_i(Y) - \lambda_1(Y)\mright| \mright) \cdot \mleft| \sum_{i = 2}^n w_i \mright| \notag \\
    &\le \mleft( \max_{2\le i\le n} \mleft|\lambda_i(Y)\mright| + \mleft|\lambda_1(Y)\mright| \mright) \cdot (1 - w_1) \notag \\
    &\le 2 \mleft\|Y\mright\|_{2} \cdot (1 - w_1) . \notag 
\end{align}
Since $ \mleft\|Y\mright\|_{2} $ has uniformly bounded second moment and $ 1 - w_1 \to 0 $ in $ L^1 $, the RHS above converges to $0$ in $ L^1 $. 
Moreover, $ \lambda_1(Y) \to \lambda_\star $ in $ L^1 $. 
We conclude
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langleq_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b,Y q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q_{{\mathrm{opt}}}(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &\overset{L^1}{\to} \lambda_\star , \notag 
\end{align}
which implies convergence in expectation: $ {\mathcal V}_{n,d}(q_{{\mathrm{opt}}},\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) \to \lambda_\star $.
Consequently, $ \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) \ge \lambda_\star - o(1) $. 

The upper bound follows trivially from the fact that 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langleq(Y) b,Y q(Y) b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q(Y) b\mright\|_{2}^2} &\le \lambda_1(Y) . \notag 
\end{align}
Thus $ \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) \le \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\lambda_1(Y)\mright] = \lambda_\star + o(1) $. 

\paragraph{Proof of \zcref[S]{itm:concl}.}
This amounts to studying the criticality conditions \zcref[S]{eqn:cond_sub,eqn:cond_sup}. 
By the asymptotic expansion \zcref[S]{eqn:Klam} and the scaling assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:d} on $d$, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{K_d(\lambda_\star,\lambda_\star)}{n}
    &= n^{-1} \lambda^{2d} \frac{\lambda^{6}}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^3} (1+o(1))
    = n^{-1 + 2 \delta \log(\lambda) + o(1)} . \notag 
\end{align}
Recalling the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:deltac} of $ \delta_c $, it becomes evident that \zcref[S]{eqn:cond_sup} holds whenever $ \delta > \delta_c $ and \zcref[S]{eqn:cond_sub} holds whenever $ \delta < \delta_c $. 
The result \zcref[S]{eqn:lim} then follows by verifying 
\begin{align}
&&
    \lim_{t\to0} \Phi(t) &= 0 , & 
    \lim_{t\to\infty} \Phi(t) &= 1 , & 
    \lim_{d\to\infty} r_d &= 2 & 
& \label{eqn:lim_Phi} 
\end{align}
using the definitions \zcref[S]{eqn:Phi,eqn:rd}. 
This concludes the proof of \zcref[S]{thm:spike}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
\cite[Section 2.4]{Montanari_Venkataramanan} considers Bayes-AMP for a more general model of $ Y $ where $ \mleft\|v\mright\|_{2} \to 1 $ and the empirical distribution of the elements in $ \sqrt{n} \, v $ converges weakly to a probability measure $ \nu $ with unit second moment. 
The Bayes-AMP for this more general model reads: 
\begin{subequations}
\label{eqn:MV}
\begin{align}
&&
    v^{t+1} &= Y f_t(v^t) - b_t f_{t-1}(v^{t-1}) , & 
    b_t &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n f_t'(v_i^t) , &
