& \label{eqn:BAMP_gen} 
\end{align}
where 
\begin{align}
&&
    f_t(v) &= \lambda \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ {\mathsf{V}} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, \alpha_t {\mathsf{V}} + \sqrt{\alpha_t} {\mathsf{X}} = v \mright] , &
    ({\mathsf{V}}, {\mathsf{X}}) &\sim \nu \otimes {\mathcal N}(0,1) . & 
& \label{eqn:f_gen}
\end{align}
The constants $ (\alpha_t)_{t\ge0} $ in the above display are defined recursively by: 
\begin{align}
\begin{split}
    \alpha_0 &= \lambda^2 - 1 , \\
    \alpha_{t+1} &= \lambda^2 \mleft( 1 - \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft({\mathsf{V}} - \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, \sqrt{\alpha_t} {\mathsf{V}} + {\mathsf{X}}\mright]\mright)^2\mright] \mright) 
    = \lambda^2 \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, \alpha_t {\mathsf{V}} + \sqrt{\alpha_t} {\mathsf{X}}\mright]^2 \mright] , 
\end{split}
\label{eqn:SE_gen}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
where the last equality is by the tower property of conditional expectation. 
Specializing \zcref[S]{eqn:MV} to our model \zcref[S]{eqn:spiked_model_prior}, it is easy to verify that 
\begin{align}
&&
    f_t(v) &= \frac{\lambda v}{\alpha_t + 1} , & 
    b_t &= \frac{\lambda}{\alpha_t + 1} , & 
    \alpha_{t+1} &= \lambda^2 \frac{\alpha_t}{\alpha_t+1} . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
Also, $ \alpha \coloneqq \lambda^2 - 1 $ is the unique nontrivial fixed point of the recursion for $ \alpha_t $, and hence $ \alpha_t = \alpha $ for all $t\ge0$. 
This also implies that $ f_t $ is Lipschitz continuous, as required by \cite[Theorem 2]{Montanari_Venkataramanan}. 
So \zcref[S]{eqn:MV} is reduced to \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP} and the conclusion of \cite[Theorem 2]{Montanari_Venkataramanan} then translates to the claimed Wasserstein-$2$ convergence result. 
Moreover, $ ({\mathsf{X}}_1, \cdots, {\mathsf{X}}_t) $ forms a centered Gaussian process whose covariance can be recursively specified as follows.
By \cite[Equation (26)]{Feng_etal}, for any $ s,t\ge1 $, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ (\sqrt{\alpha_{s+1}} {\mathsf{X}}_{s+1}) (\sqrt{\alpha_{t+1}} {\mathsf{X}}_{t+1}) \mright]
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ f_s( \alpha_s {\mathsf{V}} + \sqrt{\alpha_s} {\mathsf{X}}_s ) f_t( \alpha_t {\mathsf{V}} + \sqrt{\alpha_t} {\mathsf{X}}_t ) \mright] . \label{eqn:cov} 
\end{align}

The first limit in \zcref[S]{eqn:SE_E} is a direct consequence of the Wasserstein-$2$ convergence result. 
Indeed, letting $ {\mathsf{V}}_t \coloneqq \alpha {\mathsf{V}} + \sqrt{\alpha} {\mathsf{X}}_t $, we have that for every $ t\ge1 $, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev,v^t\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|v\mright\|_{2}^2 \mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] 
    &= \frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}} \, {\mathsf{V}}_t\mright]^2}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}^2\mright] \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t^2\mright]}
    = \frac{\alpha^2}{\alpha^2 + \alpha} = 1 - \frac{1}{\lambda^2} . \notag 
\end{align}
For the second limit, using the update rule \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMPt}, we have that for any $t\ge1$, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev^{t},Y v^{t}\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|v^{t}\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright]
    &= \lambda \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev^t,v^{t+1}\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] + \frac{1}{\lambda} \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\mleft\langlev^t,v^{t-1}\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright] \notag \\
    &= \lambda \frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t {\mathsf{V}}_{t+1}\mright]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t^2\mright]} + \frac{1}{\lambda} \frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t {\mathsf{V}}_{t-1}\mright]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t^2\mright]} \notag \\
    &= \lambda \frac{\alpha^2 + \alpha x_t}{\alpha^2 + \alpha} + \frac{1}{\lambda} \frac{\alpha^2 + \alpha x_{t-1}}{\alpha^2 + \alpha} , \label{eqn:xt} 
\end{align}
where $ x_t \coloneqq \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{X}}_t {\mathsf{X}}_{t+1}\mright] $. 
By \zcref[S]{eqn:cov}, we have the following recursion for $ x_t $: 
\begin{align}
    \alpha x_t &= \mleft( \frac{\lambda}{\alpha+1} \mright)^2 \mleft( \alpha^2 + \alpha x_{t-1} \mright) , \label{eqn:xt_recur} 
\end{align}
from which it is easy to show that $ x_t $ converges to the unique fixed point $1$ as $t\to\infty$. 
Using this in \zcref[S]{eqn:xt} produces the second result in \zcref[S]{eqn:SE_E}. 

Finally, the asymptotic alignment between $ v^t $ and $ v_1(Y) $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:align} can be established following similar steps in the proof of \cite[Lemma E.4]{Zhang_Mondelli}. 
We briefly spell out the details. 
Let $ e^t \coloneqq v^{t+1} - v^t $ and $ C\in(0,\infty) $ be a constant sufficiently large so that almost surely $ Z \coloneqq Y + \lambda C I_n $ is strictly positive definite for all large $n$, i.e., $ \lim_{n\to\infty} \lambda_n(Z) > 0 $. 
Now it is easy to check that the iteration \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMPt} is identical to
               
                                 
                                                                                                
                                                                                    
               
             
\begin{align}
&&
    v^{t+1} 
                                                    
                                                         
                                                              
    &= \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} v^t 
    + \widehat{e}^t , & 
    \textnormal{where } 
    \widehat{e}^t &\coloneqq \frac{\lambda^{-2} + C}{1 + \lambda^{-2} + C} e^t + \frac{\lambda^{-2}}{1+\lambda^{-2}+C} e^{t-1} . &
& \notag 
\end{align}
Assume $t,s$ are both large constants. 
Unrolling $ v^{t+s} $ down to $ v^t $, we obtain 
\begin{align}
&&
    v^{t+s} &= \mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} \mright)^s v^t + \widehat{e}^{t,s} , & 
    \textnormal{where } 
    \widehat{e}^{t,s} &= \sum_{r=0}^{s-1} \mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} \mright)^{s-1-r} \widehat{e}^{t+r} . &
& \label{eqn:powitr} 
\end{align}
We compute the limit of the normalized squared norm of both sides. 
For the error term on the RHS, we claim that 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{s\to\infty} \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} n^{-1} \mleft\|\widehat{e}^{t,s}\mright\|_{2}^2 &= 0 . \label{eqn:error} 
\end{align}
For the other term on the RHS, 
\begin{align}
    n^{-1} \mleft\|\mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} \mright)^s v^t\mright\|_{2}^2
    &= n^{-1} \mleft\|\mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} \mright)^s v_1(Y) v_1(Y)^\top v^t\mright\|_{2}^2
    + n^{-1} \mleft\|\mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} \mright)^s (I_n - v_1(Y) v_1(Y)^\top) v^t\mright\|_{2}^2 . \notag 
\end{align}
The first part equals 
\begin{align}
    \mleft(\frac{\lambda_1(Y) + \lambda C}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C}\mright)^{2s} \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),v^t\mright\rangle^2}{n} . \label{eqn:part1} 
\end{align}
The second part can be upper bounded as 
\begin{align}
    n^{-1} \mleft\|\mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} (I_n - v_1(Y) v_1(Y)^\top) \mright)^s v^t\mright\|_{2}^2
                                                                                                                                                               
    &\le \frac{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2}{n} \sigma_1\mleft( \mleft(\frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} (I_n - v_1(Y) v_1(Y)^\top)\mright)^{s} \mright)^2 \notag \\
    &= \frac{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2}{n} \sigma_1\mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} (I_n - v_1(Y) v_1(Y)^\top) \mright)^{2s} \notag \\
    &= \frac{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2}{n} \sigma_2\mleft( \frac{Z}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} \mright)^{2s} \notag \\
    &= \frac{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2}{n} \mleft( \frac{\lambda_2(Y) + \lambda C}{\lambda_\star + \lambda C} \mright)^{2s} . \label{eqn:part2} 
\end{align}
Moreover, by state evolution, for any $t$,
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} n^{-1} \mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2 &= \alpha^2 + \alpha = \lambda^2 (\lambda^2 - 1) \in (0,\infty) . \notag 
\end{align}
Combining this with $ \lambda_2(Y) \to 2 < \lambda_\star $ (as $n\to\infty$), we see that \zcref[S]{eqn:part2} vanishes under the limits $n\to\infty$ followed by $t\to\infty$ and then $ s\to\infty $. 
Therefore, taking the normalized squared norm and the same limit on both sides of \zcref[S]{eqn:powitr} and using \zcref[S]{eqn:part1,eqn:part2,eqn:error}, we conclude 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),v^t\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|v^t\mright\|_{2}^2} &= 1 . \notag 
\end{align}

To complete the proof of \zcref[S]{eqn:align}, it remains to justify \zcref[S]{eqn:error}. 
By state evolution, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} n^{-1} \mleft\|e^t\mright\|_{2}^2
    &= \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} \mleft( n^{-1} \mleft\|v^{t+1}\mright\|_{2}^2 + n^{-1} \mleft\|v^{t}\mright\|_{2}^2 - 2 n^{-1} \mleft\langlev^{t+1},v^t\mright\rangle \mright) \notag \\
    &= 2 (\alpha^2 + \alpha) - 2 \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} (\alpha^2 + \alpha x_t)
    = 0 , \notag 
\end{align}
by recalling from \zcref[S]{eqn:xt_recur} that $ x_t = \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{X}}_{t+1} {\mathsf{X}}_{t}\mright] \to 1 $ as $t\to\infty$. 
This implies $ \lim_{t\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} n^{-1} \mleft\|\widehat{e}^t\mright\|_{2}^2 = 0 $. 
Using this along with the triangle inequality and the fact $ \lim_{n\to\infty} \lambda_1(Z) < \infty $ proves \zcref[S]{eqn:error}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:AMP_poly}
Consider the Bayes-AMP algorithm \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP}. 
Then for every $t\ge0$, the iterate $ v^{t+1} $ can be written as 
\begin{align}
    v^{t+1} &= \frac{1}{\lambda^{t+1}} p_{t+1}(Y) v^0 , \label{eqn:ind} 
\end{align}
where $ (p_{t+1})_{t\ge0} $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:p}.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
This can be proved by induction on time. 
The $ t=0 $ case holds by \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMP0} and the definition $ p_1(x) = x $. 
For $ t=1 $, according to the update rule \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMPt}, 
\begin{align}
    v^2 &= \frac{1}{\lambda} Y v^1 - \frac{1}{\lambda^2} v^0
    = \frac{1}{\lambda^2} \mleft( Y^2 - I_n \mright) v^0 . \notag 
\end{align}
By the definition of $ p_2(x) = x^2 - 1 $, $ v^2 = p_2(Y) v^0 / \lambda^2 $. 

For any $t\ge2$, assuming the validity of \zcref[S]{eqn:ind} up to time $t-1$, we verify its validity for $ t $.
By \zcref[S]{eqn:BAMPt} and the induction hypothesis, 
\begin{align}
    v^{t+1} &= \frac{1}{\lambda} Y \frac{p_t(Y) v^0}{\lambda^{t}} - \frac{1}{\lambda^2} \frac{p_{t-1}(Y) v^0}{\lambda^{t-1}}           
    = \frac{1}{\lambda^{t+1}} \mleft( Y p_t(Y) - p_{t-1}(Y) \mright) v^0
    = \frac{1}{\lambda^{t+1}} p_{t+1}(Y) v^0 , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last step follows from the three-term recurrence relation \zcref[S]{eqn:recur} for $ p_{t+1} $. 
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By \zcref[S]{lem:AMP_poly}, it suffices to study $ {\mathcal O}_{n,t}(p_t,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) $ and $ {\mathcal V}_{n,t}(p_t,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) $ since both quantities are invariant under nonzero scaling of $ p_t $. 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        
                                                                                                                                                                                                    
  
We first collect a few facts about $ p_t $. 
By orthonormality with respect to $ \mu_{\textnormal{sc}} $ and the recurrence relation \zcref[S]{eqn:recur}, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \int p_t(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= 1 , &
    \int x p_t(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) &= \int p_{t+1}(x) p_t(x) + p_{t-1}(x) p_t(x) \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) = 0 . &
& \label{eqn:pt} 
\end{align}
Recalling from \zcref[S]{eqn:plam} the explicit expression of $ p_t(\lambda_\star) $, we have 
\begin{align}
    p_t(\lambda_\star)^2 &= \mleft( \frac{\lambda^{t+1} - \lambda^{-(t+1)}}{\lambda - \lambda^{-1}} \mright)^2 
    = \frac{\lambda^{2(t+2)}}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^2} (1+o(1)) , \label{eqn:pt2} 
\end{align}
where the last asymptotic expansion is with respect to $ t\to\infty $. 

Next, by \zcref[S]{eqn:p_close}, on the event $ {\mathcal E}_n $ from \zcref[S]{lem:event} where \zcref[S]{eqn:event1} holds, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mleft( \frac{p_t(\lambda_1(Y))}{p_t(\lambda_\star)} \mright)^2 &= 1 + o(1) . \label{eqn:pt_close} 
\end{align}
Moreover, by \zcref[S]{itm:event3} of \zcref[S]{lem:event} and \zcref[S]{eqn:pt}, 
\begin{align}
&&
    1 - \eta_n &\le \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2 \le 1 + \eta_n , & 
    \mleft| \sum_{i = 2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2 \mright| &\le 2 \eta_n , & 
& \label{eqn:pt_fact} 
\end{align}
where $ \xi_i $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:xi} with $ b = v^0 $. 

Armed with these facts, we are ready to present the proof of the claimed results. 

Applying spectral decomposition to $Y$, using \zcref[S]{eqn:pt_close,eqn:pt_fact}, we have
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),p_t(Y) v^0\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|p_t(Y) v^0\mright\|_{2}^2} &= \frac{\xi_1^2 p_t(\lambda_1(Y))^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_i^2 p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2} \label{eqn:Opt} \\
    &\in \mleft[ \frac{(1 - o(1)) g_1^2 p_t(\lambda_\star)^2/n}{1 + \eta_n + (1+o(1)) g_1^2 p_t(\lambda_\star)^2/n} , \frac{(1 + o(1)) g_1^2 p_t(\lambda_\star)^2/n}{1 - \eta_n + (1-o(1)) g_1^2 p_t(\lambda_\star)^2/n} \mright] , \notag 
\end{align}
where $ g_i $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:g}. 
Since $ {\mathcal E}_n $ is independent of $ g_1 $ and satisfies $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_n\mright)\to1 $, taking expectation, using the fact $ \eta_n\downarrow0 $ and continuity of $ \Phi $ (defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:Phi}), we have 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,t}(p_t,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= \Phi\mleft( \frac{p_t(\lambda_\star)^2}{n} \mright) + o(1) . \label{eqn:Opt_result} 
\end{align}

Turning to $ {\mathcal V}_{n,t}(p_t,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) $, we write
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlep_t(Y) v^0,Y p_t(Y) v^0\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|p_t(Y) v^0\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_i^2 p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2} . \label{eqn:Vpt} 
\end{align}
Consider
\begin{align}
    & \frac{\mleft\langlep_t(Y) v^0,Y p_t(Y) v^0\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|p_t(Y) v^0\mright\|_{2}^2} - \lambda_\star \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),p_t(Y) v^0\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|p_t(Y) v^0\mright\|_{2}^2} \notag \\
    &= \frac{\xi_1^2 (\lambda_1(Y) - \lambda_\star) p_t(\lambda_1(Y))^2 + \sum_{i=2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_i^2 p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2} \notag \\
    &= (\lambda_1(Y) - \lambda_\star) \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),p_t(Y) v^0\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|p_t(Y) v^0\mright\|_{2}^2} + \frac{\sum_{i=2}^n \xi_i^2 \lambda_i(Y) p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_i^2 p_t(\lambda_i(Y))^2} , \notag 
\end{align}
where the first equality is by \zcref[S]{eqn:Vpt,eqn:Opt}. 
On $ {\mathcal E}_n $, using \zcref[S]{eqn:pt_fact}, we can upper bound the above difference as 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{\mleft\langlep_t(Y) v^0,Y p_t(Y) v^0\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|p_t(Y) v^0\mright\|_{2}^2} - \lambda_\star \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(Y),p_t(Y) v^0\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|p_t(Y) v^0\mright\|_{2}^2} \mright|
    &\le \mleft|\lambda_1(Y) - \lambda_\star\mright| 
    + \frac{2\eta_n}{1 - \eta_n} 
    = o(1) . \label{eqn:diff}
\end{align}
Also, a crude upper bound is given by $ \mleft\|Y\mright\|_{2} + \lambda_\star $ whose second moment is uniformly bounded over $n$. 
Thus the expectation of the LHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:diff} is $ o(1) $. 
Combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:Opt_result} just proved, we conclude 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal V}_{n,t}(p_t,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) &= \lambda_\star {\mathcal O}_{n,t}(p_t,\widehat{\operatorname{GOE}}(n,\lambda)) + o(1)
    = \lambda_\star \Phi\mleft( \frac{p_t(\lambda_\star)^2}{n} \mright) + o(1) . \label{eqn:Vpt_result} 
\end{align}

Finally, we pass \zcref[S]{eqn:pt2} to the scaling limit $ t/\log(n)\to\delta $: 
\begin{align}
    \frac{p_t(\lambda_\star)^2}{n} &= \frac{\lambda^{2t}}{n} \cdot \frac{\lambda^4}{(\lambda^2 - 1)^2} \cdot (1+o(1))
    = n^{2\delta\log(\lambda) - 1 + o(1)} , \label{eqn:ptn_lim} 
\end{align}
the RHS of which converges to $ 0 $ whenever $ \delta < \delta_c $ and to $ \infty $ whenever $ \delta > \delta_c $. 
The final result \zcref[S]{eqn:lim_pt} then follows by plugging \zcref[S]{eqn:ptn_lim} into \zcref[S]{eqn:Opt_result,eqn:Vpt_result}, and recalling from \zcref[S]{eqn:lim_Phi} the limits of $ \Phi $ at $ 0 $ and $ \infty $.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
The general state evolution theory for approximate message passing algorithms (see \cite[Section 2.1]{Feng_etal}) guarantees that for any fixed $t\ge1$, the empirical distribution of the rows of $ \begin{bmatrix}v^1 & \cdots & v^t\end{bmatrix} \in {\mathbb R}^{n\times t} $ converges in Wasserstein-$2$ to a centered Gaussian process $ \begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_1 & \cdots & {\mathsf{V}}_t\end{bmatrix} \in {\mathbb R}^t $ whose covariance matrix is specified as follows: $ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_1^2\mright] = 1 $; $ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t {\mathsf{V}}_1\mright] = 0 $ for $t\ge2$; and $ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t {\mathsf{V}}_s\mright] = \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_{t-1} {\mathsf{V}}_{s-1}\mright] $ for $ t,s\ge2 $. 
From this, it follows that $ \begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_1 & \cdots & {\mathsf{V}}_t\end{bmatrix} \sim {\mathcal N}(0_t, I_t) $. 
A straightforward calculation yields: 
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{n} \mleft\langle\widehat{v}^T,X \widehat{v}^T\mright\rangle
    &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{t = 2}^{T+1} \mleft\langlev^t,X v^t\mright\rangle
    + \frac{2}{n} \sum_{2\le s<t\le T+1} \mleft\langlev^s,X v^t\mright\rangle \notag \\
    &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{t = 2}^{T+1} \mleft\langlev^t,v^{t+1} + v^{t-1}\mright\rangle
    + \frac{2}{n} \sum_{2\le s<t\le T+1} \mleft\langlev^s,v^{t+1} + v^{t-1}\mright\rangle \notag \\
    &\to \sum_{t = 2}^{T+1} \mleft( \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t {\mathsf{V}}_{t+1}\mright] + \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_t {\mathsf{V}}_{t-1}\mright] \mright)
    + 2 \sum_{2\le s<t\le T+1} \mleft( \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_s {\mathsf{V}}_{t+1}\mright] + \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_s {\mathsf{V}}_{t-1}\mright] \mright) \label{eqn:EV} \\
    &= 2 \sum_{2\le s\le T} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[{\mathsf{V}}_s^2\mright]
    = 2 (T-1) , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last line follows since the first sum in \zcref[S]{eqn:EV} vanishes and only terms with indices $ 2\le s=t-1<T+1 $ in the second sum survive. 
Moreover, since $ (v^t/\sqrt{n})_{t\ge1} $ is asymptotically orthonormal, we have
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{n} \mleft\|\widehat{v}^T\mright\|_{2}^2
    &\to \sum_{2\le t\le T+1} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ {\mathsf{V}}_t^2 \mright] = T . \notag 
\end{align}
Combining the preceding two displays and noting that $ (T-1)/T\ge1-\varepsilon $ for $ T\ge1/\varepsilon $, we obtain the desired result.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Take any $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d\setminus\{0\} $ and decompose it in the Chebyshev basis: $ q(x) = \sum_{k=0}^d a_k p_k(x) $. 
    Denote $ a \coloneqq \begin{bmatrix}a_0 & \cdots & a_d\end{bmatrix}^\top \in {\mathbb R}^{d+1} $. 
                                                                                           
                                                                                  
    Define two random matrices $ \underline{G}, \overline{G} \in {\mathbb R}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)} $ component-wise by 
    $ \underline{G}_{k,\ell} = b^\top p_k(X) p_\ell(X) b $, 
    $ \overline{G}_{k,\ell} = b^\top p_k(X) X p_\ell(X) b $
    for all $ k,\ell\in\{0,1,\cdots,d\} $. 
    Now we can write the Rayleigh quotient as
    \begin{align}
        \frac{\mleft\langleq(X)b,Xq(X)b\mright\rangle}{\mleft\|q(X)b\mright\|_{2}^2}
        &= \frac{a^\top \overline{G} a}{a^\top \underline{G} a} . \notag 
    \end{align}
    By standard Gaussian concentration and weak convergence of GOE spectrum \zcref[S]{eqn:ESD_cvg}, one has 
    \begin{align}
        b^\top f(X) b &\xrightarrow[]{\mathbb{P}} \int f(x) \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x , \notag 
    \end{align}
    for any fixed polynomial $f$. 
    Using this, orthonormality of $ (p_k)_{k\ge1} $ with respect to $ \mu_{\textnormal{sc}} $ \zcref[S]{eqn:orth} and the three-term recurrence relation \zcref[S]{eqn:recur}, one can easily see that $ \underline{G} \xrightarrow[]{\mathbb{P}} I_{d+1} $ and $ \overline{G} \xrightarrow[]{\mathbb{P}} J $ as $n\to\infty$ and $d$ fixed, where $J$ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:J}. 
    Therefore, 
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{VAL}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &= \max_{a\in{\mathbb R}^{d+1}\setminus\{0_{d+1}\}} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{a^\top \overline{G} a}{a^\top \underline{G} a} \mright]
        \to \max_{a\in{\mathbb R}^{d+1}\setminus\{0_{d+1}\}} \frac{a^\top J}{a^\top a} . \notag 
    \end{align}
    The optimization problem on the RHS is the same as \zcref[S]{eqn:J_opt}. 
    In particular, its optimum equals $ r_d $ and an optimizer is given by $ q_{\mathrm{val}} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:def_qval}. 
    This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:rig_sub}
There exists a universal constant $C>0$ and events $ {\mathcal F}_n $ such that $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal F}_n\mright) \to 1 $ and on $ {\mathcal F}_n $, 
\begin{align}
    \max_{1\le i\le n} \mleft|\lambda_i - \gamma_i\mright| &\le (\log(n))^{C\log\log(n)} n^{-2/3} , \label{eqn:F} 
\end{align}
where $ (\gamma_i)_{1\le i\le n} $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:class_loc}. 
Suppose that \zcref[S]{eqn:sub} holds. 
Then for all sufficiently large $n$, on $ {\mathcal F}_n $, one has 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{itm:rig_eig}
    \begin{align}
    &&
        2 - \frac{1}{2} \le \lambda_1(X) &\le 2 + \frac{1}{10 \cdot d^2} , & 
        \mleft\|X\mright\|_{2} &\le 3 . & 
    & \label{eqn:F_eig}
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:rig_cnt} there exists an absolute constant $ c>0 $ such that every interval $ I \subset[-2,2] $ of length $ \mleft|I\mright| \ge d^{-2} $ contains at least $ c n / d^3 $
    eigenvalues of $ X $. 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The rigidity estimate \zcref[S]{eqn:F} is precisely \zcref[S]{prop:rig}. 
By \zcref[S]{eqn:sub}, we have $ d^{-2} \asymp n^{-2/3 + 2\varepsilon} $ and $ (\log(n))^{C\log\log(n)} n^{-2/3} = o(d^{-2}) $. 
Using this in \zcref[S]{eqn:F} and noting that $ \gamma_1 = 2 $, we obtain the two-sided estimate of $ \lambda_1(X) $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:F_eig}. 
Likewise, since $ \gamma_n\to-2 $, $ \lambda_n(X) \ge -3 $ for all large $n$, implying $ \mleft\|X\mright\|_{2} \le 3 $. 
This proves \zcref[S]{itm:rig_eig}. 

Turning to \zcref[S]{itm:rig_cnt}, take any $ I\subset[-2,2] $ of length $ \mleft|I\mright| = d^{-2} $. 
Let $ I' \subset I $ be the concentric subinterval of $I$ of length $ \mleft|I\mright|/2 $. 
For all large $n$, $ (\log(n))^{C\log\log(n)} n^{-2/3} \le d^{-2}/4 $. 
Therefore, any eigenvalue of $X$ whose classical location lies in $ I' $ must itself lie in $ I $, and hence, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : \lambda_i(X) \in I \mright\}\mright| \ge \mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : \gamma_i\in I' \mright\}\mright| . \label{eqn:cnt1}
\end{align}
By the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:class_loc} of classical locations, 
\begin{align}
    \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(I') &\le \frac{1}{n} \mleft(1 + \mleft|\mleft\{i\in[n] : \gamma_i\in I'\mright\}\mright|\mright)  . \label{eqn:cnt2}
\end{align}
Moreover, for any interval $ I_0 \subset [-2,2] $ of length $ \ell \le 1 $, 
\begin{align}
    \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(I_0) &\ge \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}([2-\ell,2]) = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{2-\ell}^2 \sqrt{4 - x^2} \,\mathrm{d} x
     = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_0^\ell \sqrt{x(4 - x)} \,\mathrm{d} x
     \ge \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\pi} \int_0^\ell \sqrt{x} \,\mathrm{d} x
     = \frac{\ell^{3/2}}{\pi\sqrt{3}} , \notag 
\end{align}
where we apply the change of variable $ x \mapsto 2 - x $ to obtain the second equality. 
Taking $ I_0 = I' $ and $ \ell = \mleft|I\mright|/2 = d^{-2}/2 $ in the above estimate, in view of \zcref[S]{eqn:cnt1,eqn:cnt2}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\mleft\{i\in[n] : \lambda_i(X) \in I\mright\}\mright|
    &\ge n \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(I') - 1
    \ge \frac{n (d^{-2}/2)^{3/2}}{\pi\sqrt{3}} - 1
    = \frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{3} \, 2^{3/2}} \frac{n}{d^3} - 1 \ge c n/d^3 , \notag 
\end{align}
by \zcref[S]{eqn:sub}. 
This completes the proof of \zcref[S]{itm:rig_cnt} and therefore the lemma.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:q}
Let $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d \setminus \{0\} $ with
\begin{align}
    M &\coloneqq \max_{x\in[-2,2]} \mleft|q(x)\mright| . \label{eqn:M} 
\end{align}
Then the following results hold. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{itm:pol1} There exists an interval $ I\subset[-2,2] $ of length $ \mleft|I\mright| = d^{-2} $ such that 
    \begin{align}
        \min_{x\in I} \mleft|q(x)\mright| &\ge M/2 . \notag 
    \end{align}

    \item \label{itm:pol2}
    \begin{align}
        \max_{x\in[2,2+0.1\cdot d^{-2}]} \mleft|q(x)\mright| &\le 2 M . \label{eqn:2M} 
    \end{align}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
For \zcref[S]{itm:pol1}, let $ x_\star\in[-2,2] $ be a maximizer in \zcref[S]{eqn:M}, i.e., $ \mleft|q(x_\star)\mright| = M $. 
By Markov brothers' inequality (see \zcref[S]{prop:markov}), 
\begin{align}
    \max_{x\in[-2,2]} \mleft|q'(x)\mright| &\le d^2 M / 2 . \notag 
\end{align}
By the fundamental theorem of calculus, for any $ x\in[-2,2] $ with $ \mleft|x - x_\star\mright| \le d^{-2} $, it holds that 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|q(x) - q(x_\star)\mright| &= \mleft|\int^x_{x_\star} q'(x) \,\mathrm{d} x\mright|
    \le d^{-2} \cdot d^2 M / 2 = M / 2 , \notag 
\end{align}
which, combined with the elementary inequality 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \mleft|q(x)\mright| - \mleft|q(x_\star)\mright| \mright| &\le \mleft|q(x) - q(x_\star)\mright| , \notag 
\end{align}
implies that $ \mleft|q(x)\mright| \ge \mleft|q(x_\star)\mright| - M/2 = M/2 $. 
Note that $ \mleft|[-2,2] \cap [x_\star - d^{-2},x_\star + d^{-2}]\mright| \ge d^{-2} $. 
Therefore, an interval that fulfills the requirement of \zcref[S]{itm:pol1} can be found. 

For \zcref[S]{itm:pol2}, note that $ Q(t) \coloneqq q(2t)/M $ satisfies 
\begin{align}
    \max_{t\in[-1,1]} \mleft|Q(t)\mright| &= 1 . \notag 
\end{align}
By a well-known extremal property of Chebyshev polynomials (see \cite[Chapter 2.7.1]{Rivlin}), for any $ t\ge 1 $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|Q(t)\mright| \le T_d(t) , \label{eqn:QT}
\end{align}
where $ T_d $ is the degree-$d$ Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind. 

Next, we claim that for $ t = 1+s $ where $ s\in[0,1] $, it holds that
\begin{align}
    T_d(1+s) \le \exp\mleft(d \sqrt{2s}\mright) . \label{eqn:Td_est}
\end{align}
To see this, recall that on $ [1,\infty) $, $ T_d $ admits the following explicit expression and simple estimate: 
\begin{align}
    T_d(\cosh(t)) &= \cosh\mleft( d t \mright) \le \exp\mleft(dt\mright) . \label{eqn:Td} 
\end{align}
Now take the unique $ t\ge0 $ such that $ 1+s = \cosh(t) $. 
On $ [0,\infty) $, one has the elementary estimate $\cosh(t) \ge 1 + t^2/2$. 
This implies $ t \le \sqrt{2s} $. 
Using this in \zcref[S]{eqn:Td} justifies \zcref[S]{eqn:Td_est}. 

For $ x\in[2, 2 + 0.1 \cdot d^{-2}] $, take $ t = 1 + s = x/2 \le 1 + 0.05 \cdot d^{-2} $ for some $ s\ge0 $. 
Using this in \zcref[S]{eqn:Td_est}, we have $ d \sqrt{2s} \le d \sqrt{0.1 \cdot d^{-2}} = \sqrt{0.1} $. 
Therefore $ T_d(t) \le \exp\mleft(\sqrt{0.1}\mright) < 2 $. 
Plugging this to the RHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:QT}, we finally obtain $ \mleft|q(2t)\mright| / M \le 2 $, uniformly for all $ t\in[1,1+0.05\cdot d^{-2}] $, which is apparently equivalent to the desired \zcref[S]{eqn:2M}. 
This completes the proof of \zcref[S]{itm:pol2} and therefore the entire lemma.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Take any $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $ and consider the representation \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio}. 
We will work on the high-probability event $ {\mathcal F}_n $ from \zcref[S]{lem:rig_sub}. 
Let $M$ and $ I\subset[-2,2] $ of length $ \mleft|I\mright| = d^{-2} $ be defined as in \zcref[S]{eqn:M} and \zcref[S]{itm:pol1} of \zcref[S]{lem:q}. 
Then 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|q(x)\mright| &\ge M/2 \label{eqn:q_lb}
\end{align}
for all $x\in I$. 
Denote by $ {\mathcal I} \coloneqq \mleft\{ i\in[n] : \lambda_i(X) \in I \mright\} $ the indices of eigenvalues in $I$. 
By \zcref[S]{itm:rig_cnt} of \zcref[S]{lem:rig_sub}, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|{\mathcal I}\mright| &\ge cn/d^3 . \label{eqn:I}
\end{align}

On $ {\mathcal F}_n $, \zcref[S]{eqn:F_eig} holds, therefore by \zcref[S]{eqn:2M}, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|q(\lambda_1(X))\mright| &\le 2M . \label{eqn:q_ub}
\end{align}
Combining \zcref[S]{eqn:q_ub,eqn:q_lb}, we know that for every $ i\in{\mathcal I} $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|q(\lambda_i(X))\mright| &\ge M/2 \ge \mleft|q(\lambda_1(X))\mright| / 4 . \label{eqn:q_ulb}
\end{align}

Now, recalling \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio,eqn:g}, on $ {\mathcal F}_n $, using \zcref[S]{eqn:q_ulb}, we have
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),q(X) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(X) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{g_1^2 q(\lambda_1(X))^2}{\sum_{i = 1}^n g_i^2 q(\lambda_i(X))^2}
    \le \frac{g_1^2 q(\lambda_1(X))^2}{\sum_{i \in {\mathcal I}} g_i^2 q(\lambda_i(X))^2}
    \le \frac{g_1^2 q(\lambda_1(X))^2}{\sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2 (q(\lambda_1(X)) / 4)^2 }
    = \frac{16 \cdot g_1^2}{\sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2} . \label{eqn:ratio_sub} 
\end{align}
Conditioning on $ X $ and taking expectation only over $ (g_i)_{i\in[n]} $, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{g_1^2}{\sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, X \mright] &= \frac{1}{\mleft|{\mathcal I}\mright|} \sum_{j\in{\mathcal I}} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\frac{g_j^2}{\sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2}\mright] = \frac{1}{\mleft|{\mathcal I}\mright|} , \notag 
\end{align}
provided $ 1\in{\mathcal I} $. 
In the case where $ 1\notin{\mathcal I} $, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{g_1^2}{\sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, X \mright]
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[g_1^2\mright] \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\frac{1}{\sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2}\mright]
    = \frac{1}{\mleft|{\mathcal I}\mright| - 2} , \notag 
\end{align}
since $ \sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2 \sim \chi_{\mleft|{\mathcal I}\mright|}^2 $. 
By \zcref[S]{eqn:I} and the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:sub}, $ \mleft|{\mathcal I}\mright|\to\infty $. 
So in any case, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{g_1^2}{\sum_{i\in{\mathcal I}} g_i^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, X \mright]
    &\le \frac{C}{\mleft|{\mathcal I}\mright|} \le \frac{C' d^3}{n} . \notag 
\end{align}
Taking expectation on both sides of \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio_sub}, we have 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &\le C\frac{d^3}{n} + \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal F}_n^c\mright) , \notag 
\end{align}
uniformly for all $q$. 
Thus, using $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal F}_n\mright) \to 1 $ and \zcref[S]{eqn:sub}, we have $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) = o(1) $, which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:ratio}
Let $ (g_i)_{i\ge0} $ be i.i.d.\ standard Gaussians and let $ (r_i)_{i\ge1} $ be (deterministic) nonnegative reals. 
Set 
\begin{align}
&&
    Z &\coloneqq \sum_{i \ge 1} r_i g_i^2 , & 
    r &\coloneqq \sum_{i\ge1} r_i \in [0, \infty) . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
Then                                                                 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\frac{Z}{g_0^2 + Z}\mright] &\le 2 \cdot r^{1/3} . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Since $Z\ge0$, for any $ \eta > 0 $, it is easy to verify that
\begin{align}
    \frac{Z}{g_0^2+Z} &\le \mathds1{\mleft\{g_0^2 \le \eta\mright\}} + \frac{Z}{\eta} . \notag 
\end{align}
Taking expectations, we obtain 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{Z}{g_0^2+Z} \mright] &\le \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( g_0^2 \le \eta \mright) + \frac{r}{\eta} . \notag 
\end{align}
The probability on the RHS can be easily upper bounded: 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( g_0^2 \le \eta \mright) &= \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft|g_0\mright| \le \sqrt{\eta} \mright) = \int_{-\sqrt{\eta}}^{\sqrt{\eta}} \frac{\exp\mleft(-x^2/2\mright)}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \,\mathrm{d} x
    \le \frac{2\sqrt{\eta}}{\sqrt{2\pi}} = \sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\pi}} . \notag 
\end{align}
If $ r\le1 $, choosing $ \eta = r^{2/3} $, we obtain 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{Z}{g_0^2+Z} \mright] &\le \mleft( 1 + \sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}} \mright) r^{1/3} \le 2 \cdot r^{1/3} . \notag 
\end{align}
If $ r>1 $, then trivially, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{Z}{g_0^2+Z} \mright] &\le 1 \le r^{1/3} . \notag 
\end{align}
Combining the preceding two estimates concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We first gather a few facts regarding eigenvalues around the spectral edge of $X$. 
Denote, for $ i\in\{1,2\} $, $ L_i \coloneqq n^{2/3} (2 - \lambda_i(X)) $. 
By \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Ramirez_Rider_Virag}, 
\begin{align}
    (L_1, L_2) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Lambda_0, \Lambda_1) , \notag 
\end{align}
where the random variables $ \Lambda_0<\Lambda_1 $ on the RHS are the smallest and second smallest (simple) eigenvalues of the stochastic Airy operator. 
In particular, $ L_1,L_2 $ (as sequences implicitly indexed by $n$) are tight and 
\begin{align}
    L_2 - L_1 &= n^{2/3}(\lambda_1(X) - \lambda_2(X)) \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} \Delta \coloneqq \Lambda_1 - \Lambda_0 , \notag
\end{align}
where 
\begin{align}
    \Delta &> 0 , \qquad \textnormal{almost surely} . \label{eqn:Delta>0}
\end{align}
This implies 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(L_2 - L_1 < 1/\log(n)\mright) &\to 0 . \label{eqn:L2-L1}
\end{align}
To see this, note that by \zcref[S]{eqn:Delta>0}, for any fixed $\eta>0$, it is possible to choose $a>0$ such that $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(\Delta \le a\mright) < \eta $. 
Also, for all large $n$, $ 1/\log(n) < a $. 
Then 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(L_2 - L_1 < 1/\log(n)\mright) &\le \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(L_2 - L_1 \le a\mright) 
    \le \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(\Delta \le a\mright)
    < \eta , \notag 
\end{align}
where the second inequality is implied by convergence of $ L_2-L_1 $ in distribution, by Portmanteau theorem. 
Taking $ \eta\downarrow0 $ gives \zcref[S]{eqn:L2-L1}. 

By sign symmetry of Gaussians, $ -\lambda_n(X) \overset{\mathrm{d}}{=} \lambda_1(X) $. 
Since $ L_1 $ is tight, $ n^{2/3} (2 + \lambda_n(X)) $ is also tight. 
Therefore, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|X\mright\|_{2} &\overset{\mathrm{p}}{\to} 2 . \label{eqn:Xop}
\end{align}

Let $ \ell_n \coloneqq (\log(n))^2, u_n \coloneqq 2 - \ell_n n^{-2/3} $ and define the affine function $ f_n \colon [-3,u_n] \to [-1,1] $ as 
\begin{align}
    f_n(x) &= \frac{2}{u_n+3} (x + 3) - 1
    = \frac{2x+1+\ell_n n^{-2/3}}{5 - \ell_n n^{-2/3}} . \notag 
\end{align}
Consider $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $ defined as $ q(x) \coloneqq T_d(f_n(x)) $ where $ T_d $ is the degree-$d$ Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind. 
We record some facts about $q$ as straightforward consequences of extremal properties of $ T_d $. 
For $ x\in[-3,u_n] $, $ \mleft|q(x)\mright| \le 1 $. 
For $ x>u_n $, $ f_n(x)>1 $, so recalling from \zcref[S]{eqn:Td} the explicit expression of $ T_d $ on $ [1,\infty) $, we have 
\begin{align}
    q(x) &= \cosh(d \, \theta_n(s)) , \notag                        
\end{align}
where 
\begin{align}
&&
    s &\coloneqq n^{2/3} (x - u_n) , & 
    \theta_n(s) &\coloneqq \operatorname{arccosh}\mleft(1 + \frac{2n^{-2/3} s}{u_n + 3}\mright) . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
In particular $ q $ is positive and increasing on $ (u_n,\infty) $. 
A few estimates for $ \theta_n $ are in order. 
A straightforward calculation reveals
\begin{align}
    \theta_n'(s) &= \frac{n^{-1/3}}{\sqrt{s(u_n + 3 + n^{-2/3} s)}} . \notag 
\end{align}
Since $ u_n + 3 = 5 - \ell_n n^{-2/3} \to 5 $, there exist absolute constants $ c,C>0 $ such that for all sufficiently large $n$, 
\begin{align}
    \theta_n'(s) &\ge c n^{-1/3} \ell_n^{-1/2} , \qquad \textnormal{for all } s\in[\ell_n/2,3\ell_n/2] , \label{eqn:theta'} 
\end{align}
and 
\begin{align}
    c \, n^{-1/3} \sqrt{s} &\le \theta_n(s) \le C n^{-1/3} \sqrt{s} , \qquad \textnormal{for all } s\in[0,2\ell_n] , \label{eqn:theta} 
\end{align}
by noting that $ s\le2\ell_n $ implies $ s n^{-2/3} = o(1) $ and using the previous estimate in the fundamental theorem of calculus. 

Define the event 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal G}_n &\coloneqq \mleft\{ \mleft\|X\mright\|_{2} \le 3 , \; \mleft|L_1\mright| \le \ell_n/2 , \; \mleft|L_2\mright| \le \ell_n/2 , \; L_2 - L_1 \ge 1/\log(n) \mright\} . \notag 
\end{align}
By tightness of $ L_1,L_2 $, \zcref[S]{eqn:Xop,eqn:L2-L1}, $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal G}_n\mright) \to 1 $. 

Let $ s_j \coloneqq n^{2/3} (\lambda_j(X) - u_n) = \ell_n - L_j $, for $j\in\{1,2\}$. 
Then on $ {\mathcal G}_n $, we have 
\begin{align}
&&
    s_1, s_2 &\in [\ell_n/2, 3\ell_n/2] , & 
    s_1 - s_2 &= L_2 - L_1 \ge 1/\log(n) . & 
& \label{eqn:s1s2} 
\end{align}
Recalling the definition of $ L_1 $, we also have $ n^{2/3} \mleft|2 - \lambda_1(X)\mright| \le \ell_n/2 $, implying $ \lambda_1(X) \ge 2 - \ell_n/(2 n^{2/3}) > u_n $. 
Therefore, the formula \zcref[S]{eqn:theta} is applicable at $ \lambda_1(X) $, leading to 
\begin{align}
    q(\lambda_1(X)) &= \cosh(d\,\theta_n(s_1))
    \ge \frac{1}{2} \exp\mleft( d\,\theta_n(s_1) \mright)
    \ge \frac{1}{2} \exp\mleft( c \, d \, n^{-1/3} \sqrt{s_1} \mright)
    \ge \frac{1}{2} \exp\mleft( c' \, d \, n^{-1/3} \sqrt{\ell_n} \mright) , \label{eqn:q1} 
\end{align}
where the last two inequalities are by \zcref[S]{eqn:theta,eqn:s1s2}. 

Again using \zcref[S]{eqn:theta'} in the fundamental theorem of calculus, 
\begin{align}
    \theta_n(s_1) - \theta_n(s_2) &\ge c n^{-1/3} \ell_n^{-1/2} (s_1 - s_2)
    \ge c n^{-1/3} \ell_n^{-1/2} (\log(n))^{-1} , \label{eqn:s1-s2} 
\end{align}
where we use \zcref[S]{eqn:s1s2} in the last step. 
Now we combine \zcref[S]{eqn:q1,eqn:s1-s2} to obtain 
\begin{align}
    \frac{q(\lambda_2(X))}{q(\lambda_1(X))}
    &= \frac{\cosh(d\,\theta_n(s_2))}{\cosh(d\,\theta_n(s_1))}
    \le 2 \exp\mleft( - d\mleft( \theta_n(s_1) - \theta_n(s_2) \mright) \mright)
    \le 2 \exp\mleft( - c d n^{-1/3} \ell_n^{-1/2} (\log(n))^{-1} \mright) . \label{eqn:q2} 
\end{align}

Let 
\begin{align}
    r &\coloneqq \sum_{i = 2}^n \mleft(\frac{q(\lambda_i(X))}{q(\lambda_1(X))}\mright)^2 . \notag 
\end{align}
Since $ q $ is increasing on $ (u_n,\infty) $ and has modulus bounded by $1$ on $ [-3,u_n] $, on $ {\mathcal G}_n $, we have 
\begin{align}
    r &\le (n-1) \cdot \frac{1 + q(\lambda_2(X))^2}{q(\lambda_1(X))^2} . \notag  
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:q1,eqn:q2}, the RHS is further upper bounded by 
\begin{align}
    & 4n \exp\mleft(-2c'dn^{-1/3}\sqrt{\ell_n}\mright)
    + 4n \exp\mleft(-2cdn^{-1/3}\ell_n^{-1/2} (\log(n))^{-1}\mright) \notag \\
    &= 4n \exp\mleft(-2c'dn^{-1/3} \log(n)\mright)
    + 4n \exp\mleft(-2cdn^{-1/3}(\log(n))^{-2}\mright) \notag \\
    &= 4n \exp\mleft(- c'_1 n^{\varepsilon}\log(n)\mright) 
    + 4n \exp\mleft(- c_1 n^{\varepsilon} (\log(n))^{-2}\mright)
    = o(1) , \label{eqn:r} 
\end{align}
under the scaling \zcref[S]{eqn:sup}. 

To conclude, recalling \zcref[S]{eqn:g} and using the representation \zcref[S]{eqn:ratio}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),q(X)b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(X) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{g_1^2 q(\lambda_1(X))^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n g_i^2 q(\lambda_i(X))^2}
    = \frac{g_1^2}{g_1^2 + \sum_{i = 2}^n r_i g_i^2} , \notag 
\end{align}
where for all $ 2\le i\le n $, 
\begin{align}
    r_i &\coloneqq \mleft(\frac{q(\lambda_i(X))}{q(\lambda_1(X))}\mright)^2 . \notag 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:r} and \zcref[S]{lem:ratio}, conditioned on $ X $, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ 1 - \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),q(X)b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(X) b\mright\|_{2}^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, X \mright]
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\sum_{i = 2}^n r_i g_i^2}{g_1^2 + \sum_{i = 2}^n r_i g_i^2} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, X \mright]
    \le 2 \cdot r^{1/3} = o(1) . \notag 
\end{align}
Further averaging over $ X $, by $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal G}_n\mright) \to 1 $, we obtain $ {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\operatorname{GOE}(n)) \to 1 $ and hence $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) \to 1 $. 
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:suppress}
For a probability measure ${\mathcal D}$ over $n\times n$ real symmetric matrices, it holds that 
\begin{align}
    \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d-1}({\mathcal D}) &\le \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}^0({\mathcal D}) + \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal H}^c\mright) , \label{eqn:OPT+1} 
\end{align}
where $ {\mathcal H} \coloneqq \mleft\{ \lambda_1(X) \ge 1 , \mleft\|X\mright\|_{2} \le 3 \mright\} $ and the probability is over $ X \sim {\mathcal D} $.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Take any $ p\in{\mathcal P}_{d-1} $ and set $ q(x) = p(x) m(x) $ where $ m(x) \coloneqq (x+2)/4 $. 
Then $ q(-2) = 0 $ and $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $. 
By spectral decomposition of $X$, we write 
\begin{align}
&&
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),p(X) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|p(X) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{\alpha_1^2}{\sum_{i = 1}^n \alpha_i^2} , & 
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),q(X) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(X) b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{m(\lambda_1(X))^2 \alpha_1^2}{\sum_{i = 1}^n m(\lambda_i(X))^2 \alpha_i^2} , & 
& \label{eqn:m} 
\end{align}
where $ \alpha_i \coloneqq p(\lambda_i(X)) \mleft\langlev_i(X),b\mright\rangle $. 
On $ {\mathcal H} $, it holds that $ -3 \le \lambda_i(X) \le \lambda_1(X) $ for all $ i\in[n] $, and $ \lambda_1(X) \ge 1 $. 
Therefore, $ -\lambda_1(X) \le \lambda_i(X) + 2 \le \lambda_1(X)+2 $, implying $ \mleft|m(\lambda_i(X))\mright| \le m(\lambda_1(X)) $. 
Using this in \zcref[S]{eqn:m}, we have that on ${\mathcal H}$, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),p(X) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|p(X) b\mright\|_{2}^2} &\le \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),q(X) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(X) b\mright\|_{2}^2} . \notag 
\end{align}
Since both quantities are within $ [0,1] $, taking expectations on both sides and applying law of total expectation yield \zcref[S]{eqn:OPT+1}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:mass}
Assume \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}.
Let $ u_n \colon [0,1]\to{\mathbb R} $ be a step function on the mesh $ (k/d)_{k=0}^d $ written as 
\begin{align}
&&
    u_n(t) &= \sqrt{d} \, c_k , &
    & \textnormal{for } t\in[k/d,(k+1)/d)
    \textnormal{ and } 0\le k\le d-1 , & 
& \label{eqn:u} 
\end{align}
such that 
\begin{align}
                                    
    \sup_d \sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{d-1} c_k^2} &< \infty . \label{eqn:u_bdd} 
\end{align}
Define 
\begin{align}
&&
    p(x) &= \sum_{k=0}^{d-1} c_k p_k(x) , & 
    q(x) &= m(x) p(x) , & 
& \label{eqn:pq} 
\end{align}
where $ p_k $ is the rescaled Chebyshev polynomial in \zcref[S]{eqn:p} and $ m(x) = (x+2)/4 $. 
Let 
\begin{align}
&&
    S &\coloneqq \frac{1}{n} \operatorname{Tr}\mleft( q(X)^2 \mright) , & 
    L_j &\coloneqq n^{2/3} (2 - \lambda_j(X)) , & 
    \sigma &\coloneqq \int q(x)^2 \, \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}(\mathrm{d} x) . & 
& \label{eqn:ZLsigma} 
\end{align}
Then for any $m$ fixed (relative to $n$), there exist a function $ u\in L^2([0,1]) $ and a deterministic scalar $ \overline{\sigma} \ge 0 $ such that the sequence (indexed by $n$) of tuples $(u_n,\sigma,S,L_1,\cdots,L_m)$ admits a subsequence satisfying: 
\begin{align}
    u_n &\rightharpoonup u \qquad \textnormal{weakly in } L^2([0,1]) , \label{eqn:ulim} \\
    \sigma &\to \overline{\sigma} , \label{eqn:sigmalim} \\
    (S, L_1, \cdots, L_m) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Sigma, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) , \label{eqn:Zlim}
\end{align}
where $ -\Lambda_0 \ge -\Lambda_1 \ge \cdots $ form the Airy point process, and 
\begin{align}
    \Sigma &\coloneqq \overline{\sigma} - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 + \sum_{j = 0}^\infty \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_j)^2 . \notag 
\end{align}
In particular, $ \Sigma\ge0 $ almost surely.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The proof is divided into several steps. 

\paragraph{Existence of $ u $ and $ \overline{\sigma} $.}
We first show that the limits of $u_n$ and $\sigma$ exist. 
Since
\begin{align}
    \mleft\|u_n\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} &=\sqrt{\int_0^1 u_n(t)^2 \,\mathrm{d} t} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{d} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (\sqrt{d}\,c_k)^2} = \sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2} \label{eqn:L2} 
\end{align}
is assumed to be bounded uniformly in $n$ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:u_bdd}), there exists a subsequence of $u_n$ (indexed by $n$) that weakly converges in $ L^2([0,1]) $. 
Denote this weak limit by $ u $. 
This proves \zcref[S]{eqn:ulim}. 

Next, we show that $ \sigma $ is uniformly bounded. 
For any $x\in[-2,2]$, let us make the change of variable $ x = 2 \cos(\theta) $ for some $ \theta \in [0,\pi] $. 
Then by \zcref[S]{eqn:U_small}, $ p_k(x) = U_k(\cos(\theta)) = \sin( (k+1)\theta ) / \sin(\theta) $. 
Using this, we have 
\begin{align}
    q(2\cos(\theta)) &= \frac{1 + \cos(\theta)}{2} \cdot \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \frac{\sin( (k+1)\theta )}{\sin(\theta)}
    = \frac{\cos(\theta/2)^2}{\sin(\theta)} \cdot \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \sin( (k+1)\theta ) . \label{eqn:q_theta} 
\end{align}
Moreover, 
\begin{align}
    \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x
    &= - 2\sin(\theta) \cdot \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(2 \cos(\theta)) \,\mathrm{d}\theta
    = -2 \sin(\theta) \cdot \frac{\sqrt{1-\cos(\theta)^2}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} \theta
    = - \frac{2}{\pi} \sin(\theta)^2 \,\mathrm{d}\theta . \notag 
\end{align}
Since the change of variable $ x = 2 \cos(\theta) $ maps $ \theta\in[0,\pi] $ decreasingly to $ x\in[-2,2] $, 
\begin{align}
    \sigma &= \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^{\pi} \cos(\theta/2)^4 \mleft( \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \sin( (k+1)\theta ) \mright)^2 \,\mathrm{d}\theta . \notag 
\end{align}
Since $ \cos(\theta/2)^4\in[0,1] $,  
\begin{align}
    0 &\le \sigma \le \sum_{k,\ell=0}^{d-1} c_k c_\ell \int_0^\pi \frac{2}{\pi} \sin( (k+1)\theta ) \sin( (\ell+1)\theta ) \,\mathrm{d}\theta
    = \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2 , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last equality is by the sine orthogonality relation: 
\begin{align}
    \mathds1{\mleft\{m = n\mright\}}
                                                                                                      
                                                                              
    &= \frac{2}{\pi} \int_{0}^\pi \sin(m\theta) \sin(n\theta) \,\mathrm{d} \theta , \notag 
\end{align}
for             
 $ m,n\in{\mathbb Z}_{>0} $. 
Now by \zcref[S]{eqn:L2,eqn:u_bdd}, $ \sigma $ is bounded uniformly in $n$. 
This allows us to pass to a convergent subsequence whose limit $ \overline{\sigma}\ge0 $ exists. 
This proves \zcref[S]{eqn:sigmalim}. 

\paragraph{Uniform convergence of $q$ near the edge.}
                         
               
                                                               
             
We will prove that for any compact interval $ I \subset {\mathbb R} $, 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{s\in I} \mleft| n^{-1/2} q(2 + s n^{-2/3}) - \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s) \mright| &\to 0 . \label{eqn:q_Gamma}
\end{align}

To this end, we first consider the case $ s\le0 $. 
Specifically, fix $ L>0 $ and assume $ s\in[-L,0] $. 
Let $ \phi_n(s) \in [0,\pi] $ be defined by $ 2 + sn^{-2/3} = 2 \cos(\phi_n(s)) $. 
Set $ \alpha_n(s) \coloneqq d \phi_n(s) $ and $ \beta_n(s) \coloneqq d \sin(\phi_n(s)) $. 
By the Taylor expansion $ 2\cos(\phi) = 2 - \phi^2 + O(\phi^4) $ at $ \phi=0 $, we have $ \phi_n(s) = \sqrt{-s}\,n^{-1/3}(1+o(1)) $ (as $n\to\infty$). 
Combining this with $ \sin(\phi) = \phi+O(\phi^3) $ and the scaling assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}, it is easy to verify that 
\begin{align}
&&
    \alpha_n(s) &\to \delta \sqrt{-s} , & 
    \beta_n(s) &\to \delta \sqrt{-s} , & 
    \frac{\alpha_n(s)}{\beta_n(s)} &\to 1 , & 
& \label{eqn:alpha_beta} 
\end{align}
uniformly for all $ s\in[-L,0] $. 

For $ t\in[0,1] $, define
\begin{align}
&&
    \psi_{n,s}(t) &\coloneqq \begin{cases}
        \frac{\sin(\alpha_n(s) t)}{\beta_n(s)} , & s < 0 \\
        t , & s = 0
    \end{cases} , & 
    \psi_s(t) &\coloneqq \begin{cases}
        \frac{\sin(\delta \sqrt{-s} \, t)}{\delta\sqrt{-s}} , & s<0 \\
        t , & s = 0
    \end{cases} . & 
& \label{eqn:psi} 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:alpha_beta}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft\| \psi_{n,s} - \psi_s \mright\|_{L^\infty([0,1])} &= 0 . \label{eqn:bdd_diff} 
\end{align}
Moreover, since
\begin{align}
    \psi_{n,s}'(t) &= \begin{cases}
        \frac{\alpha_n(s)}{\beta_n(s)} \cdot \cos(\alpha_n(s) t) , & s<0 \\
        1 , & s=0
    \end{cases} \notag 
\end{align}
is uniformly bounded in $n$, we also have 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{n} \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \sup_{t\in[0,1]} \mleft| \psi_{n,s}'(t) \mright| &< \infty . \label{eqn:bdd'} 
\end{align}

Now using $ \phi = \phi_n(s) $ in the expression \zcref[S]{eqn:q_theta} of $q$, we have 
\begin{align}
    n^{-1/2} q(2 + sn^{-2/3})
    &= n^{-1/2} q(2\cos(\phi_n(s)))
    = n^{-1/2} \frac{1 + \cos(\phi_n(s))}{2} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \frac{\sin( (k+1) \phi_n(s) )}{\sin(\phi_n(s))} \notag \\
    &= n^{-1/2} d^{3/2} \cdot \frac{4 + s n^{-2/3}}{4} \cdot \frac{1}{d} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (\sqrt{d}\,c_k) \frac{\sin\mleft( d\phi_n(s) \cdot \frac{k+1}{d} \mright)}{d\sin(\phi_n(s))} \notag \\
    &= n^{-1/2} d^{3/2} \cdot \frac{4 + s n^{-2/3}}{4} \cdot \frac{1}{d} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (\sqrt{d}\,c_k) \psi_{n,s}\mleft( \frac{k+1}{d} \mright) . \label{eqn:q_TODO} 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}, the first factor converges to $ \delta^{3/2} $. 
The second factor converges to $1$. 
To prove \zcref[S]{eqn:q_Gamma} for $I=[-L,0]$, it remains to show that the third factor converges to $ \delta^{-3/2} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s) $ uniformly in $s\in[-L,0]$. 
To this end, let us estimate the difference between the empirical average and its integral counterpart. 
Since $u_n$ is a step function on the mesh $ (k/d)_{k=0}^d $ with heights $ (\sqrt{d}\,c_k)_{k=0}^{d-1} $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \frac{1}{d} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (\sqrt{d}\,c_k) \psi_{n,s}\mleft( \frac{k+1}{d} \mright) - \int_0^1 u_n(t) \psi_{n,s}(t) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright|
    &\le \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} \sqrt{d}\,\mleft|c_k\mright| \int_{k/d}^{(k+1)/d} \mleft| \psi_{n,s}\mleft(\frac{k+1}{d}\mright) - \psi_{n,s}(t) \mright| \,\mathrm{d} t . \notag 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:bdd'} and the fundamental theorem of calculus, there exists $ C_L>0 $ such that uniformly for all $n$, the RHS above is upper bounded by 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{k=0}^{d-1} \sqrt{d}\,\mleft|c_k\mright| \int_{k/d}^{(k+1)/d} \int_t^{(k+1)/d} \mleft| \psi_{n,s}'(\tau) \mright| \,\mathrm{d} \tau \,\mathrm{d} t
    &\le \sum_{k=0}^{d-1} \sqrt{d}\,\mleft|c_k\mright| \frac{C_L}{d^2} 
    \le \frac{C_L}{d} \sqrt{\frac{1}{d} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (\sqrt{d}\,c_k)^2}
    = \frac{C_L}{d} \mleft\|u_n\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last two steps are by Cauchy--Schwarz and \zcref[S]{eqn:L2}, respectively.
By \zcref[S]{eqn:u_bdd}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft| \frac{1}{d} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (\sqrt{d}\,c_k) \psi_{n,s}\mleft( \frac{k+1}{d} \mright) - \int_0^1 u_n(t) \psi_{n,s}(t) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright| &\to 0 . \label{eqn:avg_int} 
\end{align}

Next, we estimate the difference between the integral in \zcref[S]{eqn:avg_int} and its limiting counterpart. 
By triangle inequality for $ \mleft|\cdot\mright| $ and H\"older\xspace's inequality for $ \mleft\langle\cdot,\cdot\mright\rangle_{L^2([0,1])} $, 
\begin{align}
    & \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft| \int_0^1 u_n(t) \psi_{n,s}(t) \,\mathrm{d} t - \int_0^1 u(t) \psi_s(t) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright| \notag \\
    &\le \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft|\mleft\langleu_n,\psi_{n,s} - \psi_s\mright\rangle_{L^2([0,1])}\mright| + \mleft|\mleft\langleu_n - u,\psi_s\mright\rangle_{L^2([0,1])}\mright| \notag \\
    &\le \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft\|u_n\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} \mleft\|\psi_{n,s} - \psi_s\mright\|_{L^\infty([0,1])} + \mleft|\mleft\langleu_n - u,\psi_s\mright\rangle_{L^2([0,1])}\mright| . \notag 
\end{align}
In the Hilbert space $ L^2([0,1]) $, the collection of functions $ K = ( \psi_s )_{s\in[-L,0]} $ is compact. 
Applying \zcref[S]{prop:weak} to this compact subset $ K \subset L^2([0,1]) $ and the weakly convergent subsequence $ u_n $, we have
\begin{align}
     \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft|\mleft\langleu_n - u,\psi_s\mright\rangle_{L^2([0,1])}\mright| &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align} 
Since $ \mleft\|u_n\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} $ is uniformly bounded in $n$ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:u_bdd,eqn:L2}) and $ \mleft\|\psi_{n,s} - \psi_s\mright\|_{L^\infty([0,1])} $ converges to $0$ uniformly in $s\in[-L,0]$ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:bdd_diff}), we conclude 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft| \int_0^1 u_n(t) \psi_{n,s}(t) \,\mathrm{d} t - \int_0^1 u(t) \psi_s(t) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright| &= 0 . \label{eqn:int_int} 
\end{align} 

Comparing the definition of $ \psi_s $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:psi} with that of $ \Gamma_{\delta,u} $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:Gamma}, 
\begin{align}
    \int_0^1 u(t) \psi_s(t) \,\mathrm{d} t &= \delta^{-3/2} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s) , 
    \qquad \textnormal{for } s\le0 . \label{eqn:int_Gamma} 
\end{align}
So using \zcref[S]{eqn:avg_int,eqn:int_int,eqn:int_Gamma} in \zcref[S]{eqn:q_TODO}, we have
\begin{align}
    \sup_{s\in[-L,0]} \mleft| n^{-1/2} q(2 + sn^{-2/3}) - \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s) \mright| &\to 0 . \label{eqn:neg}
\end{align}
This proves \zcref[S]{eqn:q_Gamma} for $ s\in I\cap{\mathbb R}_{\le0} $. 

For the positive part where $ s\in[0,L] $, the proof is almost identical and we only highlight necessary modifications. 
Let $ \widetilde{\phi}_n(s) \ge 0 $ be defined by $ 2 + sn^{-2/3} = 2 \cosh(\widetilde{\phi}_n(s)) $. 
Set $ \widetilde{\alpha}_n(s) \coloneqq d \widetilde{\phi}_n(s) $ and $ \widetilde{\beta}_n(s) \coloneqq d\sinh(\widetilde{\phi}_n(s)) $. 
Then using Taylor expansions $ 2 \cosh(\widetilde{\phi}) = 2 + \widetilde{\phi}^2 + O(\widetilde{\phi}^4) $ and $ \sinh(\widetilde{\phi}) = \widetilde{\phi} + O(\widetilde{\phi}^3) $ at $ \widetilde{\phi} = 0 $, uniformly for all $s\in[0,L]$, we have
\begin{align}
&&
    \widetilde{\alpha}_n(s) &\to \delta \sqrt{s} , & 
    \widetilde{\beta}_n(s) &\to \delta \sqrt{s} , & 
    \frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_n(s)}{\widetilde{\beta}_n(s)} &\to 1 . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{eqn:U_big}, we have the following expression for $ \phi\ge0 $ in analogy to \zcref[S]{eqn:q_theta} for $\theta\in[0,\pi]$, 
\begin{align}
    q(2\cosh(\phi)) &= \frac{1+\cosh(\phi)}{2} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \frac{\sinh( (k+1)\phi )}{\sinh(\phi)} . \notag 
\end{align}
Using this with $ \phi = \widetilde{\phi}_n(s) $ and following derivations similar to the case of $ s\le0 $, we obtain
\begin{align}
    \sup_{s\in[0,L]} \mleft|n^{-1/2} q(2 + sn^{-2/3}) - \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s)\mright| &\to 0 . \label{eqn:pos}
\end{align}
Since any compact interval $I$ is contained in $ [-L,L] $ for some $ L\in(0,\infty) $, combining \zcref[S]{eqn:pos,eqn:neg} establishes \zcref[S]{eqn:q_Gamma}. 

\paragraph{An identity.}
We claim that 
\begin{align}
    \int_{-\infty}^0 \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s)^2 \frac{\sqrt{-s}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} s &= \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 . \label{eqn:identity} 
\end{align}
Indeed, by definition \zcref[S]{eqn:Gamma}, the LHS above equals
\begin{align}
                                                                            
    & \delta \int_{-\infty}^0 \mleft( \int_0^1 u(t) \frac{\sin(\delta\sqrt{-s}\,t)}{\sqrt{-s}} \,\mathrm{d} t \mright)^2 \frac{\sqrt{-s}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} s . \notag
\end{align}
Making the change of variable $ z \coloneqq \delta \sqrt{-s} $, we have $ s = -\delta^{-2}z^2 , \,\mathrm{d} s = -2 \delta^{-2} z \,\mathrm{d} z , \sqrt{-s} \,\mathrm{d} s = -2 \delta^{-3} z^2 \,\mathrm{d} z $. 
Consequently, the integral becomes
\begin{align}
    \delta \int_{\infty}^0 \mleft( \int_0^1 u(t) \frac{\sin(zt)}{z/\delta} \,\mathrm{d} t \mright)^2 \frac{-2\delta^{-3} z^2}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} z
    &= \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\infty \mleft( \int_0^1 u(t) \sin(zt) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright)^2  \,\mathrm{d} z . \notag 
\end{align}
This is equal to $ \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 $ by the Plancherel identity for sine transforms. 

\paragraph{Counting statistics near the edge.}
Recall that $ X\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n) $. 
For $ E \in {\mathbb R} $, define the GOE eigenvalue counting statistic as $ N_X(E) \coloneqq \mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : \lambda_i(X) \ge E \mright\}\mright| $. 
In preparation for the remainder of the proof, we collect a few facts regarding counting statistics near the right edge. 
By \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Ramirez_Rider_Virag}, $ n^{2/3}(2 - \lambda_1(X)) $ is tight. 
By sign symmetry of Gaussians, $ n^{2/3} (2 + \lambda_n(X)) $ is also tight, implying 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{M\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \lambda_n(X) < -2 - Mn^{-2/3} \mright)
    &\le \lim_{M\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft| n^{2/3}(2 + \lambda_n(X)) \mright| > M \mright)
    = 0 . \label{eqn:tight} 
\end{align}
Let $ F_X(z) \coloneqq n^{-1} \mleft|\mleft\{i\in[n] : \lambda_i(X) \le z\mright\}\mright| $ be the c.d.f.\ of the empirical spectral distribution of $X$. 
By \cite[Theorem 1]{Kholopov_Tikhomirov_Timushev}, 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{x\in{\mathbb R}} \mleft| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[F_X(x)\mright] - \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}( (-\infty,x] ) \mright| &\le C/n , \label{eqn:kolmogorov}
\end{align}
for an absolute constant $ C>0 $. 
Since the joint law of GOE eigenvalues has an absolutely continuous density with respect to the Lebesgue measure, for any fixed $E$ and $j\in[n]$, $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(\lambda_j(X) = E\mright) = 0 $, hence $ N_X(E) = n (1 - F_X(E)) $ almost surely. 
Combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:kolmogorov}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[N_X(E)\mright] - n \mu_{\textnormal{sc}}( (E,\infty) ) \mright| &\le C , \label{eqn:NX} 
\end{align}
uniformly for all $ E\in[-2,2] $. 

We also need the following estimate for the variance of $ N_X $: 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_X(E) \mright] &\le C \log(2 + n(2 - E)^{3/2}) , \label{eqn:VarX} 
\end{align}
uniformly for all $ E\in[-2,2] $. 
Such a result for GUE counting statistics is a straightforward consequence of \cite{Gustavsson}. 
We transfer it to GOE using the Forrester--Rains relation \cite{Forrester_Rains}. 
Specifically, let $ G\sim \operatorname{GUE}(n) $,\footnote{A matrix $ G $ from $ \operatorname{GUE}(n) $ is an $n\times n$ complex Hermitian matrix with independent upper triangular elements distributed according to $ G_{i,i} \sim {\mathcal N}(0,1/n) $ for $i\in[n]$ and $ G_{i,j} \sim {\mathcal N}(0,(2n)^{-1}) + \mathrm{i} {\mathcal N}(0,(2n)^{-1}) $ for $ 1\le i<j\le n $.} then \cite[Lemma 2.3]{Gustavsson} asserts that for $ E\ge0 $, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_G(E) \mright] &= \frac{1+o(1)}{2\pi^2} \log( n(1 - E/2)^{3/2} ) , \label{eqn:VarNG} 
\end{align}
where the asymptotic in $ o(1) $ is with respect to $n\to\infty$. 
We claim that this implies 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_G(E) \mright] &\le C \log(2 + n(2 - E)^{3/2}) , \label{eqn:VarG} 
\end{align}
uniformly for all $ E\in[-2,2] $. 
To see this, first consider $E\ge0$ in which case \zcref[S]{eqn:VarNG} applies. 
If $ n(1 - E/2)^{3/2} $ is bounded in $n$, then so is $ \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_G(E) \mright] $, in particular, \zcref[S]{eqn:VarG} holds. 
Otherwise, $ n(1 - E/2)^{3/2} \to \infty $ as $n\to\infty$, and \zcref[S]{eqn:VarG} also holds. 
Next consider $ E<0 $. By sign symmetry of GUE, $ N_G(E) \overset{\mathrm{d}}{=} n - N_G(-E) $ to the RHS of which \zcref[S]{eqn:VarNG} applies. 
Consequently, $ \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_G(E) \mright] = \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_G(-E) \mright] \le C \log(2 + n(2+E)^{3/2}) \le C \log(2 + n(2 - E)^{3/2}) $, i.e., \zcref[S]{eqn:VarG} still holds. 
So \zcref[S]{eqn:VarG} is valid in any case, as claimed. 

To turn \zcref[S]{eqn:VarG} into \zcref[S]{eqn:VarX}, we use the following result relating the spectrum of GUE to that of GOE.
Recall $ G\sim\operatorname{GUE}(n) $. 
Let $ (X/\sqrt{n},X'/\sqrt{n+1}) \sim \operatorname{GOE}(n) \otimes \operatorname{GOE}(n+1) $. 
Let $ s_1 > \cdots > s_{2n+1} $ be obtained by taking the union of $ (\lambda_i(X))_{i = 1}^n $ and $ (\lambda_i(X'))_{i=1}^{n+1} $ and sorting them in descending order (note that with probability $1$, these numbers are all distinct). 
Then \cite[Theorem 5.2]{Forrester_Rains} asserts that $ (\sqrt{n} \, \lambda_i(G))_{i=1}^n $ is identically distributed as $ (s_{2i})_{i = 1}^n $. 
Now for any $E\in{\mathbb R}$, it is not hard to verify that $ \mleft| N_X(E) + N_{X'}(E) - 2 N_{\sqrt{n}\,G}(E) \mright| \le 1 $. 
This implies $ \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_X(E) + N_{X'}(E) \mright] \le C \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_{\sqrt{n}\,G}(E) \mright] + C $. 
On the other hand, since $ N_X(E) $ and $ N_{X'}(E) $ are independent, $ \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_X(E) + N_{X'}(E) \mright] = \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_X(E) \mright] + \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_{X'}(E) \mright] \ge \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_X(E) \mright] $. 
Combining the preceding two estimates, we have $ \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_X(E) \mright] \le C \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_{\sqrt{n}\,G}(E) \mright] + C $. 
This allows us to conclude \zcref[S]{eqn:VarX} from \zcref[S]{eqn:VarG}. 


\paragraph{Controlling the residue.}
Analogous to the counting statistic $ N_X $, let us also define the rescaled counting statistic: 
\begin{align}
    \widetilde{N}_X(s) &\coloneqq \mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : \lambda_i(X) \ge 2 + s n^{-2/3} \mright\}\mright| = \mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : -L_i \ge s \mright\}\mright| , \notag 
\end{align}
for $ s\in{\mathbb R} $, where the second equality is by the definition of $ L_i $ from \zcref[S]{eqn:ZLsigma}. 
Define the rescaled semicircle density as 
\begin{align}
    \widetilde{\rho}(s) &\coloneqq n^{1/3} \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(2 + s n^{-2/3}) \mathds1_{[-4n^{2/3}, 0]}(s) . \label{eqn:wtrho} 
\end{align}
Note that $ \int \widetilde{\rho} = n $. 
For $ \ell\ge1 $, let 
\begin{align}
&&
    g(s) &\coloneqq n^{-1} q(2 + sn^{-2/3})^2 , & 
    W_\ell &\coloneqq S - \sigma - \sum_{j=1}^n g(-L_j) \mathds1{\mleft\{L_j \le \ell\mright\}} + \int_{-\ell}^0 g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s . & 
& \label{eqn:g_W} 
\end{align}
We shall prove that for all $ \varepsilon>0 $, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{\ell\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft|W_\ell\mright| > \varepsilon \mright) &= 0 . \label{eqn:Well} 
\end{align}

To this end, let us first derive some point-wise estimates of $ \mleft|g(s)\mright| $ and $ \mleft|g'(s)\mright| $ for $ s\in[-4n^{2/3}, -1] $. 
For $ s\in[-4n^{2/3}, 0] $, define $ \theta \in [0,\pi] $ through $ 2 + sn^{-2/3} = 2\cos(\theta) $ so that 
\begin{align}
    s \equiv s(\theta) = - 4 n^{2/3} \sin(\theta/2)^2 . \label{eqn:s_theta}
\end{align}
Then by \zcref[S]{eqn:q_theta}, 
\begin{align}
    q(2 \cos(\theta)) &= \frac{1+\cos(\theta)}{2 \sin(\theta)} r(\theta)
    = \frac{\cot(\theta/2)}{2} r(\theta) , \notag 
\end{align}
where we denote 
\begin{align}
    r(\theta) &\coloneqq \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \sin( (k+1)\theta ) . \notag 
\end{align}
Hence
\begin{align}
    g(s(\theta)) &= \frac{\cot(\theta/2)^2}{4n} r(\theta)^2 . \label{eqn:gs} 
\end{align}
By Cauchy--Schwarz, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|r(\theta)\mright| &\le \sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2} \sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} \sin( (k+1)\theta )^2} 
    \le C\sqrt{d} , \label{eqn:|r|}
\end{align}
where the last inequality uses \zcref[S]{eqn:u_bdd}. 
Similarly, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|r'(\theta)\mright| &= \mleft| \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \cos( (k+1)\theta ) (k+1) \mright|
    \le \sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2} \sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (k+1)^2}
    \le C d^{3/2} . \label{eqn:|r'|}
\end{align}
Using \zcref[S]{eqn:|r|,eqn:s_theta} in \zcref[S]{eqn:gs}, we have that uniformly for all $ \theta $ such that $ s(\theta) \in [-4n^{2/3}, -1] $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|g(s(\theta))\mright| &= \frac{\cos(\theta/2)^2 r(\theta)^2}{4n \sin(\theta/2)^2}
    \le \frac{r(\theta)^2}{n^{1/3} \mleft|s(\theta)\mright|}
    \le C \frac{d/n^{1/3}}{\mleft|s(\theta)\mright|}
    \le \frac{C'}{\mleft|s(\theta)\mright|} , \label{eqn:|g|} 
\end{align}
where the last inequality holds for all sufficiently large $n$ by the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 

Moving to $ \mleft|g'\mright| $, we use \zcref[S]{eqn:gs,eqn:s_theta} to compute 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mathrm{d} g(s(\theta))}{\mathrm{d} \theta} &= \frac{1}{2n} \cot(\theta/2) r(\theta) \mleft( \cot(\theta/2) r'(\theta) - \frac{1}{2} \csc(\theta/2)^2 r(\theta) \mright) , \notag \\
    \frac{\mathrm{d} s(\theta)}{\mathrm{d} \theta} &= -4n^{2/3} \sin(\theta/2) \cos(\theta/2)                           
    . \notag 
\end{align}
Now applying the chain rule for derivatives, using \zcref[S]{eqn:|r|,eqn:|r'|}, we obtain that uniformly for all $ \theta $ with $ s(\theta)\in[-4n^{2/3},-1] $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|g'(s(\theta))\mright| &\le \mleft|\frac{\mathrm{d} g(s(\theta))}{\mathrm{d} \theta} \cdot \frac{1}{s'(\theta)}\mright|
    \le \frac{1}{2n\mleft|s'(\theta)\mright|} \mleft( \mleft|r(\theta)\mright| \mleft|r'(\theta)\mright| \cot(\theta/2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} r(\theta)^2 \mleft|\cot(\theta/2)\mright| \csc(\theta/2)^2 \mright) \notag \\
    &= \frac{1}{8n \cdot n^{2/3} \mleft|\sin(\theta/2)\mright| \mleft|\cos(\theta/2)\mright|} \mleft( \frac{\mleft|r(\theta)\mright| \mleft|r'(\theta)\mright| \cos(\theta/2)^2}{\sin(\theta/2)^2} + \frac{r(\theta)^2 \mleft|\cos(\theta/2)\mright|}{2 \mleft|\sin(\theta/2)\mright|^3} \mright) \notag \\
    &= \frac{1}{8n \cdot n^{2/3}} \mleft( \frac{\mleft|r(\theta)\mright| \mleft|r'(\theta)\mright| \mleft|\cos(\theta/2)\mright|}{\mleft|\sin(\theta/2)\mright|^3} + \frac{r(\theta)^2}{2 \mleft|\sin(\theta/2)\mright|^4} \mright) \notag \\
    &\le \frac{C}{n\cdot n^{2/3}} \mleft( \frac{d^{1/2} \cdot d^{3/2}}{\mleft|s(\theta)\mright|^{3/2}n^{-1}} + \frac{d}{s(\theta)^2 n^{-4/3}}\mright) \notag \\
    &= C \mleft( \frac{d^2/n^{2/3}}{\mleft|s(\theta)\mright|^{3/2}} + \frac{d/n^{1/3}}{s(\theta)^2} \mright)           
    \le \frac{C'}{\mleft|s(\theta)\mright|^{3/2}} , \label{eqn:|g'|} 
\end{align}
where the last inequality holds for all sufficiently large $n$ by the assumptions \zcref[S]{eqn:crit} and $ \mleft|s(\theta)\mright| \ge 1 $. 

We also note that by definition \zcref[S]{eqn:g_W}, 
\begin{align}
    g(-4n^{2/3}) &= n^{-1} q(-2)^2 = 0 , \label{eqn:g0} 
\end{align}
since the polynomial $q(x)$ contains a factor of $ (x+2)/4 $ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:pq}). 

We express the random variables $ S, \sigma $ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:ZLsigma} using the rescaled density $ \widetilde{\rho} $ and the rescaled polynomial $ g $ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:g_W,eqn:wtrho}: 
\begin{align}
    S &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n q(\lambda_i(X))^2 
    = \sum_{i = 1}^n n^{-1} q(2 + n^{2/3} (\lambda_i(X) - 2) \cdot n^{-2/3})^2
    = \sum_{i = 1}^n g(-L_i) , \notag \\
                                  
    \sigma &= \int_{-2}^2 q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x
    = \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^0 n^{-1} q(2 + sn^{-2/3})^2 \cdot n^{1/3} \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(2 + sn^{-2/3}) \,\mathrm{d} s
    = \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^0 g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s . \notag 
\end{align}
Now $ W_\ell $ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:g_W} can be rewritten as 
\begin{align}
    W_\ell &= \mleft( S - \sum_{i = 1}^n g(-L_i) \mathds1{\mleft\{-L_i \ge -\ell\mright\}} \mright)
    - \mleft( \sigma - \int_{-\ell}^0 g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s \mright) \notag \\
                                                                                                                         
    &= \sum_{i = 1}^n g(-L_i) \mathds1{\mleft\{-L_i < -4n^{2/3}\mright\}} 
    + \sum_{i = 1}^n g(-L_i) \mathds1{\mleft\{-4n^{2/3} \le -L_i < -\ell\mright\}} 
    - \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s . \label{eqn:W12}
\end{align}

Consider the first term in \zcref[S]{eqn:W12}: 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{i = 1}^n g(-L_i) \mathds1{\mleft\{-L_i < -4n^{2/3}\mright\}} 
    &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n q(\lambda_i(X))^2 \mathds1{\mleft\{\lambda_i(X) < -2\mright\}} . \label{eqn:W1}
\end{align}
We show that this term converges to $0$ in probability. 
Fix a constant $M>0$ and let 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal E}_M &\coloneqq \mleft\{ \lambda_n(X) \ge -2 - Mn^{-2/3} \mright\} . \notag 
\end{align}
On the event $ {\mathcal E}_M $, the only contribution to \zcref[S]{eqn:W1} comes from eigenvalues in the interval $ [-2 - Mn^{-2/3},-2] $. 
For any $ x\in[-2 - Mn^{-2/3},-2] $, let $ t\ge0 $ be defined through $ x = -2\cosh(t) $. 
Using the elementary estimate $ \cosh(t) \ge 1 + t^2/2 $ for $ t\ge0 $, we have $ t \le \sqrt{M} n^{-1/3} $. 
Then, using the explicit expression \zcref[S]{eqn:U_big_neg} of Chebyshev polynomials on $ (-\infty,-1) $ and following similar reasoning leading to \zcref[S]{eqn:sinh}, we have that for any $ 0\le k\le d-1 $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|p_k(x)\mright| &= \mleft|U_k(-\cosh(t))\mright| \le (k+1) e^{k t} \le C_M (k+1) , \label{eqn:pk} 
\end{align}
where the last inequality holds since $ kt \le dt \le \sqrt{M} d n^{-1/3} \le C_M $ by the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 
Applying Cauchy--Schwarz to $p$ in \zcref[S]{eqn:pq}, using \zcref[S]{eqn:pk,eqn:u_bdd}, we get
\begin{align}
    \mleft|p(x)\mright| &\le \sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2} \sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{d-1} p_k(x)^2}
    \le C_M \sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{d-1} (k+1)^2 }
    \le C_M' d^{3/2} . \notag 
\end{align}
Therefore, for any $ x\in[-2-Mn^{-2/3},-2] $, 
\begin{align}
    n^{-1} q(x)^2 &= \frac{(x+2)^2}{16n} p(x)^2 \le C_M'' n^{-4/3} n^{-1} d^{3} \le C_M''' n^{-4/3} , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last inequality is again by \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 
It then follows that on $ {\mathcal E}_M $, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n q(\lambda_i(X))^2 \mathds1{\mleft\{\lambda_i(X) < -2\mright\}}
    &\le C_M''' n^{-1/3} \to 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
For any fixed $\varepsilon>0$, 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i = 1}^n q(\lambda_i(X))^2 \mathds1{\mleft\{\lambda_i(X) < -2\mright\}} > \varepsilon \mright)
    &\le \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_M^c\mright) . \notag 
\end{align}
Upon taking the limit as $ M\to\infty $, the probability on the RHS above converges to $0$ by \zcref[S]{eqn:tight}. 
This proves
\begin{align}
    \sum_{i = 1}^n g(-L_i) \mathds1{\mleft\{-L_i < -4n^{2/3}\mright\}} &\overset{\mathrm{p}}{\to} 0 . \label{eqn:term1} 
\end{align}

We next turn to the remaining terms in \zcref[S]{eqn:W12}: 
\begin{align}
    & \sum_{i = 1}^n g(-L_i) \mathds1{\mleft\{-4n^{2/3} \le -L_i < -\ell\mright\}} 
    - \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s \notag \\
    &= \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g(s) \, \widehat{\mu}(\mathrm{d} s) - \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s \label{eqn:whrho} \\
    &= \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g(s) \,\mathrm{d} (\widehat{F} - \widetilde{F})(s) \label{eqn:whmu} \\
    &= g(-\ell) (\widehat{F}(-\ell) - \widetilde{F}(-\ell)) - \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g'(s) (\widehat{F}(s) - \widetilde{F}(s)) \,\mathrm{d} s , \label{eqn:IBT} 
\end{align}
where in \zcref[S]{eqn:whrho} we denote $ \widehat{\mu} \coloneqq \sum_{i = 1}^n \delta_{-L_i} $, and in \zcref[S]{eqn:whmu}, 
\begin{align}
&&
    \widehat{F}(s) &\coloneqq \widehat{\mu}( (-\infty,s) ) 
    = \mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : -L_i < s \mright\}\mright| , & 
    \widetilde{F}(s) &\coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^s \widetilde{\rho}(t) \,\mathrm{d} t . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
In obtaining \zcref[S]{eqn:IBT}, we use integration by parts and the boundary condition \zcref[S]{eqn:g0}. 
Defining 
\begin{align}
&&
    \Delta(s) &\coloneqq -(\widehat{F}(s) - \widetilde{F}(s)) , & 
    \overline{\Delta}(s) &\coloneqq \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\Delta(s)\mright] , & 
    \widetilde{\Delta}(s) &\coloneqq \Delta(s) - \overline{\Delta}(s) , &
& \notag 
\end{align}
and 
\begin{align}
&&
    \overline{W}_\ell &\coloneqq - g(-\ell) \overline{\Delta}(-\ell) + \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g'(s) \overline{\Delta}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s , & 
    \widetilde{W}_\ell &\coloneqq - g(-\ell) \widetilde{\Delta}(-\ell) + \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} g'(s) \widetilde{\Delta}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s , &
& \label{eqn:olwtWell} 
\end{align}
we can further rewrite \zcref[S]{eqn:IBT} as $ \overline{W}_\ell + \widetilde{W}_\ell $. 
To control the latter, we treat the mean $ \overline{W}_\ell $ and the fluctuation $ \widetilde{W}_\ell $ separately. 

For any $ s\in[-4n^{2/3},0] $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \overline{\Delta}(s) \mright|
    &= \mleft| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ n - \widehat{F}(s) \mright] - (n - \widetilde{F}(s)) \mright| \notag \\
    &= \mleft| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft|\mleft\{ i\in[n] : -L_i \ge s \mright\}\mright|\mright] - \int_s^\infty \widetilde{\rho}(t) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright| \notag \\
    &= \mleft| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\widetilde{N}_X(s)\mright] - n \int_{2+sn^{-2/3}}^\infty \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(t) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright| \notag \\
    &= \mleft| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ N_X(2+sn^{-2/3}) \mright] - n\mu_{\textnormal{sc}}((2+sn^{-2/3},\infty)) \mright| 
    \le C , \label{eqn:olDelta} 
\end{align}
where the last inequality follows from \zcref[S]{eqn:NX} by noting that $ 2+sn^{-2/3} \in [-2,2] $. 
Combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:|g|,eqn:|g'|}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \overline{W}_\ell \mright|
    &\le \mleft|g(-\ell)\mright| \mleft|\overline{\Delta}(-\ell)\mright| + \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} \mleft|g'(s)\mright| \mleft|\overline{\Delta}(s)\mright| \,\mathrm{d} s
    \le C \mleft( \frac{1}{\ell} + \int_\ell^{4n^{2/3}} s^{-3/2} \,\mathrm{d} s \mright)
    \le \frac{C'}{\sqrt{\ell}} , \notag 
\end{align}
for all sufficiently large $n$. 
In particular, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{\ell\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mleft|\overline{W}_\ell\mright| &= 0 . \label{eqn:olWell}
\end{align}

Next, by \zcref[S]{eqn:VarX}, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ \widetilde{N}_X(s) \mright]  
    = \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ N_X(2 + sn^{-2/3}) \mright]
    &\le C \log( 2 + \mleft|s\mright|^{3/2} ) \le C' \log( 2 + \mleft|s\mright| ) , \notag 
\end{align}
for any $ s\in[-4n^{2/3},0] $. 
Hence, using Cauchy--Schwarz, we have
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft|\widetilde{\Delta}(s)\mright|\mright]
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft| \Delta(s) - \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\Delta(s)\mright] \mright|\mright]
    \le \sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ \Delta(s) \mright]} \notag \\
    &= \sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ n - \widehat{F}(s) \mright]} 
    = \sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ \widetilde{N}_X(s) \mright]}
    \le C' \sqrt{\log(2 + \mleft|s\mright|)} , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last line follows from similar reasoning leading to \zcref[S]{eqn:olDelta}. 
Combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:|g|,eqn:|g'|}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft|\widetilde{W}_\ell\mright|\mright]
    &\le \mleft|g(-\ell)\mright| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft|\widetilde{\Delta}(-\ell)\mright|\mright] + \int_{-4n^{2/3}}^{-\ell} \mleft|g'(s)\mright| \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft|\widetilde{\Delta}(s)\mright|\mright] \,\mathrm{d} s \notag \\
    &\le C \mleft( \frac{\sqrt{\log(2 + \mleft|\ell\mright|)}}{\ell} + \int_\ell^{4n^{2/3}} \frac{\sqrt{\log(2+s)}}{s^{3/2}} \,\mathrm{d} s \mright) \notag \\
    &\le C \mleft( \frac{\sqrt{\log(2 + \mleft|\ell\mright|)}}{\ell} + \int_\ell^\infty \frac{\sqrt{\log(2+s)}}{s^{3/2}} \,\mathrm{d} s \mright) . \notag 
\end{align}
Note that the terms in the last line above depend only on $\ell$ and converge to $0$ as $\ell\to\infty$. 
Thus $ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft|\widetilde{W}_\ell\mright|\mright] \to 0 $ as $n\to\infty$ followed by $ \ell\to\infty $. 
Invoking Markov's inequality, for any $ \varepsilon>0 $, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{\ell\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft| \widetilde{W}_\ell \mright| > \varepsilon \mright)
    &\le \lim_{\ell\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mleft|\widetilde{W}_\ell\mright|\mright] / \varepsilon 
    = 0 . \label{eqn:wtWell} 
\end{align}
Finally, recalling \zcref[S]{eqn:W12,eqn:olwtWell}, taking \zcref[S]{eqn:term1,eqn:olWell,eqn:wtWell} collectively, we conclude \zcref[S]{eqn:Well}. 

\paragraph{Convergence of $ Y_\ell $.}
Recall that our eventual aim is to show the convergence of $S$. 
We have just controlled the residue term $ W_\ell $ and have already shown the convergence of $ \sigma $. 
It remains to investigate the convergence of the last two terms in the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:g_W} of $ W_\ell $. 
Indeed, denote these terms by $ Y_\ell $ and define two accompanying truncated versions
\begin{align}
    Y_\ell &\coloneqq \sum_{j=1}^n g(-L_j) \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -L_j\mright\}} - \int_{-\ell}^0 g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s , \notag \\
                                  
    Y_{\ell,\ell'} &\coloneqq \sum_{j=1}^n g(-L_j) \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -L_j \le \ell'\mright\}} - \int_{-\ell}^0 g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s , \notag \\
                                  
    Y_{\ell,\ell',N} &\coloneqq \sum_{j=1}^N g(-L_j) \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -L_j \le \ell'\mright\}} - \int_{-\ell}^0 g(s) \widetilde{\rho}(s) \,\mathrm{d} s , \notag 
\end{align}
for $ \ell'>0 $ and $ N\in{\mathbb Z}_{\ge m} $ fixed (relative to $n$). 
The limits of these objects will be shown to be 
\begin{align}
    \Upsilon_\ell &\coloneqq \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -\Lambda_{j-1}\mright\}} - \int_{-\ell}^0 \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s)^2 \frac{\sqrt{-s}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} s , \notag \\
                                  
    \Upsilon_{\ell,\ell'} &\coloneqq \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -\Lambda_{j-1} \le \ell'\mright\}} - \int_{-\ell}^0 \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s)^2 \frac{\sqrt{-s}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} s , \notag \\
                                  
    \Upsilon_{\ell,\ell',N} &\coloneqq \sum_{j=1}^N \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -\Lambda_{j-1} \le \ell'\mright\}} - \int_{-\ell}^0 \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s)^2 \frac{\sqrt{-s}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} s , \notag 
\end{align}
respectively. 

To show the desired convergence, first note that by \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Ramirez_Rider_Virag}, for any fixed $m\ge1$, 
\begin{align}
    (L_1, \cdots, L_m) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) . \label{eqn:Airy}
\end{align}
Now consider $ Y_{\ell,\ell',N} $.
