By \zcref[S]{eqn:q_Gamma}, $ g \to (\Gamma_{\delta,u})^2 $ point-wise uniformly on $ [-\ell,\ell'] $. 
Moreover, it is easy to see that 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{s\in[-\ell,0]} \mleft| \widetilde{\rho}(s) - \pi^{-1} \sqrt{-s} \mright| &\to 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
Since the Airy point process has no atom at any deterministic point, by the continuous mapping theorem, we have the following joint convergence as $n\to\infty$: 
\begin{align}
    ( Y_{\ell,\ell',N}, L_1, \cdots, L_m ) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Upsilon_{\ell,\ell',N}, \Lambda_{0}, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) . \label{eqn:Yellell'N} 
\end{align}

Next, we pass this result to the convergence of $ Y_{\ell,\ell'} $ by taking $N\to\infty$. 
To do so, note that on the event $ \mleft\{ -L_{N+1} < - \ell \mright\} $, $ Y_{\ell,\ell'} = Y_{\ell,\ell',N} $ since none of $ (L_i)_{i=N+1}^n $ contributes to $ Y_{\ell,\ell'} $. 
So $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( Y_{\ell,\ell',N} \ne Y_{\ell,\ell'} \mright) \le \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( -L_{N+1} \ge -\ell \mright) $. 
By \zcref[S]{eqn:Airy}, passing to the $n\to\infty$ limit, we have
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( Y_{\ell,\ell',N} \ne Y_{\ell,\ell'} \mright) &\le \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( -\Lambda_N \ge -\ell \mright) . \notag 
\end{align}
Since $ \Lambda_{j-1} \to \infty $ as $j\to\infty$ almost surely, the RHS above converges to $0$ as $N\to\infty$. 
Therefore, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{N\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( Y_{\ell,\ell',N} \ne Y_{\ell,\ell'} \mright) &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
Following a similar argument, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{N\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \Upsilon_{\ell,\ell',N} \ne \Upsilon_{\ell,\ell'} \mright) &\le \lim_{N\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( -\Lambda_N \ge -\ell \mright) = 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
Combining the preceding two displays with \zcref[S]{eqn:Yellell'N}, we have 
\begin{align}
    ( Y_{\ell,\ell'}, L_1, \cdots, L_m ) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} ( \Upsilon_{\ell,\ell'}, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1} ) . \label{eqn:Yellell'} 
\end{align}

Finally, we establish the convergence of $ Y_{\ell} $ by taking $ \ell'\to\infty $. 
This is similar to the last step. 
On the event $ \mleft\{ -L_1 \le \ell' \mright\} $, $ Y_{\ell,\ell'} = Y_\ell $ since $ (-L_j)_{j\in[n]} $ is nonincreasing.
So $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( Y_\ell \ne Y_{\ell,\ell'} \mright) \le \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( -L_1 > \ell' \mright) $. 
Taking the $n\to\infty$ limit and using the tightness of $ L_1 $, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{\ell'\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( Y_\ell \ne Y_{\ell,\ell'} \mright) &\le \lim_{\ell'\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(-L_1 > \ell'\mright) = 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
Similarly, by tightness of $ \Lambda_0 $, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{\ell'\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \Upsilon_\ell \ne \Upsilon_{\ell,\ell'} \mright) &= \lim_{\ell'\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( -\Lambda_0 > \ell' \mright) = 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
Using this and sending $ \ell'\to\infty $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:Yellell'}, we conclude 
\begin{align}
    ( Y_\ell, L_1, \cdots, L_m ) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} ( \Upsilon_\ell, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1} ) . \label{eqn:Yell}
\end{align}

\paragraph{Summability of a limiting series.}
We prove 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 &< \infty , \qquad \mathrm{a.s.} \label{eqn:Gamma_sum} 
\end{align}
Denoting $ H(r) \coloneqq \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-r)^2 $ and recalling the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:Gamma} of $ \Gamma_{\delta,u} $, we have $ H(r) = \delta^3 I(z(r))^2 / z(r)^2 $ for $ r>0 $, where $ z(r) \coloneqq \delta\sqrt{r} $ and
\begin{align}
    I(z) &\coloneqq \int_0^1 u(t) \sin(tz) \,\mathrm{d} t . \notag 
\end{align}
Note that both $I$ and $I'$ are uniformly bounded. 
Indeed, by Cauchy--Schwarz and $ L^2 $-boundedness of $u$, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|I(z)\mright| &\le \sqrt{\int_0^1 u(t)^2 \,\mathrm{d} t} \sqrt{\int_0^1 \sin(tz)^2 \,\mathrm{d} t} \le \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} < C , \notag 
\end{align}
and similarly, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|I'(z)\mright| &= \mleft|\int_0^1 u(t) \cos(tz) t \,\mathrm{d} t\mright|
    \le \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} \sqrt{\int_0^1 t^2 \,\mathrm{d} t} < C . \notag 
\end{align}
This implies that for $r\ge1$, $ 0 \le H(r) \le C/r $ and 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|H'(r)\mright| &= \frac{\delta^4}{\sqrt{r}} \mleft| \frac{I(z(r)) I'(z(r))}{z(r)^2} - \frac{I(z(r))^2}{z(r)^3} \mright|           
    \le \frac{C'}{\sqrt{r}} \mleft( \frac{1}{r} + \frac{1}{r^{3/2}} \mright)
    \le C / r^{3/2} . \label{eqn:H'} 
\end{align}

Now denote by $ \mu_{\Lambda} \coloneqq \sum_{j\ge1} \delta_{-\Lambda_{j-1}} $ the (random) empirical measure of the Airy point process $ (-\Lambda_{j-1})_{j\ge1} $. 
Denote by $ F_\Lambda(r) \coloneqq \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \mu_\Lambda([-r,0)) \mright] $. 
Then \cite[Theorem 1.6]{Kim} shows that for any $r>0$, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mu_\Lambda( [-r,\infty) )\mright] &= \frac{2}{3\pi} r^{3/2} + D_\Lambda(r) , \notag 
\end{align}
for a function $ D_\Lambda $ satisfying $ \sup_{r>0} \mleft|D_\Lambda(r)\mright| < \infty $. 
Since the Airy point process is locally finite and its largest point $ -\Lambda_0 $ is almost surely finite, we have $ \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mu_\Lambda([0,\infty))\mright] = D_\Lambda(0) < \infty $ and therefore 
\begin{align}
    F_\Lambda(r) &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mu_\Lambda( [-r,\infty) )\mright] - \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mu_\Lambda( [0,\infty) )\mright]
    = \frac{2}{3\pi} r^{3/2} + D(r) , \label{eqn:FLambda}  
\end{align}
where $ D(r) \coloneqq D_\Lambda(r) - D_\Lambda(0) $ is uniformly bounded. 

Let us compute \zcref[S]{eqn:Gamma_sum} restricted to the interval $ [-\ell,0) $ for a large fixed constant $\ell>0$:  
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \sum_{j\ge1} H(\Lambda_{j-1}) \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -\Lambda_{j-1} < 0\mright\}} \mright] &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \int_{-\ell}^0 H(-x) \, \mu_\Lambda(\mathrm{d} x) \mright] \notag \\
    &= \int_{-\ell}^0 H(-x) \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[\mu_\Lambda(\mathrm{d} x)\mright]
    = \int^{\ell}_0 H(r) \,\mathrm{d} F_\Lambda(r) . \label{eqn:EsumH} 
\end{align}
We split the integration region into $ [1,\ell) $ and $ [0,1) $. 
For the first part, 
\begin{align}
    \int^{\ell}_{1} H(r) \,\mathrm{d} F_\Lambda(r)
    &= \int^{\ell}_{1} H(r) \frac{\sqrt{r}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} r + \int^{\ell}_1 H(r) \,\mathrm{d} D(r) . \notag
\end{align}
Since $ H $ is nonnegative by definition, the first part is at most 
\begin{align}
    \int^{\ell}_{1} H(r) \frac{\sqrt{r}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} r &\le \int_0^\infty \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-r)^2 \frac{\sqrt{r}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} r = \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 , \notag 
\end{align}
by \zcref[S]{eqn:identity}, which is uniformly bounded in $\ell$. 
For the second part, we apply integration by parts and obtain
\begin{align}
    \int^{\ell}_1 H(r) \,\mathrm{d} D(r)
    &= D(\ell) H(\ell) - D(1) H(1) - \int_1^\ell D(r) H'(r) \,\mathrm{d} r , \notag 
\end{align}
whose absolute value is, by \zcref[S]{eqn:H'}, at most
\begin{align}
    C \mleft( \frac{1}{\ell} + 1 + \int_1^\ell \frac{1}{r^{3/2}} \,\mathrm{d} r \mright) , \notag 
\end{align}
in particular, uniformly bounded for all $\ell\ge1$. 

For the integral on $ [0,1) $, we can similarly decompose it into parts with respect to the two components of $ F_\Lambda $ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:FLambda}). 
The part with respect to $ 2 (3\pi)^{-1} r^{3/2} $ is still uniformly bounded in $\ell$ by \zcref[S]{eqn:identity}. 
The other part with respect to $D(r)$ is also uniformly bounded in $\ell$ since by the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:Gamma}, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{r\downarrow0} H(r) &= \delta^3 \mleft(\int_0^1 u(t) t \,\mathrm{d} t\mright)^2
    \le \delta^3 \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 \mleft(\int_0^1 t^2 \,\mathrm{d} t\mright) < C . \notag 
\end{align}
Therefore, back to \zcref[S]{eqn:EsumH}, we have
\begin{align}
    \sup_{\ell\ge1} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \sum_{j\ge1} H(\Lambda_{j-1}) \mathds1{\mleft\{-\ell \le -\Lambda_{j-1} < 0\mright\}} \mright] &< \infty . \notag 
\end{align}
Since the expectation is nondecreasing in $\ell$, monotone convergence gives
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \sum_{j\ge1} H(\Lambda_{j-1}) \mathds1{\mleft\{-\Lambda_{j-1} < 0\mright\}} \mright] &< \infty . \notag 
\end{align}
Finally, the positive part
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \sum_{j\ge1} H(\Lambda_{j-1}) \mathds1{\mleft\{-\Lambda_{j-1} \ge 0\mright\}} \mright] \notag 
\end{align}
can be easily seen finite since the Airy point process $ (-\Lambda_{j-1})_{j\ge1} $ is almost surely bounded above and locally finite. 
Since \zcref[S]{eqn:Gamma_sum} has finite expectation, it is finite almost surely. 

Using \zcref[S]{eqn:Gamma_sum,eqn:identity}, we have 
\begin{align}
    \Upsilon_\ell &\to \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 - \int_{-\infty}^0 \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s)^2 \frac{\sqrt{-s}}{\pi} \,\mathrm{d} s
    = \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 \eqqcolon \Upsilon , \label{eqn:Upsilonell} 
\end{align}
as $\ell\to\infty$. 

\paragraph{Concluding the result.}
For fixed $\ell$, the convergence results $ \sigma \to \overline{\sigma} $ and \zcref[S]{eqn:Yell} imply 
\begin{align}
    (Y_\ell + \sigma , L_1, \cdots, L_m) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} ( \Upsilon_\ell + \overline{\sigma} , \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1} ) , \label{eqn:Y1} 
\end{align}
as $ n\to\infty $. 
Further using \zcref[S]{eqn:Upsilonell} to take the $\ell\to\infty$ limit, we obtain
\begin{align}
    ( \Upsilon_\ell + \overline{\sigma} , \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1} ) &\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\to} (\Upsilon + \overline{\sigma}, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1})
    = (\Sigma, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) , \label{eqn:Y2}
\end{align}
where the last equality is by the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:Upsilonell} of $ \Upsilon $. 

Denoting 
\begin{align}
&&
    {\bm{S}} &\coloneqq (S, L_1, \cdots, L_m) , & 
    {\bm{S}}_\ell &\coloneqq (Y_\ell + \sigma, L_1, \cdots, L_m) , & 
    {\bm \Sigma} &\coloneqq (\Sigma, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) , & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
let us verify the convergence of $ {\bm{S}} $ to $ {\bm \Sigma} $ in distribution. 
Let $ f \colon {\mathbb R}^{m+1} \to {\mathbb R} $ be an arbitrary $1$-bounded $1$-Lipschitz function. 
For any $\varepsilon>0$, let $ {\mathcal E}_\varepsilon \coloneqq \mleft\{ \mleft| S - (Y_\ell + \sigma) \mright| > \varepsilon \mright\} $. 
Then 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|f({\bm{S}}) - f({\bm{S}}_\ell)\mright| &\le \mleft| (f({\bm{S}}) - f({\bm{S}}_\ell)) \mathds1_{{\mathcal E}_\varepsilon} \mright| + \mleft| (f({\bm{S}}) - f({\bm{S}}_\ell)) \mathds1_{{\mathcal E}_\varepsilon^c} \mright| \notag \\
    &\le (\mleft|f({\bm{S}})\mright| + \mleft|f({\bm{S}}_\ell)\mright|) \mathds1_{{\mathcal E}_\varepsilon} + \mleft|S - (Y_\ell + \sigma)\mright| \mathds1_{{\mathcal E}_\varepsilon^c}           
    \le 2 \mathds1_{{\mathcal E}_\varepsilon} + \varepsilon , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last inequality follows from $ \mleft\|f\mright\|_{\infty}\le1 , \mleft\|f\mright\|_{\mathrm{Lip}} \le 1 $ by the choice of $f$. 
Taking expectations and using Jensen's inequality, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[f({\bm{S}})\mright] - \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[f({\bm{S}}_\ell)\mright]\mright|
    &\le \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \mleft| f({\bm{S}}) - f({\bm{S}}_\ell) \mright| \mright]
    \le 2 \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_\varepsilon\mright) + \varepsilon . \label{eqn:fZ} 
\end{align}
Note that $ S - (Y_\ell + \sigma) = W_\ell $ (see \zcref[S]{eqn:g_W}), so by \zcref[S]{eqn:Well}, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{\ell\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\mathcal E}_\varepsilon\mright) &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
Using this in \zcref[S]{eqn:fZ}, sending $ n\to\infty $, $ \ell\to\infty $ and $ \varepsilon\to0 $ in that order, we arrive at 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{\varepsilon\to0} \lim_{\ell\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mleft|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[f({\bm{S}})\mright] - \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[f({\bm{S}}_\ell)\mright]\mright|
    &= 0 . \label{eqn:Z} 
\end{align}
Moreover, we have already shown in \zcref[S]{eqn:Y1,eqn:Y2} that $ {\bm{S}}_\ell \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} {\bm \Sigma} $ under the sequential limit $ n\to\infty $ followed by $ \ell\to\infty $. 
Combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:Z}, we conclude $ {\bm{S}} \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} {\bm \Sigma} $ as $n\to\infty$, as promised in \zcref[S]{eqn:Zlim}. 
Since $S\ge0$ for all $n$, $\Sigma\ge0$ almost surely. 
This completes the proof of the lemma.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:Sconv}
Assume \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 
Let the polynomials $ p,q $, the step function $ u_n $ and its weak limit $ u $ in $ L^2([0,1]) $ be as in \zcref[S]{eqn:pq,eqn:u_bdd,eqn:u}. 
Let $ X \sim \operatorname{GOE}(n) $ and define $ L_j $ for $j\in[n]$ as in \zcref[S]{eqn:ZLsigma}. 
Let $ S $ be any random variable such that for any fixed $m$, 
\begin{align}
    (S, L_1, \cdots, L_m) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Sigma, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) . \notag 
\end{align}
Then 
\begin{align}
    \mleft( S, n^{-1} q(\lambda_1(X))^2, \cdots, n^{-1} q(\lambda_m(X))^2 \mright)
    &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Sigma, \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2, \cdots, \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{m-1})^2) . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Recall $g$ from \zcref[S]{eqn:g_W}. 
By \zcref[S]{eqn:q_Gamma}, for any compact $I\subset{\mathbb R}$, 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{s\in I} \mleft| g(s) - \Gamma_{\delta,u}(s)^2 \mright| &\to 0 . \label{eqn:gconv}
\end{align}
Note that the function $ \Gamma_{\delta,u} $ is continuous. 

Denote $ {\bm{L}} \coloneqq (S, L_1, \cdots, L_m) $ and $ {\bm \Lambda} \coloneqq (\Sigma, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) $. 
Define functions $ T_n, T \colon {\mathbb R}^{m+1} \to {\mathbb R}^{m+1} $ as 
\begin{align}
&& 
    T_n(s, \ell_1, \cdots, \ell_m) &= (s, g(-\ell_1), \cdots, g(-\ell_m)) , & 
    T(s, \ell_1, \cdots, \ell_{m}) &= (s, \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\ell_1)^2, \cdots, \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\ell_{m})^2) . & 
& \notag
\end{align}
Since the assumption $ {\bm{L}} \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} {\bm \Lambda} $ implies tightness, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there is a compact set $ K_\varepsilon \subset {\mathbb R}^{m+1} $ such that 
\begin{align}
&&
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\bm{L}} \notin K_\varepsilon\mright) &< \varepsilon , & 
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft({\bm \Lambda} \notin K_\varepsilon\mright) &< \varepsilon . 
& \label{eqn:K} 
\end{align}
Applying \zcref[S]{eqn:gconv} with $I\subset{\mathbb R}$ being a compact interval containing 
\begin{align}
    \bigcup_{i=1}^m \mleft\{ -\ell_i : (s,\ell_1, \cdots, \ell_i, \cdots, \ell_m) \in K_\varepsilon \mright\} , \notag 
\end{align}
we have 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{(s,\ell_1, \cdots, \ell_m)\in K_\varepsilon} \mleft\| T_n(s, \ell_1, \cdots, \ell_m) - T(s, \ell_1, \cdots, \ell_m) \mright\|_{2} &\to 0 . \label{eqn:T} 
\end{align}
Combining \zcref[S]{eqn:K,eqn:T}, we have
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft\|T_n({\bm{L}}) - T({\bm{L}})\mright\|_{2} > \varepsilon \mright)
    &\le \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( {\bm{L}} \notin K_\varepsilon \mright)
    < 2\varepsilon , \notag 
\end{align}
that is, $ \mleft\|T_n({\bm{L}}) - T({\bm{L}})\mright\|_{2} \to 0 $ in probability and therefore also in distribution. 
On the other hand, since $T$ is continuous and $ {\bm{L}} \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} {\bm \Lambda} $ by assumption, the continuous mapping theorem gives $ T({\bm{L}}) \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} T({\bm \Lambda}) $. 
Putting together the preceding two convergence results, we have $ T_n({\bm{L}}) \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} T({\bm \Lambda}) $, which is precisely the sought conclusion.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:a}
Assume \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 
Let the polynomials $ p,q $, the step function $ u_n $ and its weak limit $ u $ in $ L^2([0,1]) $ be as in \zcref[S]{eqn:pq,eqn:u_bdd,eqn:u}. 
Let $ X\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n) $. 
Then for any $\varepsilon>0$, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{M\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} n^{-1} q(\lambda_i(X))^2 > \varepsilon \mright) &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
In the proof, we need the following estimate for the diagonal part of the Christoffel--Darboux kernel defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:Kd}: 
\begin{align}
    K_{d-1}(x,x) &= \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} p_k(x)^2
    \le \min\mleft\{ d^3, \frac{4d}{4 - x^2} \mright\} , \label{eqn:Kd_est} 
\end{align}
for any $x\in[-2,2]$. 
The first bound follows from \zcref[S]{eqn:pm}: 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} p_k(x)^2 &\le \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} (k+1)^2 \le d^3 . \notag  
\end{align}
The second bound follows from the sine representation of Chebyshev polynomials on $[-1,1]$ given in \zcref[S]{eqn:U_small}. 
Indeed, under the change of variable $ x = 2\cos(\theta) $, we have
\begin{align}
    \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} p_k(x)^2 &= \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} \frac{\sin( (k+1)\theta )^2}{\sin(\theta)^2}
    \le \frac{d}{1 - \cos(\theta)^2} = \frac{4d}{4 - x^2} . \notag 
\end{align}

Now for $p$ in \zcref[S]{eqn:pq}, by Cauchy--Schwarz and the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:u_bdd}, we have 
\begin{align}
    p(x)^2 &\le \mleft(\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2\mright) \mleft(\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} p_k(x)^2\mright) 
    \le C K_{d-1}(x,x) , \notag 
\end{align}
for all $x\in[-2,2]$. 
Using \zcref[S]{eqn:Kd_est},
\begin{align}
    n^{-1} q(x)^2 &= \frac{(x+2)^2}{16n} p(x)^2
    \le C \min\mleft\{ \frac{(x+2)^2 d^3}{n} , \frac{(x+2) d}{n (2-x)} \mright\} , \label{eqn:nq2} 
\end{align}
for all $ x\in[-2,2) $. 

By the sign symmetry of GOE, the convergence $ n^{2/3}(2 - \lambda_1(X)) \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} \Lambda_0 $ from \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Ramirez_Rider_Virag}, and tightness of $ \Lambda_0 $, for any $\varepsilon>0$, there exists $L>0$ such that 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \lambda_n(X) < -2-Ln^{-2/3} \mright)
    &= \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \lambda_1(X) > 2 + Ln^{-2/3} \mright)
    = \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \Lambda_0 < -L \mright) < \varepsilon . \notag 
\end{align}
Moreover, under this choice of $L$, there exists $M>0$ such that 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \lambda_M(X) > 2-Ln^{-2/3} \mright)
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \Lambda_{M-1} < L \mright)
    < \varepsilon , \notag 
\end{align}
since $ \Lambda_{j-1} \to \infty $ as $j\to\infty$. 
Also, $ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(\lambda_1(X) > 3\mright) < \varepsilon $ for all sufficiently large $n$; see \zcref[S]{eqn:Xop}. 
In view of these estimates, defining 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal G}_{L,M} &\coloneqq \mleft\{
        \lambda_n(X) \ge -2-Ln^{-2/3} , \; 
        \lambda_M(X) \le 2 - Ln^{-2/3} , \;
        \lambda_1(X) \le 3
    \mright\} , \notag 
\end{align}
we have 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( {\mathcal G}_{L,M}^c \mright) &\le 3 \varepsilon . \label{eqn:GLM} 
\end{align}

On the event $ {\mathcal G}_{L,M} $, consider an integer $i\in [M,n]$. 
If $ \lambda_i(X) \in [-2,2-Ln^{-2/3}] $, from \zcref[S]{eqn:nq2}, it holds
\begin{align}
    n^{-1} q(\lambda_i(X))^2 &\le C \frac{d (\lambda_i(X) + 2)}{n (2 - \lambda_i(X))}
    \le C \frac{4d}{Ln^{1/3}}
    \le \frac{C'}{L} , \label{eqn:q21} 
\end{align}
where the last inequality is by the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 
If $ \lambda_i(X) \in [-2-Ln^{-2/3}, -2) $, then we write $ \lambda_i(X) = -2-sn^{-2/3} $ for some $ s\in(0,L] $. 
By \zcref[S]{eqn:bdd_diff} (and its counterpart for $s\in[0,L]$),                                           
we have that for any compact interval $I\subset{\mathbb R}$,
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in I} \sup_{t\in[0,1]} \mleft| d^{-1} p_{\mleft\lceil(d-1)t\mright\rceil}(2 + sn^{-2/3}) - \psi_s(t) \mright| &= 0 , \label{eqn:p_psi} 
\end{align}
where $ \psi_s(t) $ is defined as 
\begin{align}
    \psi_s(t) &\coloneqq \begin{cases}
        \frac{\sin(\delta \sqrt{-s} \, t)}{\delta \sqrt{-s}} , & s < 0 \\
        t , & s = 0 \\
        \frac{\sinh(\delta \sqrt{s} \, t)}{\delta \sqrt{s}} , & s > 0 
    \end{cases} . \notag 
\end{align}
Noting from \zcref[S]{eqn:U_big_neg,eqn:U_big} that $ p_k(-x) = (-1)^k p_k(x) $ for $ x\notin(-2,2) $, using \zcref[S]{eqn:p_psi} with $ I = [0,L] $, we have 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{s\in[0,L]} \sup_{t\in[0,1]} \mleft| (-1)^{\mleft\lceil(d-1)t\mright\rceil} d^{-1} p_{\mleft\lceil(d-1)t\mright\rceil}(-2-sn^{-2/3}) - \psi_s(t) \mright| &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
Using this with $ 2+sn^{-2/3} = -\lambda_i(X) $, we further have that for all sufficiently large $n$, 
\begin{align}
    \sup_{0\le k\le d-1} \mleft| p_k(\lambda_i(X)) \mright|
    &\le 2d \sup_{s\in[0,L]} \sup_{t\in[0,1]} \mleft|\psi_s(t)\mright|
    \le C_L d . \notag 
\end{align}
Therefore, by Cauchy--Schwarz, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|p(\lambda_i(X))\mright| &\le \sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{d-1} c_k^2} \sqrt{ \sum_{k=0}^{d-1} p_k(\lambda_i(X))^2 }
    \le C_L d^{3/2} , \notag 
\end{align}
and hence, 
\begin{align}
    n^{-1} q(\lambda_i(X))^2 &\le \frac{L^2n^{-4/3}}{16n} C_L^2 d^3 \le C_L' n^{-4/3} , \label{eqn:q22} 
\end{align}
by the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 

Combining \zcref[S]{eqn:q21,eqn:q22}, for all sufficiently large $n$, on $ {\mathcal G}_{L,M} $, we have
\begin{align}
    \max_{i\in[M,n]} n^{-1} q(\lambda_i(X))^2 &\le 2C'/L , \notag 
\end{align}
which, upon further combined with \zcref[S]{eqn:GLM}, immediately implies 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} n^{-1} q(\lambda_i(X))^2 > 2C'/L \mright) &\le 3\varepsilon . \notag 
\end{align}
Since this holds for all sufficiently large $ L,M $, the claimed result follows.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:conv}
Let $ a_1, \cdots, a_n $ be nonnegative random variables satisfying 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{M\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i > \zeta \mright) &= 0 , \label{eqn:ai} 
\end{align}
for every $\zeta>0$. 
Let $ (Z_i)_{i\ge1} $ be i.i.d.\ $ \chi_1^2 $ random variables independent of $ (a_i)_{i=1}^n $. 
Denote $ S \coloneqq \sum_{i=1}^n a_i $ and assume that for every fixed $M$, 
\begin{align}
    (S, a_1, \cdots, a_M) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Sigma, \eta_1, \cdots, \eta_M) \label{eqn:Sa}
\end{align}
as $n\to\infty$. 
Then denoting $ \beta \coloneqq \Sigma - \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j $, we have
\begin{enumerate}
    \item\label{itm:beta1} $ \beta\ge0 $ almost surely; 

    \item\label{itm:beta2} $ \sum_{i=1}^n a_i Z_i \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} \beta + \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j Z_j $. 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Note that $ S - \sum_{j = 1}^m a_j = \sum_{j = m+1}^n a_j \ge 0 $ almost surely. 
Passing to the limit immediately yields \zcref[S]{itm:beta1}. 

For \zcref[S]{itm:beta2}, define 
\begin{align}
&&
    D_n &\coloneqq \sum_{i = 1}^n a_i Z_i , & 
    D_{n,M} &\coloneqq \sum_{i = 1}^{M-1} a_i Z_i + \sum_{i = M}^n a_i . & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
By definition, 
\begin{align}
    D_n - D_{n,M} &= \sum_{i = M}^n a_i (Z_i - 1) , \notag 
\end{align}
whose variance conditioned on $ (a_i)_{i = 1}^n $ is 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ D_n - D_{n,M} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, (a_i)_{i = 1}^n \mright]
    &\le \sum_{i = M}^n a_i^2 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ Z_i - 1 \mright]
    = 2 \sum_{i = M}^n a_i^2 
    \le 2 \mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i \mright) \sum_{i = M}^n a_i
    \le 2 \mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i \mright) S . \label{eqn:var} 
\end{align}

Now fix $ \varepsilon>0 $ and $ \gamma>0 $. 
Since $ S \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} \Sigma $, $S$ is tight uniformly over $n$. 
So there exists $K$ such that 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(S > K\mright) &< \gamma/3 . \label{eqn:gammaS} 
\end{align}
Next, choose $ \zeta>0 $ sufficiently small such that $ 2K\zeta \varepsilon^{-2} < \gamma/3 $. 
By the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:ai}, for all sufficiently large $M$, 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i > \zeta \mright) &< \gamma/3 . \label{eqn:gammaa}  
\end{align}

For a large enough $M$, let 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal G} &\coloneqq \mleft\{ S\le K , \; \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i \le \zeta \mright\} . \notag 
\end{align}
Then 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft| D_n - D_{n,M} \mright| > \varepsilon \mright)
    &\le \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( {\mathcal G}^c \mright) + \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft\{ \mleft| D_n - D_{n,M} \mright| > \varepsilon \mright\} \cap {\mathcal G} \mright) . \label{eqn:term12} 
\end{align}
By union bound and subadditivity of $ \limsup $, the first term is at most 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( {\mathcal G}^c \mright)
    &\le \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( S > K \mright) + \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i > \zeta \mright)
    \le 2\gamma/3 , \notag
\end{align}
where the last step is by \zcref[S]{eqn:gammaS,eqn:gammaa}. 
For the second term, conditioning on $ (a_i)_{i=1}^n $, using measurability of $ {\mathcal G} $ with respect to $(a_i)_{i=1}^n$, and applying Chebyshev's inequality, we have 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft\{ \mleft| D_n - D_{n,M} \mright| > \varepsilon \mright\} \cap {\mathcal G} \mright)
    &= \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft| D_n - D_{n,M} \mright| > \varepsilon \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, (a_i)_{i = 1}^n \mright) \mathds1_{{\mathcal G}} \mright] \notag \\
    &\le \varepsilon^{-2} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\mleft[ D_n - D_{n,M} \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, (a_i)_{i = 1}^n \mright] \mathds1_{\mathcal G} \mright] \notag \\
    &\le \varepsilon^{-2} \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ 2 \mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i \mright) S \mathds1_{\mathcal G} \mright]
    \le \varepsilon^{-2} \cdot 2 \zeta K 
    < \gamma/3 , \notag 
\end{align}
where the last line is by \zcref[S]{eqn:var} and the choice of $ \zeta $. 

Taking $\limsup$ as $ n\to\infty $ in \zcref[S]{eqn:term12} and combining the preceding two estimates, we obtain 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft| D_n - D_{n,M} \mright| > \varepsilon \mright)
    &\le \gamma , \notag 
\end{align}
for all large $M$. 
Since $ \gamma $ is arbitrary, further taking the $M\to\infty$ limit, we arrive at 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{M\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \mleft| D_n - D_{n,M} \mright| > \varepsilon \mright) &= 0 . \label{eqn:Dn} 
\end{align}

For fixed $M$, by the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:Sa} and the independence between $ (Z_i)_{i\ge1} $ and $ (a_i)_{i=1}^n $, as $n\to\infty$, $ D_{n,M} $ converges in distribution: 
\begin{align}
    D_{n,M} &= \sum_{i = 1}^{M-1} a_i Z_i + S - \sum_{i = 1}^{M-1} a_i
    \overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} \sum_{i = 1}^{M-1} \eta_i Z_i + \Sigma - \sum_{i = 1}^{M-1} \eta_i 
    \eqqcolon D_M . \label{eqn:DnM} 
\end{align}
By \zcref[S]{itm:beta1} and tightness of $ \Sigma $, $ \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j \le \Sigma < \infty $ almost surely. 
Conditioned on $ (\eta_j)_{j\ge1} $, 
\begin{align}
    \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j Z_j \,\ifnum\currentgrouptype=16 \middle\fi|\, (\eta_j)_{j\ge1} \mright]
    &= \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j \overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{<} \infty , \notag 
\end{align}
so $ \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j Z_j < \infty $ almost surely. 
Since $\sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j$ and $\sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j Z_j$ are both nonnegative series, they are convergent almost surely, implying 
\begin{align}
    D_M &\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\to} \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j Z_j + \Sigma - \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j
    = \sum_{j\ge1} \eta_j Z_j + \beta , \notag 
\end{align}
as $M\to\infty$. 
Combining this with \zcref[S]{eqn:Dn,eqn:DnM} proves \zcref[S]{itm:beta2}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:Oq}
Assume \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 
Let the polynomials $ p,q $, the step function $ u_n $ and its weak limit $ u $ in $ L^2([0,1]) $, the scalar $\sigma$ and its limit $ \overline{\sigma} $ all be as in \zcref[S]{eqn:pq,eqn:u_bdd,eqn:u,eqn:ZLsigma,eqn:ulim,eqn:sigmalim}. 
Let $ X\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n) $. 
Then $ \overline{\sigma} - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 \ge 0 $ and 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &\to \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2 Z_1}{\overline{\sigma } - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 + \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 Z_j} \mright] , \notag 
\end{align}
where $ (Z_j)_{j\ge1} $ are i.i.d.\ $ \chi_1^2 $ random variables independent of the Airy point process $ (-\Lambda_{j-1})_{j\ge1} $.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
As usual, we start by applying the spectral decomposition to $X$ and write the squared overlap as
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),q(X) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(X)b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{q(\lambda_1(X))^2 \mleft\langleb,v_1(X)\mright\rangle^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n q(\lambda_i(X))^2 \mleft\langleb,v_i(X)\mright\rangle^2} . \notag 
\end{align}
Since $ b\sim {\mathcal N}(0_n,I_n/n) $ independent of $X$, $ g_i \coloneqq \sqrt{n} \mleft\langleb,v_i(X)\mright\rangle $ (for $i\in[n]$) are i.i.d.\ standard Gaussians and $ Z_i \coloneqq g_i^2 $ (for $i\in[n]$) are i.i.d.\ $ \chi_1^2 $ random variables, all independent of $X$. 
Further defining $ a_i \coloneqq n^{-1} q(\lambda_i(X))^2 $, we can write the squared overlap as 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mleft\langlev_1(X),q(X) b\mright\rangle^2}{\mleft\|q(X)b\mright\|_{2}^2}
    &= \frac{a_1 Z_1}{\sum_{i=1}^n a_i Z_i} . \label{eqn:aZ} 
\end{align}

Denote 
\begin{align}
    S &\coloneqq \sum_{i = 1}^n a_i = \frac{1}{n} \operatorname{Tr}( q(X)^2 ) . \notag
\end{align}
Recall $ L_j $ from \zcref[S]{eqn:ZLsigma}. 
Then for any fixed $m$, \zcref[S]{lem:mass} gives
\begin{align}
    (S, L_1, \cdots, L_m) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Sigma, \Lambda_0, \cdots, \Lambda_{m-1}) , \notag 
\end{align}
where $ \Sigma \coloneqq \overline{\sigma} - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 + \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 $. 
By \zcref[S]{lem:Sconv}, this then implies
\begin{align}
    (S, a_1, \cdots, a_m) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} (\Sigma, \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2, \cdots, \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{m-1})^2) . \label{eqn:Saconv} 
\end{align}
Also, by \zcref[S]{lem:a}, we have that for any $ \zeta>0 $, 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{M\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft( \max_{i\in[M,n]} a_i > \zeta \mright) &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
In view of the last two displays, \zcref[S]{lem:conv} is applicable. 
\zcref[S]{itm:beta1} of \zcref[S]{lem:conv} ensures that almost surely, $ 0\le \Sigma - \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 = \overline{\sigma} - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 $. 
\zcref[S]{itm:beta2} of \zcref[S]{lem:conv} yields the convergence of the denominator of \zcref[S]{eqn:aZ}: 
\begin{align}
    \sum_{i = 1}^n a_i Z_i &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} \overline{\sigma} - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 + \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 Z_j . \notag 
\end{align}
The numerator of \zcref[S]{eqn:aZ} can be easily seen to converge to $ \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2 Z_1 $ in distribution by \zcref[S]{eqn:Saconv}. 
Therefore, the continuous mapping theorem ensures the convergence of \zcref[S]{eqn:aZ} in distribution. 
Since \zcref[S]{eqn:aZ} is within $[0,1]$, the dominated convergence theorem then gives the desired convergence in expectation.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:ulim}
Assume \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}. 
Let $ u \in L^2([0,1]) $ with $ \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} = 1 $. 
Then there exists a sequence of step functions $ (u_n)_{n\ge1} $ with heights $ (\sqrt{d}\,c_k)_{k=0}^{d-1} $ on the mesh $ (k/d)_{k = 0}^d $ such that $ u_n \to u $ strongly in $ L^2([0,1]) $ and for 
\begin{align}
    q(x) &= \frac{x+2}{4} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k p_k(x) , \label{eqn:qmesh} 
\end{align}
it holds that 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &\to \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2 Z_1}{\sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 Z_j} \mright] , \label{eqn:Osub} 
\end{align}
where $ (Z_j)_{j\ge1} $ are i.i.d.\ $ \chi_1^2 $ random variables independent of the Airy point process $ (-\Lambda_{j-1})_{j\ge1} $.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Fix any $ u\in L^2([0,1]) $ with $ \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} = 1 $. 
Let $ u_n $ be the quantization of $u$ on the mesh $ (k/d)_{k = 0}^d $, that is, for any $ 0\le k\le d-1 $ and any $ t\in[k/d,(k+1)/d) $, set $ u_n(t) = \sqrt{d}\,c_k $ where 
\begin{align}
&&
    c_k &= \frac{1}{{\mathfrak c}} \int_{k/d}^{(k+1)/d} u(t) \,\mathrm{d} t , &
    {\mathfrak c} &\coloneqq \sqrt{\sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} \mleft( \int_{k/d}^{(k+1)/d} u(t) \,\mathrm{d} t \mright)^2} , & 
& \notag 
\end{align}
such that $ \mleft\|u_n\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} = 1 $. 
By construction, $ u_n \to u $ strongly in $ L^2([0,1]) $ as $n\to\infty$. 

For $q$ defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:qmesh}, we will show that 
\begin{align}
    \sigma_n &\coloneqq \int_{-2}^2 q(x)^2 \rho_{\textnormal{sc}}(x) \,\mathrm{d} x \to 1 . \label{eqn:sigma1} 
\end{align}
Using the three-term recurrence relation \zcref[S]{eqn:recur}, we write $q$ as 
\begin{align}
    q(x) &= \frac{1}{4} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \mleft( x p_k(x) + 2 p_k(x) \mright) \notag \\
    &= \frac{1}{4} \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k \mleft( p_{k+1}(x) + p_{k-1}(x) + 2p_k(x) \mright)
    = \frac{1}{4} \sum_{k=0}^{d} \mleft( c_{k-1} + 2 c_k + c_{k+1} \mright) p_k(x) , \notag 
\end{align}
with the convention in the last line that quantities with overflowing indices are zero, i.e., $ p_{-1} = c_{-1} = c_d = 0 $. 
Denoting $ b_k = c_{k-1}/4 + c_k/2 + c_{k+1}/4 $, we can simply write $ q(x) = \sum_{k = 0}^{d} b_k p_k(x) $ and hence $ \sigma_n = \mleft\|q\mright\|_{L^2(\mu_{\textnormal{sc}})}^2 = \sum_{k = 0}^{d} b_k^2 $. 
Strong convergence of $ u_n $ in $ L^2([0,1]) $ implies $ \sum_{k = -1}^{d-1} (c_{k+1} - c_k)^2 \to 0 $.                   
Hence, it follows that
\begin{align}
    \sum_{k = 0}^{d} (b_k - c_k)^2
    &= \sum_{k = 0}^{d} \mleft( \frac{c_{k-1} - c_k}{4} + \frac{c_{k+1} - c_k}{4} \mright)^2
    \le 2 \sum_{k = 0}^{d} \mleft[ \mleft( \frac{c_{k-1} - c_k}{4} \mright)^2 + \mleft( \frac{c_{k+1} - c_k}{4} \mright)^2 \mright]
    \to 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
By construction of $ u_n $, we have $ 1 = \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 = \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2 $. 
Therefore $ \sigma_n = \sum_{k = 0}^{d} b_k^2 \to 1 $. 
This proves \zcref[S]{eqn:sigma1}. 

Now take any subsequence of $ u_n $. 
Since the original sequence $ u_n $ converges to $u$ strongly and $ \sigma_n \to 1 $, this subsequence must have the same limit. 
Moreover, this subsequence has a further subsubsequence along which the conclusion of \zcref[S]{lem:Oq} holds, which in particular implies \zcref[S]{eqn:Osub}. 
Therefore, the same limit \zcref[S]{eqn:Osub} holds for the original sequence $ u_n $.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Denote by $ {\mathcal A}_\delta $ the RHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:OPT}. 
We will prove matching upper and lower bounds on the LHS of \zcref[S]{eqn:OPT}. 

\paragraph{Upper bound.}
By \zcref[S]{lem:suppress} and strong convergence of GOE, $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d-1}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) \le \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}^0(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) + o(1) $. 
Since $ (d-1)/n^{1/3} \to \delta $ by the assumption \zcref[S]{eqn:crit}, for the upper bound, it suffices to prove 
\begin{align}
    \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}^0(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &\le {\mathcal A}_\delta . \label{eqn:limsup}
\end{align}

Let $ q \in {\mathcal P}_d \setminus \{0\} $ satisfying $ q(-2) = 0 $ be a sequence of polynomials approaching the supremum in the definition \zcref[S]{eqn:OPT0} of $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}^0(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) $. 
Without loss of generality, we can write $ q(x) = p(x) (x+2)/4 $ for some $ p\in{\mathcal P}_{d-1} \setminus \{0\} $. 
Writing $ p $ in the basis of Chebyshev polynomials $ p(x) = \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k p_k(x) $, by homogeneity of $ {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(\cdot,\operatorname{GOE}(n)) $, we can without loss of generality assume $ \sum_{k = 0}^{d-1} c_k^2 = 1 $. 
As in \zcref[S]{eqn:u}, let $ u_n $ be a step function on the mesh $ (k/d)_{k = 0}^{d} $ with heights $ (\sqrt{d}\,c_k)_{k = 0}^{d-1} $. 
Then by \zcref[S]{eqn:L2}, $ \mleft\|u_n\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} = 1 $. 
Take any subsequence of $ (u_n)_{n\ge1} $. 
By \zcref[S]{lem:mass}, there is a subsubsequence along which $ u_n $ converges weakly to $u$ in $ L^2([0,1]) $ and $ \sigma_n \to \sigma $ (where $ \sigma_n $ and $ \sigma $ are given in \zcref[S]{eqn:ZLsigma,eqn:sigmalim}, respectively). 
Moreover, by \zcref[S]{lem:Oq}, along this subsubsequence, 
\begin{align}
    {\mathcal O}_{n,d}(q,\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &\to \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2 Z_1}{\sigma - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 + \sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 Z_j} \mright]
    \le \mathop{\mathbb{E}}\mleft[ \frac{\Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_0)^2 Z_1}{\sum_{j\ge1} \Gamma_{\delta,u}(-\Lambda_{j-1})^2 Z_j} \mright]
    \le {\mathcal A}_\delta , \notag 
\end{align}
where the first inequality follows since $ \sigma - \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])}^2 \ge 0 $. 
Therefore, every subsequence of $ \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}^0(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) $ has a subsubsequential limit that is at most $ {\mathcal A}_\delta $. 
This implies \zcref[S]{eqn:limsup}. 

\paragraph{Lower bound.}
Fix any $ u\in L^2([0,1]) $ with $ \mleft\|u\mright\|_{L^2([0,1])} = 1 $. 
By \zcref[S]{lem:ulim}, there is a sequence of polynomials $ q\in{\mathcal P}_d $ of the form \zcref[S]{eqn:qmesh} such that \zcref[S]{eqn:Osub} holds. 
Taking supremum over $ u\in L^2([0,1]) \setminus \{0\} $, we immediately obtain
\begin{align}
    \liminf_{n\to\infty} \mathrm{OPT}_{n,d}(\operatorname{GOE}(n)) &\ge {\mathcal A}_\delta . \notag 
\end{align}

Combining the upper and lower bounds completes the proof of \zcref[S]{eqn:OPT}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:markov}
For any $ a>0 $ and any $ p\in{\mathcal P}_d $, 
\begin{align}
    \max_{x\in[-a,a]} \mleft|p'(x)\mright| &\le \frac{d^2}{a} \max_{x\in[-a,a]} \mleft|p(x)\mright| . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:rig}
Let $ X \sim \operatorname{GOE}(n) $. 
Then there exist positive absolute constants $ C $ and $ c $ such that for any sufficiently large $n$, 
\begin{multline}
    \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(\exists j\in[n] , \, \mleft|\lambda_j(X) - \gamma_j\mright| \ge (\log(n))^{C \log\log(n)} \min\mleft\{j,n-j+1\mright\}^{-1/3} n^{-2/3}\mright) \\
    \le C \exp\mleft( - (\log(n))^{c \log\log(n)} \mright) . \label{eqn:rig_result} 
\end{multline}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
The result \zcref[S]{eqn:rig_result} is proved in \cite[Theorem 2.2]{Erdos_Yau_Yin} for a real symmetric random matrix $H$ with independent upper triangular elements subject to the following assumptions: 
\begin{enumerate}                    
                                     
    \item for all $i\le j$, $ H_{i,j} $ has mean zero and variance $ \sigma_{i,j}^2 $ (potentially depending on $n$) satisfying 
    \begin{align}
        \sum_{i = 1}^n \sigma_{i,j}^2 &= 1 \notag 
    \end{align}
    for every $j\in[n]$; 

    \item there exist positive constants $ \delta_-,\delta_+ $ independent of $n$ such that $ 1 $ is a simple eigenvalue of $B$ and $ \mathrm{spec}(B) \subset [-1+\delta_-,1-\delta_+] \cup \{1\} $, where $ B \coloneqq (\sigma_{i,j}^2)_{1\le i,j\le n} $; 

    \item there exists an absolute constant $ C_0 $ (independent of $n$) such that 
    \begin{align}
        \max_{1\le i,j\le n} \sigma_{i,j}^2 &\le \frac{C_0}{n} ; \notag 
    \end{align}

    \item there exists an absolute constant $ \vartheta > 0 $ (independent of $n$) such that for any $ 1\le i,j\le n $ and $x\ge1$, it holds that 
    \begin{align}
        \mathop{\mathbb{P}}\mleft(\mleft|H_{i,j}\mright| > x \sigma_{i,j}\mright) &\le \vartheta^{-1} \exp\mleft( - x^{\vartheta} \mright) . \notag 
    \end{align}
                                     
\end{enumerate}
It is easy to verify that $ H = \sqrt{\frac{n}{n+1}} X $ where $ X \sim \operatorname{GOE}(n) $ satisfies all assumptions above. 
Indeed, 
\begin{align}
    \sigma_{i,j}^2 &= \frac{1 + \mathds1{\mleft\{i = j\mright\}}}{n+1} , \notag 
\end{align} 
which fulfills the first and third assumptions with $ C_0 = 2 $. 
The matrix $B$ has eigenvalues $1$ and $ 1/(n+1) $ with multiplicities $1$ and $ n-1 $, respectively. 
So as long as $n\ge3$, the second assumption is verified where $ \delta_-,\delta_+ $ can be taken to be any positive constants less than $ 3/4 $. 
Finally, standard Gaussian tail bound guarantees the validity of the fourth assumption with $ \vartheta = 1 $. 

Since the $ C,c $ in \cite[Theorem 2.2]{Erdos_Yau_Yin} depend only on $ C_0, \delta_{-}, \delta_{+}, \vartheta $, the same result \zcref[S]{eqn:rig_result} also holds for any sufficiently large $n$ with $ X $ in place of $H$ once we apply the triangle inequality
\begin{align}
    \mleft| \lambda_j(H) - \gamma_j \mright|
    &\ge \sqrt{\frac{n}{n+1}} \mleft| \lambda_j(X) - \gamma_j \mright| - \mleft| \sqrt{\frac{n}{n+1}} - 1 \mright| \mleft|\gamma_j\mright|
    \ge \sqrt{\frac{n}{n+1}} \mleft| \lambda_j(X) - \gamma_j \mright| - \frac{1}{n} \notag 
\end{align}
and suitably adjust $ C,c $ so as to absorb the nuisance factors $ \sqrt{n/(n+1)} $ and $1/n$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:out}
Consider $Y$ in \zcref[S]{eqn:spiked_model} with $ \lambda > 1 $ fixed. 
Then 
\begin{align}
    \sqrt{n} (\lambda_1(Y) - \lambda_\star) &\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\to} {\mathcal N}\mleft( 0, 2 \mleft( 1 - \frac{1}{\lambda^2} \mright) \mright) , \notag 
\end{align}
where $ \lambda_\star $ is defined in \zcref[S]{eqn:lambda_star}.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:weak}
Let $ H $ be a Hilbert space equipped with inner product $ \mleft\langle\cdot,\cdot\mright\rangle_H $. 
If a sequence $ f_n $ converges (as $n\to\infty$) weakly to $ f $ in $H$ and $ K\subset H $ is compact, then 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{g\in K} \mleft|\mleft\langlef_n - f,g\mright\rangle\mright| &= 0 . \notag 
\end{align}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Since weakly convergent sequences are bounded, we have 
\begin{align}
    \sup_n \mleft\|f_n - f\mright\|_{H} &\le M < \infty , \notag 
\end{align}
where $ \mleft\|\cdot\mright\|_{H} \coloneqq \sqrt{\mleft\langle\cdot,\cdot\mright\rangle_H} $. 
Fix $ \varepsilon>0 $ and let $ K_\varepsilon \subset K $ be a finite $\varepsilon$-net of $K$. 
Such a $ K_\varepsilon $ exists by compactness of $K$. 
For any $ g\in K $, associate to it a representative $ g_\varepsilon\in K_\varepsilon $ satisfying $ \mleft\|g - g_\varepsilon\mright\|_{H} \le \varepsilon $. 
Then by triangle inequality for $\mleft|\cdot\mright|$ and Cauchy--Schwarz for $ \mleft\langle\cdot,\cdot\mright\rangle_H $, 
\begin{align}
    \mleft|\mleft\langlef_n-f,g\mright\rangle_H\mright| &\le \mleft|\mleft\langlef_n-f,g-g_\varepsilon\mright\rangle_H\mright| + \mleft|\mleft\langlef_n-f,g_\varepsilon\mright\rangle_H\mright|
    \le M\varepsilon + \mleft|\mleft\langlef_n-f,g_\varepsilon\mright\rangle_H\mright| . \notag 
\end{align}
Taking $ \sup $ over $g\in K$ and limit as $n\to\infty$ in this order, by weak convergence of $ f_n $ to $f$ and finiteness of $ K_\varepsilon $, we have 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{g\in K} \mleft|\mleft\langlef_n-f,g\mright\rangle_H\mright|
    &\le M\varepsilon + \lim_{n\to\infty} \sup_{g_\varepsilon\in K_\varepsilon} \mleft|\mleft\langlef_n-f,g_\varepsilon\mright\rangle_H\mright|
    = M\varepsilon . \notag 
\end{align}
Finally, by finiteness of $M$, sending $ \varepsilon\downarrow0 $ yields the result.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
